<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove global existence and uniqueness for arbitrary smooth admissible initial data in the three-dimensional, one-species relativistic Vlasov–Maxwell system. The particle density is initially compactly supported, and the electromagnetic fields have finite energy and bounded derivatives of all orders. The solution remains smooth on every finite time interval. This resolves the large-data global classical regularity problem for this model, without size or symmetry restrictions on the data.

- **claim-global-existence** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-global)): Every smooth admissible datum <a href="#eq:data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:data">[eq:data]</a> generates a unique global classical solution of <a href="#eq:rvm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rvm">[eq:rvm]</a>. For every finite $T$, this solution satisfies $$f\in C^\infty([0,T]\times\mathbb R^3_x\times\mathbb R^3_v),\qquad E,B\in C^\infty([0,T]\times\mathbb R^3_x;\mathbb R^3),$$ and $f$ has compact phase-space support on $[0,T]$. The two divergence constraints hold for all time.
  - Explanation: Every smooth admissible datum gives a unique global classical solution, smooth with compact phase-space support on each finite time interval, with both divergence constraints preserved.
- **claim-signed-increment** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-increment)): For each smooth admissible datum and finite $T_0$, there are constants $M,A\ge1$ and $P_0$ with the following property. On every compact classical solution interval $[0,t_*]$ as above, for every dyadic $P\ge P_0$ satisfying <a href="#eq:P-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:P-range">[eq:P-range]</a>, every supported receiver, every $w=2^k$, $k\ge0$, and every interval $[t_1,t_2]\subset[0,t_*]$ on which $w/8\le q_X(t)\le8w$ throughout, one has $$\label{eq:bootstrap} \|V_X(t_2)-V_X(t_1)\| \le MP\sqrt{t_2-t_1}+A(t_2-t_1)S(w).$$ The constants are independent of $t_*$ and $P$.
  - Explanation: A bound on the signed momentum increment along supported receiver characteristics, uniform in the compact interval and in the dyadic parameter.
- **claim-local-continuation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-prop-continuation)): Every smooth admissible datum has a unique maximal classical solution. It is smooth on each compact subinterval of its lifespan. If its maximal time $T_{\max}$ is finite and $$\sup\{\,\|v\|:0\le t<T_{\max},\ (x,v)\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}f(t)\,\}<\infty,$$ then the solution has a classical extension beyond $T_{\max}$.
  - Explanation: A unique maximal classical solution exists, it is smooth on compact subintervals, and bounded momentum support at a finite maximal time yields a classical extension.
- **claim-transport-cone-energy** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ret-energy)): The characteristic flow preserves phase volume and transports $f_0$. In particular $0\leq f\leq\\|f_0\\|_\infty$. Define $$\mathcal U=\frac{\|E\|^2+\|B\|^2}{2}+\int qf\,dv, \qquad \mathcal S=E\times B+\int quf\,dv, \qquad \mathcal H_0=\int_{\mathbb R^3}\mathcal U(0,x)\,dx.$$ Then $$\label{eq:energy} \int_{\mathbb R^3}\mathcal U(s,x)\,dx=\mathcal H_0 \qquad(0\leq s\leq t_*).$$ For every vertex $(t,x)$ in this interval, put $r=t-s$, $y=x-rn$, $n\in S^2$, and $$d=1-n\cdot u(v),\qquad G=\|E(s,y)+n\times B(s,y)\|+\|n\cdot B(s,y)\|.$$ Then $$\label{eq:cone-energy} \int_0^t\int_{S^2}r^2 \left(G^2+\int qd f(s,x-rn,v)\,dv\right)dn\,ds \leq4\mathcal H_0.$$
  - Explanation: The flow preserves phase volume, the density is transported, total energy is conserved, and the energy flux through backward light cones is controlled by the initial energy.
- **claim-retarded-phase-change** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ret-phase-change)): Away from the cone tip, the map sending $(y,v)$ at time $s=t-\|x-y\|$ to its initial label is one-to-one, with Jacobian $$\label{ret:phase-jacobian} dz=d\,dy\,dv,\qquad d=1-n\cdot u(v)>0.$$ Its image consists, up to the null set of tip labels, of $$\mathcal L_{t,x}=\{z:\|x-Y(0,z)\|<t\}.$$ For such a label, the retarded time solves $$\label{ret:time-equation} s+\|x-Y(s,z)\|=t.$$ Consequently <a href="#ret:potentials" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ret:potentials">[ret:potentials]</a> equals $$\label{ret:label-potentials} (\Phi,\mathcal A)(t,x)=\frac1{4\pi} \int_{\mathcal L_{t,x}} f_0(z)\frac{(1,u(V(s,z)))}{rd}\,dz.$$
  - Explanation: Retarded phase points on the backward cone are in one-to-one correspondence with initial labels, with an explicit Jacobian, giving the potentials as integrals over labels.
- **claim-retarded-representation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ret-representation)): Let $E^{\rm fr},B^{\rm fr}$ be the homogeneous wave solutions with data $$(E^{\rm fr},\partial_tE^{\rm fr})\|_{t=0} =(E_0,\nabla\times B_0),\qquad (B^{\rm fr},\partial_tB^{\rm fr})\|_{t=0} =(B_0,-\nabla\times E_0).$$ For $t>0$, define initial boundary terms by $$\label{ret:initial-boundary} \begin{split} (E^{\rm bd},B^{\rm bd})(t,x) =\frac1{4\pi t}\int_{\|x-y_0\|=t}\int_{\mathbb R^3} \frac{f_0(y_0,v_0)}{d_0} \bigl(n_0-u_0,\ n_0\times(n_0-u_0)\bigr)\,dv_0\,dS_{y_0},\\ n_0=(x-y_0)/t,\qquad u_0=u(v_0),\qquad d_0=1-n_0\cdot u_0. \end{split}$$ Set these terms to zero at $t=0$. Then $$\label{ret:fields} \begin{split} E(t,x)&=E^{\rm fr}(t,x)+E^{\rm bd}(t,x) +\frac1{4\pi}\int_{\mathcal L_{t,x}}f_0(z)\,\mathscr K\,dz,\\ B(t,x)&=B^{\rm fr}(t,x)+B^{\rm bd}(t,x) +\frac1{4\pi}\int_{\mathcal L_{t,x}}f_0(z)\,(n\times\mathscr K)\,dz, \end{split}$$ where $$\label{ret:kernel} \mathscr K=\frac{n-u}{r^2q^2d^3} +\frac{(n-u)(n\cdot b)-db}{rd^3}.$$ All these integrals are well defined at the stated classical regularity. Moreover, $$\label{ret:data-bound} \sup_{0\leq t\leq T_0,\ x\in\mathbb R^3} (\|E^{\rm fr}\|+\|B^{\rm fr}\|+\|E^{\rm bd}\|+\|B^{\rm bd}\|) \leq C_{T_0,f_0,E_0,B_0}.$$
  - Explanation: The fields split into homogeneous waves, initial boundary terms and a retarded integral over labels with an explicit kernel, with the data parts bounded.
- **claim-pointwise-force-bounds** (bound, [source](#stmt-ret-direct-bounds)): Let $\theta,\phi>0$ satisfy $\theta^2\asymp d$ and $\phi^2\asymp D$, with fixed comparison constants. The transport kernel splits into two terms, according to the normal and tangential parts of $n-u$, bounded by $$\label{ret:transport-bounds} T_r=\frac{Cf}{r^2q^2\theta^2},\qquad T_\perp=\frac{Cf\phi}{r^2q^2\theta^3}.$$ With $G$ as in Lemma <a href="#ret:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ret:energy">4</a>, the source acceleration satisfies $$\label{ret:acceleration-bounds} \|b\|\leq\frac Cq(G+\theta^2\|B\|),\qquad \|n\cdot b\|\leq\frac{C\theta}q(G+\theta^2\|B\|),\qquad \|q'\|\leq C(G+\theta\|B\|).$$ Consequently $$\label{ret:source-bounds} \|S_{\rm ker}\|\leq S_g+S_b,\qquad S_g=\frac{Cf\phi}{rq\theta^2}G,\qquad S_b=\frac{Cf\phi}{rq}\|B\|.$$ If $V_X\ne0$ and $P_X$ is orthogonal projection onto $V_X^\perp$, then $\|P_XS_{\rm ker}\|\leq C\phi(S_g+S_b)$.
  - Explanation: Pointwise majorants for the transport and source kernels in terms of angular scales and the cone field quantity.
- **claim-receiver-time-jacobian** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ret-receiver-time)): Along a retarded source label satisfying $t=s+\|X(t)-Y(s,z)\|$ away from collisions, $$\label{ret:receiver-jacobians} \frac{dt}{ds}=\frac dD,\qquad ds\,f_0(z)\,dz=D\,dt\,f(t-r,y,v)\,dy\,dv.$$ For $f_0(z)dz$-almost every label there is no same-time collision $Y(t,z)=X(t)$ anywhere in the compact time interval. Such a label has one continuously differentiable retarded branch after its initial-cone entry, when that entry occurs, and $r$ is bounded below on each closed part of this branch in the compact interval.
  - Explanation: Source time and receiver time are related along a retarded branch by an explicit Jacobian, and same-time collisions are a null set of labels.
- **claim-crude-occupation** (bound, [source](#stmt-occ-crude-lemma)): For every bin and every allowed set of receiver times, $$\begin{aligned} \mathsf M&\leq C\frac{\max(I,h)}p, \label{occ:energy}\\ \mathsf M&\leq C I\frac{\phi^2}{\theta^2}. \label{occ:time} \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: Two crude bounds on the occupation measure of a bin, one from energy and one from hit duration.
- **claim-stable-cell-occupation** (bound, [source](#stmt-occ-cell-lemma)): In every actual bin, $$\label{occ:actual} \mathsf M\leq C\frac{hk_1}{p} \max(1,I/\lambda,h/\lambda).$$
  - Explanation: In actual bins the occupation measure is bounded using short stable cells on which the relative velocity keeps a fixed projection.
- **claim-baseline-occupation** (bound, [source](#stmt-occ-refined-proposition)): Suppose $h\leq I$, excluding only deficit bins with $h\leq\lambda$. Then $$\label{occ:refined} \mathsf M\leq C\frac{hk_1}{p} \left(1+\frac I\lambda\mathcal E\right), \qquad \mathcal E=\begin{cases} \mathcal E_{p\phi},&\text{small sector},\\ 1,&\text{remaining sector}. \end{cases}$$ Consequently, with $R=1+\sqrt{I/\lambda}\sqrt{\mathcal E}$, $$\label{occ:R-bound} \mathsf M\leq C\frac{hk_1}{p}R^2$$ for all $h$, except deficit bins with $h\leq\min(I,\lambda)$.
  - Explanation: Baseline occupation bound with a joint energy budget in the small sector and a consequent bound with the factor R squared.
- **claim-integrated-force-bounds** (bound, [source](#stmt-dir-direct)): Assume the bootstrap <a href="#eq:bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bootstrap">[eq:bootstrap]</a>. The bounded data term, $T_r$, $T_\perp$, $S_b$, and the remaining-sector part of $S_g$ have combined integral at most $$\label{dir:baseline} C\bigl[P\sqrt I+(M+\sqrt A)I S(w)\bigr].$$ The integral of the full force satisfies $$\label{dir:absolute} \int_{\mathcal T}\|K(t)\|\,dt \le C\sqrt w\bigl[P\sqrt I+(M+\sqrt A)I S(w)\bigr].$$ The constant $C$ is independent of $P,w,I,M,A$.
  - Explanation: Under the bootstrap, the data, transport, magnetic and remaining-sector good-field terms obey the baseline bound, and the full force obeys it with an extra square-root factor in the scale.
- **claim-signed-force-identity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-can-identity)): At every non-tip point of a retarded branch, $$\label{can:label-identity} \mathcal K =-\left(\frac{k_0}{rd}\right)' +\frac{e}{r^2D^2}\left(\frac{n}{q_X^2D}-a\right) +\frac{n(a_t\cdot k_0)}{rD^2}.$$ Consequently the two nondifferentiated terms, in $dt\,dy\,dv$ measure, are $$\label{can:bulk} \mathcal R=\frac{fe}{r^2D}\left(\frac{n}{q_X^2D}-a\right), \qquad \mathcal A=\frac{fn(a_t\cdot k_0)}{rD}.$$
  - Explanation: The retarded force kernel equals a total derivative along the source branch plus a geometric residual and a receiver-acceleration residual.
- **claim-cutoff-cancellation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-can-cutoff-cancellation)): Let $\mathcal S$ be any fixed set of representative indices, all in the small sector, and put $\chi=\sum_{j\in\mathcal S}\beta_j$. Selection may depend on the interval and fixed parameters, but not on the variables being differentiated. For differentiation through source variables $(q,u)$ alone, or through geometric variables $(r,n)$ alone, $$\label{can:partition-cancel} \partial\chi=-\sum_{j\notin\mathcal S}\partial\beta_j.$$ This derivative vanishes unless selected and unselected supports meet. On their intersection, representatives in each coordinate differ by at most a factor of four. In particular all relevant supports remain in the wider small-sector range above.
  - Explanation: Derivatives of a selected partition sum equal minus the derivatives of the unselected terms, so errors live where selected and unselected supports overlap.
- **claim-weighted-signed-formula** (theorem, [source](#stmt-can-weighted-formula)): Let $J=[t_1,t_2]$ lie in a compact classical time interval. Let $0\leq\psi\leq1$ be Lipschitz on $J$, vanish at its endpoints, and be extended by zero outside $J$. Let $\chi$ be a selected sum as in Lemma <a href="#can:cutoff-cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:cutoff-cancellation">14</a>. One may integrate <a href="#can:label-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:label-identity">[can:label-identity]</a> labelwise against $\psi\chi$ and a bounded $C^1$ matrix $\Pi(t)$, including $\Pi=\mathrm{Id}$. More precisely, $$\begin{aligned} \label{can:weighted-terms} \int_J\!\int\psi\chi\Pi\mathcal K_t\,dy\,dv\,dt ={}&\mathcal B_0+\int_J\!\int \biggl[\psi\chi\Pi(\mathcal R+\mathcal A) +\psi_t\chi\Pi\frac{fk_0}{r}\notag\\ &\hspace{25mm}+\psi\chi\Pi_t\frac{fk_0}{r} +\psi\Pi\frac{fDk_0}{rd}\chi'_s\biggr]\,dy\,dv\,dt, \end{aligned}$$ where the spatial integral has $r=\|X(t)-y\|<t$, all source quantities are evaluated at $s=t-r$, and $\mathcal B_0$ is the initial-cone boundary term. If $\\|\Pi\\|\lesssim1$, that boundary term is bounded in norm by $$\label{can:initial-boundary-bound} C\int_J\|\psi(t_0)\|\,dt_0.$$ The constant in <a href="#can:initial-boundary-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:initial-boundary-bound">[can:initial-boundary-bound]</a> depends only on the datum and fixed time horizon. For $\Pi=\mathrm{Id}$, and for $\Pi=wP_X/\|V_X\|$ when $\|V_X\|\asymp w$, the derivative kernels satisfy the bounds above, uniformly in the fixed selected index set. For a general $\Pi$, its derivative term retains the explicit bound $Cf\theta\\|\Pi_t\\|/(r\phi)$.
  - Explanation: Integration by parts along source labels against a weight and a matrix multiplier yields bulk terms, derivative terms and a bounded initial-cone boundary term.
- **claim-direction-changes** (bound, [source](#stmt-ang-direction)): Assume the signed increment bootstrap <a href="#eq:bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bootstrap">[eq:bootstrap]</a> on $[0,\tau]$. Let $w$ be a dyad with $w\ge P^{3/5}$, and let $J\subset[0,\tau]$ be a closed interval of length at most $(w/P)^2$ on which a supported characteristic satisfies $w/8\le q_X\le8w$. Suppose $$w^{-3/5}\le\delta\le w^{-1/10}.$$ Starting at the left endpoint of $J$, stop whenever the direction $\xi=V_X/\|V_X\|$ first has chordal distance $\delta$ from its direction at the preceding stopping time. Include the last, possibly incomplete, interval. For sufficiently large $P$, the number of intervals is at most $$\label{ang:count} C(M,A)L^6/\delta.$$
  - Explanation: Under the bootstrap, the number of direction changes of a given angular size along a high-energy characteristic is bounded.
- **claim-improved-occupation** (bound, [source](#stmt-ang-improved-hit)): Assume the signed increment bootstrap on $[0,\tau]$. Let a small-sector bin satisfy $$\label{ang:window} p,w\ge P^{7/10},\qquad j^{-49/100}\le\phi\le j^{-4/25}\quad(j=p,w).$$ Let the eligible receiver times lie in a closed interval $J\subset[0,\tau]$ of length at most $I\le(w/P)^2$, with receiver energy in $[w/8,8w]$ at those times. Set $I_m^0=(m/P)^2$ and $\beta=29/25$. Then, for $P\ge P_*(M,A)$, the hit measure satisfies $$\label{ang:improved-occupation} \mathsf M\le C\frac hp\phi^{-\beta} \max\left(1,\frac I{I_m^0},\frac h{I_m^0}\right).$$ The constant $C$ is independent of $M,A$.
  - Explanation: At intermediate angles for high energies, direction control yields an improved occupation bound with a negative power of the angular scale.
- **claim-weight-sum** (bound, [source](#stmt-sel-weight-sum)): The sum of $W$ over all small-sector indices is bounded by a constant depending only on $\epsilon$ and the fixed decomposition. The unselected small-sector good-field terms have total integral at most $CP\sqrt I$.
  - Explanation: The bin weight is summable over small-sector indices, and unselected good-field terms are bounded by the baseline.
- **claim-selected-coefficient** (bound, [source](#stmt-sel-exponents)): For fixed $M,A$, there is a threshold $P_*(M,A)$ such that every selected index with $P\ge P_*(M,A)$ satisfies the exact representative inequality $$\label{sel:safe} U\le w^{-1/2}W.$$
  - Explanation: For large enough P, every selected index satisfies the receiver coefficient inequality, proved by contradiction through exponent bookkeeping.
- **claim-spatial-cutoff-sum** (bound, [source](#stmt-sel-spatial-sum)): The total spatial-cutoff error is at most $C\mathcal B(I,w)$.
  - Explanation: The total spatial-cutoff error is within the baseline budget, without an extra logarithm.
- **claim-time-weight-sum** (bound, [source](#stmt-sel-time-weight)): The total time-weight derivative is at most $C_\eta\mathcal B(I,w)$.
  - Explanation: The total time-weight derivative contribution is within the baseline budget.
- **claim-weighted-impulse** (bound, [source](#stmt-sel-bulk-impulse)): For the weight $\psi$ above and $P\ge P_*(M,A)$, $$\label{sel:bulk} \left\|\int_J\psi(t)K(t)\,dt\right\| \le C_\eta\big[P\sqrt I+(M+\sqrt A)I S(w)\big].$$ The constant $C_\eta$ is independent of $M,A$.
  - Explanation: The time-weighted impulse is bounded by the baseline budget, with a constant independent of the bootstrap constants.
- **claim-exterior-comparison** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cont-exterior)): Suppose that a $C^1$ solution exists on $[0,S]$, that $f_0$ is compactly supported as above, and that $F(0)\in H^k(\mathbb R^3)$ for every integer $k$. Then the fields belong to $C([0,S];L^2)$ and have bounded first spatial derivatives on the entire slab. More precisely, $$F(t,x)=U(t)F(0)(x)\qquad\text{if }\|x\|>R_0+t.$$
  - Explanation: Outside the expanding particle region the fields coincide with the free Maxwell evolution of the initial fields.
- **claim-vacuum-fields** (construction, [source](#stmt-cont-vacuum)): Let $D_E,D_B\in C_b^\infty(\mathbb R^3)\cap L^2(\mathbb R^3)$ be divergence-free. There is a global smooth vacuum Maxwell solution on $[0,\infty)$ with these data, continuous into $L^2$, whose spatial derivatives of every order are bounded on each finite slab. If the data vanish on $B(0,L)$, the solution vanishes wherever $\|x\|+t<L$.
  - Explanation: Divergence-free bounded-derivative finite-energy data have a global smooth vacuum Maxwell solution with finite propagation.

Define $$\\mathcal U=\\frac{|E|^2+|B|^2}{2}+\\int qf\\,dv,\n \\qquad\n \\mathcal S=E\\times B+\\int quf\\,dv,\n \\qquad\n \\mathcal H_0=\\int_{\\mathbb R^3}\\mathcal U(0,x)\\,dx.$$ Then $$\\label{eq:energy}\n \\int_{\\mathbb R^3}\\mathcal U(s,x)\\,dx=\\mathcal H_0\n \\qquad(0\\leq s\\leq t_*).$$ For every vertex $(t,x)$ in this interval, put $r=t-s$, $y=x-rn$, $n\\in S^2$, and $$d=1-n\\cdot u(v),\\qquad\n G=|E(s,y)+n\\times B(s,y)|+|n\\cdot B(s,y)|.$$ Then $$\\label{eq:cone-energy}\n \\int_0^t\\int_{S^2}r^2\n \\left(G^2+\\int qd f(s,x-rn,v)\\,dv\\right)dn\\,ds\n \\leq4\\mathcal H_0.$$",
          "source_id": "PM-362.1"
        }

      "exact_statement": "The characteristic flow preserves phase volume and transports $f_0$. In particular $0\\leq f\\leq\\|f_0\\|_\\infty$.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-ret-phase-change",
        "exact_quote": "Away from the cone tip, the map sending $(y,v)$ at time $s=t-|x-y|$ to its initial label is one-to-one, with Jacobian $$\\label{ret:phase-jacobian}\n dz=d\\,dy\\,dv,\\qquad d=1-n\\cdot u(v)>0.$$ Its image consists, up to the null set of tip labels, of $$\\mathcal L_{t,x}=\\{z:|x-Y(0,z)|<t\\}.$$ For such a label, the retarded time solves $$\\label{ret:time-equation}\n s+|x-Y(s,z)|=t.$$ Consequently <a href=\"#ret:potentials\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"ret:potentials\">[ret:potentials]</a> equals $$\\label{ret:label-potentials}\n (\\Phi,\\mathcal A)(t,x)=\\frac1{4\\pi}\n       \\int_{\\mathcal L_{t,x}} f_0(z)\\frac{(1,u(V(s,z)))}{rd}\\,dz.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-362.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {

          "source_id": "PM-362.1"
        }

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-ret-representation",
        "exact_quote": "Let $E^{\\rm fr},B^{\\rm fr}$ be the homogeneous wave solutions with data $$(E^{\\rm fr},\\partial_tE^{\\rm fr})|_{t=0}\n       =(E_0,\\nabla\\times B_0),\\qquad\n (B^{\\rm fr},\\partial_tB^{\\rm fr})|_{t=0}\n       =(B_0,-\\nabla\\times E_0).$$ For $t>0$, define initial boundary terms by $$\\label{ret:initial-boundary}\n \\begin{split}\n (E^{\\rm bd},B^{\\rm bd})(t,x)\n =\\frac1{4\\pi t}\\int_{|x-y_0|=t}\\int_{\\mathbb R^3}\n \\frac{f_0(y_0,v_0)}{d_0}\n \\bigl(n_0-u_0,\\ n_0\\times(n_0-u_0)\\bigr)\\,dv_0\\,dS_{y_0},\\\\\n n_0=(x-y_0)/t,\\qquad u_0=u(v_0),\\qquad d_0=1-n_0\\cdot u_0.\n \\end{split}$$ Set these terms to zero at $t=0$. Then $$\\label{ret:fields}\n \\begin{split}\n E(t,x)&=E^{\\rm fr}(t,x)+E^{\\rm bd}(t,x)\n                  +\\frac1{4\\pi}\\int_{\\mathcal L_{t,x}}f_0(z)\\,\\mathscr K\\,dz,\\\\\n B(t,x)&=B^{\\rm fr}(t,x)+B^{\\rm bd}(t,x)\n                  +\\frac1{4\\pi}\\int_{\\mathcal L_{t,x}}f_0(z)\\,(n\\times\\mathscr K)\\,dz,\n \\end{split}$$ where $$\\label{ret:kernel}\n \\mathscr K=\\frac{n-u}{r^2q^2d^3}\n       +\\frac{(n-u)(n\\cdot b)-db}{rd^3}.$$ All these integrals are well defined at the stated classical regularity. Moreover, $$\\label{ret:data-bound}\n \\sup_{0\\leq t\\leq T_0,\\ x\\in\\mathbb R^3}\n (|E^{\\rm fr}|+|B^{\\rm fr}|+|E^{\\rm bd}|+|B^{\\rm bd}|)\n \\leq C_{T_0,f_0,E_0,B_0}.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-362.1"
      },

        "anchor": "stmt-ret-direct-bounds",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\theta,\\phi>0$ satisfy $\\theta^2\\asymp d$ and $\\phi^2\\asymp D$, with fixed comparison constants. The transport kernel splits into two terms, according to the normal and tangential parts of $n-u$, bounded by $$\\label{ret:transport-bounds}\n T_r=\\frac{Cf}{r^2q^2\\theta^2},\\qquad\n T_\\perp=\\frac{Cf\\phi}{r^2q^2\\theta^3}.$$ With $G$ as in Lemma <a href=\"#ret:energy\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"ret:energy\">4</a>, the source acceleration satisfies $$\\label{ret:acceleration-bounds}\n |b|\\leq\\frac Cq(G+\\theta^2|B|),\\qquad\n |n\\cdot b|\\leq\\frac{C\\theta}q(G+\\theta^2|B|),\\qquad\n |q'|\\leq C(G+\\theta|B|).$$ Consequently $$\\label{ret:source-bounds}\n |S_{\\rm ker}|\\leq S_g+S_b,\\qquad\n S_g=\\frac{Cf\\phi}{rq\\theta^2}G,\\qquad\n S_b=\\frac{Cf\\phi}{rq}|B|.$$ If $V_X\\ne0$ and $P_X$ is orthogonal projection onto $V_X^\\perp$, then $|P_XS_{\\rm ker}|\\leq C\\phi(S_g+S_b)$.",
        "source_id": "PM-362.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

        "exact_quote": "Along a retarded source label satisfying $t=s+|X(t)-Y(s,z)|$ away from collisions, $$\\label{ret:receiver-jacobians}\n \\frac{dt}{ds}=\\frac dD,\\qquad\n ds\\,f_0(z)\\,dz=D\\,dt\\,f(t-r,y,v)\\,dy\\,dv.$$ For $f_0(z)dz$-almost every label there is no same-time collision $Y(t,z)=X(t)$ anywhere in the compact time interval.         "exact_quote": "Suppose $h\\leq I$, excluding only deficit bins with $h\\leq\\lambda$.       "exact_statement": "Suppose $h\\leq I$, excluding only deficit bins with $h\\leq\\lambda$.         "anchor": "stmt-dir-direct",
        "exact_quote": "Assume the bootstrap <a href=\"#eq:bootstrap\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:bootstrap\">[eq:bootstrap]</a>.       },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-can-identity",
        "exact_quote": "At every non-tip point of a retarded branch, $$\\label{can:label-identity}\n \\mathcal K\n =-\\left(\\frac{k_0}{rd}\\right)'\n  +\\frac{e}{r^2D^2}\\left(\\frac{n}{q_X^2D}-a\\right)\n  +\\frac{n(a_t\\cdot k_0)}{rD^2}.$$ Consequently the two nondifferentiated terms, in $dt\\,dy\\,dv$ measure, are $$\\label{can:bulk}\n \\mathcal R=\\frac{fe}{r^2D}\\left(\\frac{n}{q_X^2D}-a\\right),\n \\qquad\n \\mathcal A=\\frac{fn(a_t\\cdot k_0)}{rD}.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-362.1"
      },

          "source_id": "PM-362.1"
        }
      ],

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-can-cutoff-cancellation",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\mathcal S$ be any fixed set of representative indices, all in the small sector, and put $\\chi=\\sum_{j\\in\\mathcal S}\\beta_j$. Selection may depend on the interval and fixed parameters, but not on the variables being differentiated. For differentiation through source variables $(q,u)$ alone, or through geometric variables $(r,n)$ alone, $$\\label{can:partition-cancel}\n \\partial\\chi=-\\sum_{j\\notin\\mathcal S}\\partial\\beta_j.$$ This derivative vanishes unless selected and unselected supports meet. On their intersection, representatives in each coordinate differ by at most a factor of four. In particular all relevant supports remain in the wider small-sector range above.",
        "source_id": "PM-362.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-can-cutoff-cancellation",

          "source_id": "PM-362.1"
        }

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-can-weighted-formula",
        "exact_quote": "Let $J=[t_1,t_2]$ lie in a compact classical time interval. Let $0\\leq\\psi\\leq1$ be Lipschitz on $J$, vanish at its endpoints, and be extended by zero outside $J$. Let $\\chi$ be a selected sum as in Lemma <a href=\"#can:cutoff-cancellation\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"can:cutoff-cancellation\">14</a>. One may integrate <a href=\"#can:label-identity\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"can:label-identity\">[can:label-identity]</a> labelwise against $\\psi\\chi$ and a bounded $C^1$ matrix $\\Pi(t)$, including $\\Pi=\\mathrm{Id}$. More precisely, $$\\begin{aligned}\n\\label{can:weighted-terms}\n \\int_J\\!\\int\\psi\\chi\\Pi\\mathcal K_t\\,dy\\,dv\\,dt\n ={}&\\mathcal B_0+\\int_J\\!\\int\n \\biggl[\\psi\\chi\\Pi(\\mathcal R+\\mathcal A)\n       +\\psi_t\\chi\\Pi\\frac{fk_0}{r}\\notag\\\\\n &\\hspace{25mm}+\\psi\\chi\\Pi_t\\frac{fk_0}{r}\n       +\\psi\\Pi\\frac{fDk_0}{rd}\\chi'_s\\biggr]\\,dy\\,dv\\,dt,\n\\end{aligned}$$ where the spatial integral has $r=|X(t)-y|<t$, all source quantities are evaluated at $s=t-r$, and $\\mathcal B_0$ is the initial-cone boundary term. If $\\|\\Pi\\|\\lesssim1$, that boundary term is bounded in norm by $$\\label{can:initial-boundary-bound}\n C\\int_J|\\psi(t_0)|\\,dt_0.$$ The constant in <a href=\"#can:initial-boundary-bound\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"can:initial-boundary-bound\">[can:initial-boundary-bound]</a> depends only on the datum and fixed time horizon. For $\\Pi=\\mathrm{Id}$, and for $\\Pi=wP_X/|V_X|$ when $|V_X|\\asymp w$, the derivative kernels satisfy the bounds above, uniformly in the fixed selected index set. For a general $\\Pi$, its derivative term retains the explicit bound $Cf\\theta\\|\\Pi_t\\|/(r\\phi)$.",
        "source_id": "PM-362.1"
      },

If $\\|\\Pi\\|\\lesssim1$, that boundary term is bounded in norm by $$\\label{can:initial-boundary-bound}\n C\\int_J|\\psi(t_0)|\\,dt_0.$$ The constant in <a href=\"#can:initial-boundary-bound\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"can:initial-boundary-bound\">[can:initial-boundary-bound]</a> depends only on the datum and fixed time horizon.         "anchor": "stmt-ang-direction",
        "exact_quote": "Assume the signed increment bootstrap <a href=\"#eq:bootstrap\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:bootstrap\">[eq:bootstrap]</a> on $[0,\\tau]$. Let $w$ be a dyad with $w\\ge P^{3/5}$, and let $J\\subset[0,\\tau]$ be a closed interval of length at most $(w/P)^2$ on which a supported characteristic satisfies $w/8\\le q_X\\le8w$. Suppose $$w^{-3/5}\\le\\delta\\le w^{-1/10}.$$ Starting at the left endpoint of $J$, stop whenever the direction $\\xi=V_X/|V_X|$ first has chordal distance $\\delta$ from its direction at the preceding stopping time. Include the last, possibly incomplete, interval. For sufficiently large $P$, the number of intervals is at most $$\\label{ang:count}\n C(M,A)L^6/\\delta.$$",

      },

Include the last, possibly incomplete, interval. For sufficiently large $P$, the number of intervals is at most $$\\label{ang:count}\n C(M,A)L^6/\\delta.$$",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **C_b^infty** ([source](#eq:data)): Here $C_b^\infty$ means that every spatial derivative is bounded.
- **dyadic number** ([source](#eq:P-range)): A dyadic number is a power of two.
- **actual and deficit bins** ([source](#occ:lambda)): Call a bin *actual* if $\omega>C_0/m$, and *deficit* otherwise.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-energy-flow** [claim](#stmt-ret-energy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ret-energy): Liouville volume preservation, energy conservation by cutoffs, cone energy identity. -> phase-volume preservation, conserved energy, cone flux bound
- **step-phase-change** [claim](#stmt-ret-phase-change) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ret-phase-change): Rank-one determinant of the retarded map and monotone retarded time equation. -> label potentials with Jacobian
- **step-representation** [claim](#stmt-ret-representation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ret-representation): Kirchhoff formula, implicit differentiation, boundary and cone-tip handling. -> retarded field representation
- **step-pointwise-bounds** [claim](#stmt-ret-direct-bounds) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ret-direct-bounds): Component estimates of the kernels using exact angle identities. -> transport and source majorants
- **step-receiver-time** [claim](#stmt-ret-receiver-time) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ret-receiver-time): Differentiate the retarded relation; grid argument excludes collisions. -> time Jacobian and measure identity
- **step-crude-occupation** [claim](#stmt-occ-crude-lemma) / [proof](#proof-stmt-occ-crude-lemma): Spatial change of variables and label hit duration. -> crude occupation bounds
- **step-cell-occupation** [claim](#stmt-occ-cell-lemma) / [proof](#proof-stmt-occ-cell-lemma): Stable cells from the bootstrap and monotone crossing. -> occupation bound on actual bins
- **step-baseline-occupation** [claim](#stmt-occ-refined-proposition) / [proof](#proof-stmt-occ-refined-proposition): Averaging over cells with a joint energy budget. -> baseline occupation with factor R
- **step-direct-force** [claim](#stmt-dir-direct) / [proof](#sec:direct): Near and far bounds per bin, dyadic balancing, angular and momentum sums. -> integrated force bounds
- **step-signed-identity** [claim](#stmt-can-identity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-can-identity): Product rule along the source branch with exact geometric identities. -> total-derivative form of the kernel
- **step-cutoff-cancellation** [claim](#stmt-can-cutoff-cancellation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-can-cutoff-cancellation): Differentiate the partition of unity. -> cutoff errors on overlaps
- **step-weighted-formula** [claim](#stmt-can-weighted-formula) / [proof](#proof-stmt-can-weighted-formula): Labelwise integration by parts with weights and initial-cone boundary term. -> weighted signed formula
- **step-direction-changes** [claim](#stmt-ang-direction) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ang-direction): First-exit intervals, weighted signed formula, baseline estimates. -> bound on direction changes
- **step-improved-occupation** [claim](#stmt-ang-improved-hit) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ang-improved-hit): Common direction partitions and monotone crossing. -> improved occupation at intermediate angles
- **step-weight-sum** [claim](#stmt-sel-weight-sum) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sel-weight-sum): Geometric sums of the bin weight. -> summable weight
- **step-selected-coefficient** [claim](#stmt-sel-exponents) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sel-exponents): Contradiction by exponent inequalities, three steps. -> receiver coefficient bound
- **step-spatial-sum** [claim](#stmt-sel-spatial-sum) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sel-spatial-sum): Radial crossing balance of near and far bounds. -> spatial cutoff error bound
- **step-time-weight** [claim](#stmt-sel-time-weight) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sel-time-weight): Compare with the central estimate; near-selection inequality. -> time-weight derivative bound
- **step-weighted-impulse** [claim](#stmt-sel-bulk-impulse) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sel-bulk-impulse): Split selected and unselected parts and assemble the bounds. -> weighted impulse estimate
- **step-increment-closure** [claim](#stmt-prop-increment) / [proof](#sec:closure): Edge restoration, strict improvement, continuity argument. -> signed increment estimate
- **step-exterior** [claim](#stmt-cont-exterior) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cont-exterior): Local energy argument on backward cones. -> exterior field comparison
- **step-vacuum** [claim](#stmt-cont-vacuum) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cont-vacuum): Spherical-mean formulas and mollification. -> smooth vacuum fields
- **step-continuation** [claim](#stmt-prop-continuation) / [proof](#sec:continuation-proof): Local Sobolev theory, field modification, bounded-momentum criterion. -> maximal solution and continuation
- **step-global-theorem** [claim](#stmt-thm-global) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-continuation): Contradiction: momentum doubling times have divergent sum. -> global existence and uniqueness

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-global-wellposedness**: Each smooth admissible datum generates exactly one global classical solution of the one-species relativistic Vlasov–Maxwell system, with no size or symmetry restriction on the data.
- **inv-maximal-solution-continuation**: A smooth admissible datum has a unique maximal classical solution, and the solution continues past a finite maximal time whenever its momentum support stays bounded.
- **inv-signed-increment-bound**: Along supported receiver characteristics, momentum increments are bounded by a square-root term plus a term proportional to the interval length, with constants independent of the solution interval and of the dyadic scale parameter.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-software-guarantee**: Treat the global existence theorem as a correctness or termination guarantee for a numerical plasma code. -> The theorem is about exact classical solutions of the continuous system; discretised schemes, truncated momentum supports and rounding are not covered.
- **anti-uniform-momentum-bound**: Assume a single momentum bound valid for all times. -> The momentum support bound holds only on each finite time interval and may grow with that interval.
- **anti-other-data-classes**: Apply the result to data outside the stated class, such as noncompact particle data. -> Existence and uniqueness are proved only for the smooth admissible class with compactly supported particle density.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The results are existence, uniqueness and a priori regularity statements for a nonlinear partial differential system with arbitrary smooth data; the proof is analytic (bootstrap, continuity and compactness arguments) and no finite procedure computes the solution or its constants, so no runnable reference implementation exists.

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result is an existence, uniqueness and regularity theorem for a PDE system. It gives no procedure with inputs and outputs, and no specification that code could be checked against. Any program would need a separate formal model linking it to the hypotheses. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The theorem concerns exact classical solutions of the continuous system on every finite time interval. It says nothing about discretised time stepping, particle-in-cell schemes or real-time behaviour, so it cannot certify a simulation. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper presents no tool, checker or workflow. Its proof devices (dyadic bins, bootstrap, cone energy flux) are analytic arguments and do not form a reusable utility. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No finite procedure is stated. The proof uses a continuity argument, a contradiction with a divergent sum of lower bounds, and non-explicit constants. The solution is not computed by any stated step sequence, so no pseudocode or complexity is claimed. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The dyadic partitions and bins are analytic decompositions used to sum estimates. The paper defines no storage layout, index or update operations. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no kernel, dataflow or numerical precision discussion. The estimates are inequalities on continuous quantities, with no hardware cost model. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper concerns adversaries, secrecy, hardness assumptions or protocols. The system describes a physical collisionless plasma. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper has no learned model, loss or training data. Its large-data hypothesis means arbitrary initial data in an analytic class, not a dataset. The theorem gives no generalisation or optimisation guarantee. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No objective, constraint set or solver appears. The constants M, A and the threshold on P are chosen in a fixed order to close a bootstrap, not to optimise anything. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The model has a single continuum field system and no nodes, messages, failures or consensus. Finite propagation speed of fields is physical and not a communication protocol. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The only natural reuse is within analysis: the signed-increment bootstrap and the bounded-momentum continuation step. These are proof techniques for this system, not transferable engineering constructions, so no engineering contract is claimed. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **lim-admissible-data-class** (hypothesis): The global theorem is stated only for smooth admissible data: nonnegative smooth compactly supported particle density, fields smooth with bounded derivatives and finite energy, and both Gauss constraints. Blocked use: ['Applying the theorem to noncompactly supported particle data', 'Applying the theorem to data violating the divergence constraints']
- **lim-finite-horizon-momentum** (edge_case): The momentum support bound holds on each finite time interval and may grow with that interval, so no uniform-in-time bound follows. Blocked use: ['Quoting one momentum bound valid for all times', 'Deducing long-time asymptotics from the theorem']
- **lim-continuation-bounded-momentum** (hypothesis): The continuation clause requires bounded momentum support up to a finite maximal time; without it no extension is asserted. Blocked use: ['Extending a solution past a finite time without a momentum bound']
- **lim-bootstrap-constants** (formal_scope): The signed increment bound is for dyadic P at least a threshold, with constants M and A fixed from the datum and horizon; the constants are not given explicit values. Blocked use: ['Reading M, A or P_0 as explicit numerical constants', 'Using the bound for P below the threshold']
- **lim-nonconstructive-proof** (nonconstructive): The global result is proved by contradiction from a finite maximal time, using a divergent sum of lower bounds; no algorithm or lifespan formula is produced. Blocked use: ['Treating the argument as a procedure computing solutions or blow-up times', 'Deriving software or numerical guarantees from it']
- **lim-symmetry-comparison** (counterexample_scope): The data class here differs from the cylindrically symmetric noncompact class of other work, so the two results are not interchangeable. Blocked use: ['Identifying this theorem with symmetric large-data results']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 362 (`lean/docs/362.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Global classical solutions of the three-dimensional relativistic Vlasov–Maxwell system](../../preprints/Global-classical-solutions-of-the-three-dimensional-relativistic-Vlasov-Maxwell-system-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalized result gives global existence and uniqueness for the three-dimensional one-species relativistic Vlasov–Maxwell system. The initial particle density is nonnegative, smooth, and compactly supported; the initial fields have bounded derivatives of all orders, finite energy, and satisfy both Gauss constraints. The solution is smooth on every finite time interval, has compact particle phase support there, and is unique among classical solutions. No smallness, symmetry, or neutrality assumption is imposed.

Comparator source `VlasovMaxwell.lean` (4303 bytes, sha256 `67941fd3f10cf129d42dc4ef6ea6b8585fb90ad942108d7d71827ce9960b9cf5`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

noncomputable section
open MeasureTheory Set
open scoped ContDiff
namespace RVM

abbrev Vec := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 3)
abbrev Phase := Vec × Vec
abbrev Particle := ℝ × Phase → ℝ
abbrev Field := ℝ × Vec → Vec

def q (v : Vec) : ℝ := Real.sqrt (1 + ‖v‖ ^ 2)
def velocity (v : Vec) : Vec := (q v)⁻¹ • v

def basis (i : Fin 3) : Vec := WithLp.toLp 2 (Pi.single i (1 : ℝ))
def dot (a b : Vec) : ℝ := ∑ i : Fin 3, a i * b i
def cross (a b : Vec) : Vec := WithLp.toLp 2
  ![a 1 * b 2 - a 2 * b 1, a 2 * b 0 - a 0 * b 2,
    a 0 * b 1 - a 1 * b 0]

def spatialPartial (g : Vec → ℝ) (x : Vec) (i : Fin 3) : ℝ :=
  fderiv ℝ g x (basis i)
def divergence (F : Vec → Vec) (x : Vec) : ℝ :=
  ∑ i : Fin 3, spatialPartial (fun y => F y i) x i

def curl (F : Vec → Vec) (x : Vec) : Vec := WithLp.toLp 2
  ![spatialPartial (fun y => F y 2) x 1 - spatialPartial (fun y => F y 1) x 2,
    spatialPartial (fun y => F y 0) x 2 - spatialPartial (fun y => F y 2) x 0,
    spatialPartial (fun y => F y 1) x 0 - spatialPartial (fun y => F y 0) x 1]

def rho (g : Phase → ℝ) (x : Vec) : ℝ := ∫ v : Vec, g (x, v)
def current (g : Phase → ℝ) (x : Vec) : Vec :=
  ∫ v : Vec, g (x, v) • velocity v

structure Datum where
  f₀ : Phase → ℝ
  E₀ : Vec → Vec
  B₀ : Vec → Vec

def BoundedSmooth (F : Vec → Vec) : Prop :=
  ContDiff ℝ ∞ F ∧ ∀ n : ℕ, ∃ C : ℝ, ∀ x : Vec,
    ‖iteratedFDeriv ℝ n F x‖ ≤ C

def Admissible (d : Datum) : Prop :=
  ContDiff ℝ ∞ d.f₀ ∧ HasCompactSupport d.f₀ ∧ (∀ z, 0 ≤ d.f₀ z) ∧
  BoundedSmooth d.E₀ ∧ BoundedSmooth d.B₀ ∧
  MemLp d.E₀ 2 volume ∧ MemLp d.B₀ 2 volume ∧
  (∀ x, divergence d.E₀ x = rho d.f₀ x) ∧
  (∀ x, divergence d.B₀ x = 0)

structure Solution where
  f : Particle
  E : Field
  B : Field

def particleHalfSpace : Set (ℝ × Phase) := {z | 0 ≤ z.1}
def fieldHalfSpace : Set (ℝ × Vec) := {z | 0 ≤ z.1}
def particleSlab (T : ℝ) : Set (ℝ × Phase) := {z | z.1 ∈ Icc 0 T}
def fieldSlab (T : ℝ) : Set (ℝ × Vec) := {z | z.1 ∈ Icc 0 T}

def TimeContinuousL2 (F : Field) : Prop :=
  ∃ F₂ : ℝ → Lp Vec 2 (volume : Measure Vec),
    ContinuousOn F₂ (Ici 0) ∧
    ∀ t, 0 ≤ t → (F₂ t : Vec → Vec) =ᵐ[volume] (fun x => F (t, x))

def Equations (s : Solution) : Prop := ∀ t, 0 ≤ t →
  (∀ x v,
    derivWithin (fun t' => s.f (t', x, v)) (Ici 0) t +
    (∑ i : Fin 3, velocity v i * spatialPartial (fun y => s.f (t, y, v)) x i) +
    (∑ i : Fin 3, (s.E (t, x) + cross (velocity v) (s.B (t, x))) i *
      spatialPartial (fun v' => s.f (t, x, v')) v i) = 0) ∧
  (∀ x, derivWithin (fun t' => s.E (t', x)) (Ici 0) t -
    curl (fun y => s.B (t, y)) x = -current (fun z => s.f (t, z)) x) ∧
  (∀ x, derivWithin (fun t' => s.B (t', x)) (Ici 0) t +
    curl (fun y => s.E (t, y)) x = 0) ∧
  (∀ x, divergence (fun y => s.E (t, y)) x = rho (fun z => s.f (t, z)) x) ∧
  (∀ x, divergence (fun y => s.B (t, y)) x = 0)

def CompactOnFiniteHorizons (s : Solution) : Prop :=
  ∀ T : ℝ, 0 ≤ T → ∃ K : Set Phase, IsCompact K ∧
    ∀ t ∈ Icc 0 T, tsupport (fun z => s.f (t, z)) ⊆ K

def Classical (d : Datum) (s : Solution) : Prop :=
  ContDiffOn ℝ 1 s.f particleHalfSpace ∧
  ContDiffOn ℝ 1 s.E fieldHalfSpace ∧ ContDiffOn ℝ 1 s.B fieldHalfSpace ∧
  (∀ t, 0 ≤ t → ∀ z, 0 ≤ s.f (t, z)) ∧
  TimeContinuousL2 s.E ∧ TimeContinuousL2 s.B ∧
  CompactOnFiniteHorizons s ∧ Equations s ∧
  (∀ z, s.f (0, z) = d.f₀ z) ∧
  (∀ x, s.E (0, x) = d.E₀ x) ∧ (∀ x, s.B (0, x) = d.B₀ x)

def SmoothOnFiniteHorizons (s : Solution) : Prop := ∀ T : ℝ, 0 ≤ T →
  ContDiffOn ℝ ∞ s.f (particleSlab T) ∧
  ContDiffOn ℝ ∞ s.E (fieldSlab T) ∧ ContDiffOn ℝ ∞ s.B (fieldSlab T)

def SameNonnegativeTime (s s' : Solution) : Prop := ∀ t, 0 ≤ t →
  (∀ z, s.f (t, z) = s'.f (t, z)) ∧
  (∀ x, s.E (t, x) = s'.E (t, x)) ∧ (∀ x, s.B (t, x) = s'.B (t, x))

theorem global_classical_solution (d : Datum) (hd : Admissible d) :
    ∃ s : Solution, Classical d s ∧ SmoothOnFiniteHorizons s ∧
      ∀ s' : Solution, Classical d s' → SameNonnegativeTime s s' := by
  sorry

end RVM

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove global existence and uniqueness for arbitrary smooth admissible initial data in the three-dimensional, one-species relativistic Vlasov–Maxwell system. The particle density is initially compactly supported, and the electromagnetic fields have finite energy and bounded derivatives of all orders. The solution remains smooth on every finite time interval. This resolves the large-data global classical regularity problem for this model, without size or symmetry restrictions on the data.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

<a id="eq:rvm"></a>
The relativistic Vlasov–Maxwell system describes a collisionless plasma whose particles move under their self-consistent electromagnetic field. For momentum $v\in\mathbb R^3$, write $$q(v)=\sqrt{1+|v|^2},\qquad u(v)=\frac{v}{q(v)}.$$ The particle density is $f=f(t,x,v)\ge0$; the electric and magnetic fields are $E=E(t,x)$ and $B=B(t,x)$. We use the one-species normalization $$\label{eq:rvm}
\begin{aligned}
 \partial_t f+u(v)\cdot\nabla_x f+
     (E+u(v)\times B)\cdot\nabla_v f&=0,\\
 \partial_t E-\nabla_x\times B&=-j_f,
 &\partial_t B+\nabla_x\times E&=0,\\
 \nabla_x\cdot E&=\rho_f,
 &\nabla_x\cdot B&=0,
\end{aligned}$$ where $$\rho_f(t,x)=\int_{\mathbb R^3}f(t,x,v)\,dv,
 \qquad j_f(t,x)=\int_{\mathbb R^3}u(v)f(t,x,v)\,dv.$$ There is no background charge.

<a id="eq:data"></a>
A *smooth admissible datum* is a triple $(f_0,E_0,B_0)$ satisfying $$\label{eq:data}
\begin{gathered}
 f_0\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^3_x\times\mathbb R^3_v),\qquad f_0\ge0,\\
 E_0,B_0\in C_b^\infty(\mathbb R^3;\mathbb R^3)\cap L^2(\mathbb R^3;\mathbb R^3),\\
 \nabla_x\cdot E_0=\rho_{f_0},\qquad \nabla_x\cdot B_0=0.
\end{gathered}$$ Here $C_b^\infty$ means that every spatial derivative is bounded. A *classical solution* on $[0,T)$ is a $C^1$ triple solving <a href="#eq:rvm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rvm">[eq:rvm]</a> pointwise, with $E,B\in C([0,T);L^2_x)$, such that the support of $f$ in $(x,v)$ is contained in a compact set on every compact time subinterval.

<a id="stmt-thm-global"></a>
<div id="thm:global" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Every smooth admissible datum <a href="#eq:data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:data">[eq:data]</a> generates a unique global classical solution of <a href="#eq:rvm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rvm">[eq:rvm]</a>. For every finite $T$, this solution satisfies $$f\in C^\infty([0,T]\times\mathbb R^3_x\times\mathbb R^3_v),\qquad
 E,B\in C^\infty([0,T]\times\mathbb R^3_x;\mathbb R^3),$$ and $f$ has compact phase-space support on $[0,T]$. The two divergence constraints hold for all time.*

</div>

Theorem <a href="#thm:global" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:global">1</a> resolves the large-data global classical regularity problem for this one-species model in three spatial and three momentum dimensions. The data have no size or symmetry restriction. The field assumptions allow a nonzero total charge and its Coulomb tail; they do not require square-integrability of all field derivatives. The momentum support bound is on each finite time interval and may grow with that interval.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Historical context

The distinction between weak existence and classical regularity has shaped the Cauchy problem for Vlasov–Maxwell. Wollman established local existence and uniqueness for classical solutions (Wollman 1984) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Wollman1984](#ref-Wollman1984)]. DiPerna and Lions developed global weak existence for large data (DiPerna and Lions 1989) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[DiPernaLions1989](#ref-DiPernaLions1989)]; a direct formulation for the relativistic one-species system is given by Rein (Rein 2004) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Rein2004Weak](#ref-Rein2004Weak)]. These weak existence results do not furnish the regularity and uniqueness asserted in Theorem <a href="#thm:global" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:global">1</a>.

For classical solutions, Glassey and Strauss identified bounded momentum support as a continuation condition (R. T. Glassey and Strauss 1986) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[GlasseyStrauss1986](#ref-GlasseyStrauss1986)] and proved global existence for initially dilute plasmas (R. T. Glassey and Strauss 1987) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[GlasseyStrauss1987](#ref-GlasseyStrauss1987)]. Global classical existence was also established by Glassey and Schaeffer in dimensional reductions: one and one-half dimensions, two and one-half dimensions, and the planar problem (Robert Glassey and Schaeffer 1990; R. Glassey and Schaeffer 1997; R. T. Glassey and Schaeffer 1998a, 1998b) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[GlasseySchaeffer1990OneHalf](#ref-GlasseySchaeffer1990OneHalf), [GlasseySchaeffer1997TwoHalf](#ref-GlasseySchaeffer1997TwoHalf), [GlasseySchaeffer1998PlanarI](#ref-GlasseySchaeffer1998PlanarI), [GlasseySchaeffer1998PlanarII](#ref-GlasseySchaeffer1998PlanarII)]. For the spherically symmetric electrostatic reduction, Glassey and Schaeffer proved global classical existence for the repulsive relativistic Vlasov–Poisson system (R. T. Glassey and Schaeffer 1985) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[GlasseySchaeffer1985Symmetric](#ref-GlasseySchaeffer1985Symmetric)]. Rein proved persistence of global existence under small perturbations of suitably decaying global reference solutions, including nearly symmetric configurations (Rein 1990) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Rein1990Generic](#ref-Rein1990Generic)]. These results use restrictions on size, dimension, or the reference configuration.

The continuation theory also led to new methods. Klainerman and Staffilani gave a Fourier-based proof of the bounded-momentum criterion (Klainerman and Staffilani 2002) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[KlainermanStaffilani2002](#ref-KlainermanStaffilani2002)], while Bouchut, Golse and Pallard gave a physical-space proof using a division lemma for wave kernels (Bouchut, Golse, and Pallard 2003) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[BouchutGolsePallard2003](#ref-BouchutGolsePallard2003)]. Luk and Strain showed that it suffices to control the momentum support after projection onto a fixed two-dimensional plane (Luk and Strain 2014, Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[LukStrain2014Continuation](#ref-LukStrain2014Continuation)]. Moment and spatial-density conditions for continuation were developed by Pallard (Pallard 2005, 2015) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Pallard2005](#ref-Pallard2005), [Pallard2015Refined](#ref-Pallard2015Refined)], Sospedra–Alfonso and Illner (Sospedra-Alfonso and Illner 2010) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[SospedraAlfonsoIllner2010](#ref-SospedraAlfonsoIllner2010)], and Luk and Strain (Luk and Strain 2016) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[LukStrain2016Moments](#ref-LukStrain2016Moments)] <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[LukStrain2014Continuation](#ref-LukStrain2014Continuation)]. We use the Glassey–Strauss criterion as stated in Luk and Strain (Luk and Strain 2014, Theorem 1.1); Section <a href="#cont:continuation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cont:continuation">10.5</a> verifies its hypotheses for the normalization and initial field class considered here.

Small-data theory also developed beyond compact momentum support. Schaeffer allowed a rapidly decaying momentum tail, retaining compact spatial support and a suitable smallness condition (Schaeffer 2004) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Schaeffer2004](#ref-Schaeffer2004)]. Using a vector-field method with modified commutators, Bigorgne obtained global existence and sharp asymptotic estimates under weighted smallness assumptions (Bigorgne 2020) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Bigorgne2020](#ref-Bigorgne2020)]. His result permits nonzero charge and noncompact particle data. Wang proved a small-data result without compact support using vector-field and Fourier methods (Wang 2022) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Wang2022SmallData](#ref-Wang2022SmallData)]. Wei and Yang allow arbitrarily large Maxwell fields with suitable spatial decay while requiring a sufficiently small weighted particle density (Wei and Yang 2021) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[WeiYang2021](#ref-WeiYang2021)]. Bigorgne subsequently proved global existence and modified scattering with a small distribution function and a large Maxwell field (Bigorgne 2025) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Bigorgne2025SmallDistribution](#ref-Bigorgne2025SmallDistribution)]. These theorems preserve a particle-smallness hypothesis. By prescribing asymptotic data and choosing a sufficiently late starting time, Bigorgne also constructs particular global solutions with large particle and field norms (Bigorgne 2023) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Bigorgne2023ScatteringMap](#ref-Bigorgne2023ScatteringMap)]. This construction does not prescribe arbitrary Cauchy data.

Wang’s July 2026 two-paper treatment establishes global existence for large cylindrically symmetric data (Wang 2026a, 2026b) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Wang2026I](#ref-Wang2026I), [Wang2026II](#ref-Wang2026II)]. The first paper develops an iterative smoothing construction and the second supplies pointwise estimates. Their Theorem 1.1 assumes $H^s$ fields, $s\ge6$, a weighted $H^s$ particle density, and covariance of the data under rotations about a fixed axis. It allows localized noncompact particle data and has no smallness requirement. The theorem here instead concerns arbitrary compatible compactly supported particle data, without symmetry, and fields in the class <a href="#eq:data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:data">[eq:data]</a>. Thus the two data classes should not be identified.

The formulas used below have several classical antecedents. The retarded-field representation goes back to Glassey and Strauss (R. T. Glassey and Strauss 1986) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[GlasseyStrauss1986](#ref-GlasseyStrauss1986)]. Luk and Strain organize its most singular terms using electromagnetic components controlled by energy flux through light cones (Luk and Strain 2014, Proposition 2.2 and Section 3) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[LukStrain2014Continuation](#ref-LukStrain2014Continuation)]; Pallard also uses the orthogonality of the acceleration kernel to the cone’s spatial normal (Pallard 2015, Lemma 2.1 and Section 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Pallard2015Refined](#ref-Pallard2015Refined)]. The change from retarded phase points to initial particle labels appears in Lazarovici (Lazarovici 2016, Lemma 9.2) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Lazarovici2016](#ref-Lazarovici2016)]. The moving-characteristic cone change of variables is used by Pallard (Pallard 2005) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Pallard2005](#ref-Pallard2005)]; its proof is also given by Luk and Strain (Luk and Strain 2014, Proposition 5.2) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[LukStrain2014Continuation](#ref-LukStrain2014Continuation)]. We derive the required formulas in the normalization <a href="#eq:rvm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:rvm">[eq:rvm]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Proof strategy

The central estimate concerns the *signed* change of a particle’s momentum. Along a characteristic $(X(t),V_X(t))$, with $X'=u(V_X)$ and $V_X'=E(t,X)+u(V_X)\times B(t,X)$, it is the impulse $$V_X(t_2)-V_X(t_1)
 =\int_{t_1}^{t_2}
   \bigl(E(t,X(t))+u(V_X(t))\times B(t,X(t))\bigr)\,dt.$$ We take the norm after integration. Section <a href="#sec:setup" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:setup">2</a> states the estimate precisely and shows that it places a lower bound on the time needed to double the largest particle momentum. These lower bounds have a divergent sum, which rules out unbounded momentum support in finite time and allows the continuation criterion to apply.

To estimate the impulse, we follow source particles to their intersections with the backward light cone of the particle receiving the force. We divide the source contribution into ranges of momentum, distance, and angles between the source velocity, receiver velocity, and light ray. Energy flux through the cone and bounds on the time that source particles spend in these ranges give the direct force estimates of Sections <a href="#occ:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="occ:section">4</a> and <a href="#sec:direct" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:direct">5</a>. At receiver energy comparable to $w$, the absolute-force bound has an extra factor $\sqrt w$ compared with the signed estimate we need. The loss occurs when the source velocity is much closer to the light-ray direction than the receiver velocity is.

In this narrow angular region, we integrate the retarded force along a source trajectory before taking absolute values. The identity in Section <a href="#can:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:section">6</a> combines the part without source acceleration with the part containing it. The former cancels a term generated by differentiating the cone geometry. The force is thereby written as a total derivative plus less singular bulk terms, one of which contains the receiver’s acceleration. Expressing that acceleration through $V_X'$ turns this into an error proportional to the receiver force. The proof must control its coefficient as well as the other integration-by-parts errors.

We use the signed identity twice, under an assumed bound on all signed momentum increments. First, after projection perpendicular to a particle’s momentum, it bounds the number of direction changes of a prescribed angular size. This step uses only the preliminary occupation and direct force estimates. When both source and receiver momenta are large, direction control gives a stronger occupation bound at intermediate relative angles: between changes of direction, relative motion makes a monotone crossing of the region contributing to a fixed range. Section <a href="#ang:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ang:section">7</a> proves the direction bound before using it to improve occupation.

For the second use, Section <a href="#sel:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sel:section">8</a> selects only those ranges whose direct force estimates exceed a summable threshold. The other ranges already satisfy the required bound. On the selected ranges, the coefficient multiplying the receiver force is at most a constant times $w^{-1/2}$ after summation. This exactly compensates for the $\sqrt w$ loss in the available absolute-force bound. The occupation improvement need only hold in the stated angular window: the selection inequalities force any possible obstruction to this coefficient bound into that window, where improved occupation rules it out. This yields an impulse estimate with a time weight that vanishes at the interval endpoints. Section <a href="#sec:closure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:closure">9</a> restores the short endpoint intervals using the assumed signed bound, obtains a strict improvement, and closes the bootstrap by continuity.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Organization

Section <a href="#sec:setup" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:setup">2</a> states the signed estimate and deduces Theorem <a href="#thm:global" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:global">1</a> from it and a continuation result. Sections <a href="#ret:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ret:section">3</a>–<a href="#sec:direct" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:direct">5</a> derive the retarded force, occupation estimates and direct bounds. Section <a href="#can:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:section">6</a> proves the signed cancellation; Section <a href="#ang:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ang:section">7</a> bounds direction changes and improves occupation. Section <a href="#sel:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sel:section">8</a> estimates the selected signed force, and Section <a href="#sec:closure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:closure">9</a> closes the bootstrap. Section <a href="#sec:continuation-proof" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:continuation-proof">10</a> proves local existence, uniqueness, smooth persistence and the field modification needed to apply the bounded-momentum continuation theorem to <a href="#eq:data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:data">[eq:data]</a>.

<a id="sec:setup"></a>
### The signed momentum estimate and its consequence

Our analytic goal is an estimate on momentum increments along characteristics. We state it first and explain why its logarithmic time scale excludes finite-time breakdown. Its proof occupies the central sections of the paper.

Fix a finite horizon $T_0>0$ and a compact interval $[0,t_*]\subset[0,T_{\max})$, with $t_*\le T_0$, of a classical solution. A characteristic with initial label $z=(y_{\rm in},v_{\rm in})$ solves $$Y'(s,z)=u(V(s,z)),\qquad
 V'(s,z)=E(s,Y(s,z))+u(V(s,z))\times B(s,Y(s,z)).$$ It is *supported* if $z\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}f_0$. A distinguished supported characteristic, along which we estimate the force, is written $(X(t),V_X(t))$ and called the *receiver*. We write $$a(t)=u(V_X(t)),\quad q_X(t)=q(V_X(t)),\quad K(t)=V_X'(t).$$ The signed increment is the vector impulse $$V_X(t_2)-V_X(t_1)=\int_{t_1}^{t_2}K(t)\,dt.$$ Source characteristics continue to use the letters $Y,V,u$.

<a id="eq:P-range"></a>
For nonempty $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}f_0$, choose a dyadic number $P$ such that $$\label{eq:P-range}
 P\ge1024\max_{\substack{0\le t\le t_*\\z\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}f_0}}q(V(t,z)),
 \qquad L=\log(2+P),\qquad S(w)=\frac{P^2L}{w}.$$ A dyadic number is a power of two. Receiver scales are $w=2^k$, $k\ge0$. In every nonempty range $w/8\le q_X\le8w$ one has $w\le P$.

<a id="stmt-prop-increment"></a>
<div id="prop:increment" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:bootstrap"></a>
**Proposition 2** (Signed momentum increments). *For each smooth admissible datum and finite $T_0$, there are constants $M,A\ge1$ and $P_0$ with the following property. On every compact classical solution interval $[0,t_*]$ as above, for every dyadic $P\ge P_0$ satisfying <a href="#eq:P-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:P-range">[eq:P-range]</a>, every supported receiver, every $w=2^k$, $k\ge0$, and every interval $[t_1,t_2]\subset[0,t_*]$ on which $w/8\le q_X(t)\le8w$ throughout, one has $$\label{eq:bootstrap}
 |V_X(t_2)-V_X(t_1)|
 \le MP\sqrt{t_2-t_1}+A(t_2-t_1)S(w).$$ The constants are independent of $t_*$ and $P$.*

</div>

<a id="stmt-prop-continuation"></a>
<div id="prop:continuation" class="proposition">

**Proposition 3** (Local theory and continuation). *Every smooth admissible datum has a unique maximal classical solution. It is smooth on each compact subinterval of its lifespan. If its maximal time $T_{\max}$ is finite and $$\sup\{\,|v|:0\le t<T_{\max},\ (x,v)\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}f(t)\,\}<\infty,$$ then the solution has a classical extension beyond $T_{\max}$.*

</div>

The proof is given in the final section. It combines the Glassey–Strauss bounded-momentum criterion in the $H^5$ formulation quoted by Luk and Strain (Luk and Strain 2014, Theorem 1.1 and Footnote 1, arXiv version) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[LukStrain2014Continuation](#ref-LukStrain2014Continuation)] with a finite-horizon modification of the initial fields. The modification retains the initial Coulomb field and cuts off vector potentials for the divergence-free remainders. The removed part evolves as a vacuum Maxwell field that vanishes throughout the particle region for the chosen horizon. This supplies Sobolev field data for the continuation theorem while preserving the original particle dynamics.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-continuation"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-continuation</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:global" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:global">1</a> from Propositions <a href="#prop:increment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:increment">2</a> and <a href="#prop:continuation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:continuation">3</a>.* If $f_0=0$, the vacuum Maxwell solution is global and smooth, as shown in the final section. Suppose $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}f_0\ne\varnothing$ and, toward a contradiction, $T_{\max}<\infty$. Proposition <a href="#prop:continuation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:continuation">3</a> then forces unbounded momentum support. Set $T_0=T_{\max}$ and $$Q(t)=\max_{z\in\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}f_0}q(V(t,z)).$$ Continuity of the characteristic flow and compactness of the label set make $Q$ continuous. For all sufficiently large integers $n$, let $t_n<T_{\max}$ be the first time when $Q(t_n)=2^n$. Choose a label attaining this value. Along that label let $s_n$ be its last time before $t_n$ at energy $2^{n-1}$. Such a time exists because the initial energy is smaller; moreover $s_n\ge t_{n-1}$. On $[s_n,t_n]$ the energy lies in $[2^{n-1},2^n]$.

Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:increment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:increment">2</a> on the prefix $[0,t_n]$ with $P=1024\cdot2^n$ and $w=2^n$. Since $q$ is $1$-Lipschitz, writing $\Delta_n=t_n-s_n$ gives $$2^{n-1}\le |V(t_n)-V(s_n)|
 \le 1024M\,2^n\sqrt{\Delta_n}
   +1024^2A\,2^n\Delta_n\log(2+1024\cdot2^n).$$ At least one term on the right is at least $2^{n-2}$. Consequently $$t_n-t_{n-1}\ge\Delta_n
 \ge \frac{c}{\log(2+1024\cdot2^n)}$$ for a fixed $c>0$. The sum of these lower bounds diverges, contradicting $t_n<T_{\max}$. Thus $T_{\max}=\infty$. Uniqueness, smoothness and propagation of the constraints follow from Proposition <a href="#prop:continuation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:continuation">3</a> and its proof. ◻

</details>

To prove Proposition <a href="#prop:increment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:increment">2</a>, we assume <a href="#eq:bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bootstrap">[eq:bootstrap]</a> for all such receivers, scales and intervals in $[0,\tau]$, where $\tau\le t_*$, and derive a uniform strict improvement. Until that improvement is proved, <a href="#eq:bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bootstrap">[eq:bootstrap]</a> is a bootstrap assumption. In the estimates, $C$ and the constants implicit in $\lesssim$ or $\asymp$ may depend on the datum, $T_0$, and fixed decomposition parameters, but not on $t_*,P,M,A$. Additional dependence is indicated explicitly. Lower thresholds on $P$ may depend on $M,A$. The final choices are made in the order: a time-ramp fraction, then $M$, then $A$, then the threshold on $P$.

For nonnegative estimates we allow integration over any measurable subset of receiver times in an interval $J\subset[0,\tau]$ of length at most $I$, provided those times satisfy $w/8\le q_X\le8w$ and <a id="eq:short-I"></a>$$\label{eq:short-I}
 0<I\le (w/P)^2.$$ The number $I$ is an upper bound on length in these estimates. For the final signed estimate the interval has length exactly $I$ and the receiver stays in the indicated range throughout. This distinction permits summation over disjoint time pieces without introducing a new constant for each piece.

Figure <a href="#fig:dependencies" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:dependencies">1</a> records the conditional dependencies. We now prove Proposition <a href="#prop:increment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:increment">2</a>. Energy and timelike geometry give preliminary bounds on absolute force. Those bounds lose a factor at large receiver momentum; the subsequent signed cancellation removes this loss.

<figure id="fig:dependencies">

<figcaption>Main dependencies in the conditional proof of the signed momentum estimate. The dashed box is a bootstrap assumption. The first use of signed cancellation controls particle direction from this assumption and the baseline estimates; improved occupation is obtained afterward. The second use yields a strict improvement, and a continuity argument establishes the initially assumed bound.</figcaption>
</figure>
