<a id="ret:section"></a>
### Energy and the retarded force

The estimates below use the conserved energy and two different changes of variables on a backward light cone. We first derive the force kernels without derivatives of the density. This representation belongs to the Glassey–Strauss approach (R. T. Glassey and Strauss 1986) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[GlasseyStrauss1986](#ref-GlasseyStrauss1986)]; the organization by cone-controlled field components also appears in (Luk and Strain 2014, Proposition 2.2 and Section 3) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[LukStrain2014Continuation](#ref-LukStrain2014Continuation)]. We give the source-label calculation explicitly, including its initial boundary term.

Throughout this section, fix a classical solution on a compact interval $[0,t_*]$, with $t_*\leq T_0<\infty$, in the class specified in the main theorem. All limiting operations are justified on this fixed compact interval. Constants in the final bounds depend only on the initial data and $T_0$; constants used solely to remove a cutoff may also depend on the compact-interval momentum support. Retain the characteristic notation fixed in Section <a href="#sec:setup" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:setup">2</a>.

<a id="stmt-ret-energy"></a>
<div id="ret:energy" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:energy"></a>
<a id="eq:cone-energy"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Transport and cone energy). *The characteristic flow preserves phase volume and transports $f_0$. In particular $0\leq f\leq\|f_0\|_\infty$. Define $$\mathcal U=\frac{|E|^2+|B|^2}{2}+\int qf\,dv,
 \qquad
 \mathcal S=E\times B+\int quf\,dv,
 \qquad
 \mathcal H_0=\int_{\mathbb R^3}\mathcal U(0,x)\,dx.$$ Then $$\label{eq:energy}
 \int_{\mathbb R^3}\mathcal U(s,x)\,dx=\mathcal H_0
 \qquad(0\leq s\leq t_*).$$ For every vertex $(t,x)$ in this interval, put $r=t-s$, $y=x-rn$, $n\in S^2$, and $$d=1-n\cdot u(v),\qquad
 G=|E(s,y)+n\times B(s,y)|+|n\cdot B(s,y)|.$$ Then $$\label{eq:cone-energy}
 \int_0^t\int_{S^2}r^2
 \left(G^2+\int qd f(s,x-rn,v)\,dv\right)dn\,ds
 \leq4\mathcal H_0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ret-energy"><summary>Proof of stmt-ret-energy</summary>

*Proof.* The phase vector field has zero divergence: $u$ is independent of $x$, $E$ is independent of $v$, and $\nabla_v\cdot(u\times B)=B\cdot(\nabla_v\times\nabla_v q)=0$. Liouville’s formula gives volume preservation, and the Vlasov equation gives $f(s,Y,V)=f_0(z)$. The flow exists backwards from every finite phase point on this compact time interval. Indeed, its spatial path stays in a bounded ball because $|u|<1$; the fields are bounded on the resulting compact spacetime set, so its momentum cannot escape in finite time.

Multiplication of Vlasov by $q$ and integration in $v$, followed by Maxwell’s energy identity, give $$\partial_s\mathcal U+\nabla_x\cdot\mathcal S=0,
 \qquad |\mathcal S|\leq\mathcal U.$$ There is no momentum boundary term because $f$ has compact phase-space support on the interval. Integrate against spatial cutoffs equal to one on a ball of radius $R$, with gradient bounded by $C/R$. The error tends to zero: the fields are bounded in $C([0,t_*];L^2)$ and the particle energy has compact support and is continuous in time. Passing to the limit proves conservation.

The outward spatial normal of the ball $|y-x|<t-s$ is $-n$. Its energy derivative is therefore minus the boundary integral of $\mathcal U-n\cdot\mathcal S$. At the cone tip the ball energy tends to zero by local continuity. Hence the exact cone identity is $$\int_0^t\int_{S^2}r^2
   (\mathcal U-n\cdot\mathcal S)(s,x-rn)\,dn\,ds
 =\int_{|y-x|<t}\mathcal U(0,y)\,dy.$$ Its particle part is $\int qd f\,dv$, and its field part is $$\frac12\bigl(|E+n\times B|^2+|n\cdot B|^2\bigr).$$ Since $(a+b)^2\leq2(a^2+b^2)$, the last identity implies <a href="#eq:cone-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cone-energy">[eq:cone-energy]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The retarded source labels

<a id="ret:potentials"></a>
For fixed $t,x$, set $r=|x-y|$, $s=t-r$, and $n=(x-y)/r$ when $r>0$. The zero-data wave potentials are $$\label{ret:potentials}
 (\Phi,\mathcal A)(t,x)=\frac1{4\pi}
  \int_{|x-y|<t}\int_{\mathbb R^3}
       \frac{f(t-|x-y|,y,v)}{|x-y|}(1,u(v))\,dv\,dy.$$

The following change to source labels also appears in (Lazarovici 2016, sec. 9, Lemma 9.2) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[Lazarovici2016](#ref-Lazarovici2016)]. We give the calculation for the present flow.

<a id="stmt-ret-phase-change"></a>
<div id="ret:phase-change" class="lemma">

<a id="ret:phase-jacobian"></a>
<a id="ret:time-equation"></a>
<a id="ret:label-potentials"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Retarded phase change). *Away from the cone tip, the map sending $(y,v)$ at time $s=t-|x-y|$ to its initial label is one-to-one, with Jacobian $$\label{ret:phase-jacobian}
 dz=d\,dy\,dv,\qquad d=1-n\cdot u(v)>0.$$ Its image consists, up to the null set of tip labels, of $$\mathcal L_{t,x}=\{z:|x-Y(0,z)|<t\}.$$ For such a label, the retarded time solves $$\label{ret:time-equation}
 s+|x-Y(s,z)|=t.$$ Consequently <a href="#ret:potentials" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ret:potentials">[ret:potentials]</a> equals $$\label{ret:label-potentials}
 (\Phi,\mathcal A)(t,x)=\frac1{4\pi}
       \int_{\mathcal L_{t,x}} f_0(z)\frac{(1,u(V(s,z)))}{rd}\,dz.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ret-phase-change"><summary>Proof of stmt-ret-phase-change</summary>

*Proof.* Let $Z_s$ be the fixed-time inverse phase flow. Its determinant is one by Lemma <a href="#ret:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ret:energy">4</a>, and $\partial_sZ_s=-D Z_s\,(u,V')$. Since $\nabla_y s=n$, the derivative of $Z_{t-|x-y|}(y,v)$ is the fixed-time derivative multiplied by $$\mathrm{Id}-(u,V')\otimes(n,0).$$ The determinant of this rank-one perturbation is $1-n\cdot u=d$.

Along a fixed label, the function on the left of <a href="#ret:time-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ret:time-equation">[ret:time-equation]</a> is strictly increasing. More explicitly, on any compact part of that characteristic, its increment between $s_1<s_2$ is at least $(1-\sup|u|)(s_2-s_1)>0$. It starts below $t$ exactly for labels in $\mathcal L_{t,x}$ and at $s=t$ is at least $t$. Thus it has exactly one root. The root is a tip only if $Y(t,z)=x$. This set has phase Lebesgue measure zero, by the volume-preserving fixed-time flow and Fubini’s theorem. This proves injectivity and the image assertion. Transport of $f$ and <a href="#ret:phase-jacobian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ret:phase-jacobian">[ret:phase-jacobian]</a> now give <a href="#ret:label-potentials" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ret:label-potentials">[ret:label-potentials]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### The exact electric and magnetic kernels

Along a source characteristic write <a id="ret:source-acceleration"></a>$$\label{ret:source-acceleration}
 b=u'=\frac{\mathrm{Id}-u\otimes u}{q}(E+u\times B),$$ where these fields, $u$, and $q$ are evaluated at its retarded point.

<a id="stmt-ret-representation"></a>
<div id="ret:representation" class="proposition">

<a id="ret:initial-boundary"></a>
<a id="ret:kernel"></a>
<a id="ret:data-bound"></a>
**Proposition 6** (Retarded field representation). *Let $E^{\rm fr},B^{\rm fr}$ be the homogeneous wave solutions with data $$(E^{\rm fr},\partial_tE^{\rm fr})|_{t=0}
       =(E_0,\nabla\times B_0),\qquad
 (B^{\rm fr},\partial_tB^{\rm fr})|_{t=0}
       =(B_0,-\nabla\times E_0).$$ For $t>0$, define initial boundary terms by $$\label{ret:initial-boundary}
 \begin{split}
 (E^{\rm bd},B^{\rm bd})(t,x)
 =\frac1{4\pi t}\int_{|x-y_0|=t}\int_{\mathbb R^3}
 \frac{f_0(y_0,v_0)}{d_0}
 \bigl(n_0-u_0,\ n_0\times(n_0-u_0)\bigr)\,dv_0\,dS_{y_0},\\
 n_0=(x-y_0)/t,\qquad u_0=u(v_0),\qquad d_0=1-n_0\cdot u_0.
 \end{split}$$ Set these terms to zero at $t=0$. Then <a id="ret:fields"></a>$$\label{ret:fields}
 \begin{split}
 E(t,x)&=E^{\rm fr}(t,x)+E^{\rm bd}(t,x)
                  +\frac1{4\pi}\int_{\mathcal L_{t,x}}f_0(z)\,\mathscr K\,dz,\\
 B(t,x)&=B^{\rm fr}(t,x)+B^{\rm bd}(t,x)
                  +\frac1{4\pi}\int_{\mathcal L_{t,x}}f_0(z)\,(n\times\mathscr K)\,dz,
 \end{split}$$ where $$\label{ret:kernel}
 \mathscr K=\frac{n-u}{r^2q^2d^3}
       +\frac{(n-u)(n\cdot b)-db}{rd^3}.$$ All these integrals are well defined at the stated classical regularity. Moreover, $$\label{ret:data-bound}
 \sup_{0\leq t\leq T_0,\ x\in\mathbb R^3}
 (|E^{\rm fr}|+|B^{\rm fr}|+|E^{\rm bd}|+|B^{\rm bd}|)
 \leq C_{T_0,f_0,E_0,B_0}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ret-representation"><summary>Proof of stmt-ret-representation</summary>

*Proof.* *Wave representation.* Kirchhoff’s formula gives $\Box(\Phi,\mathcal A)=(\rho_f,j_f)$ with zero data, where $\Box=\partial_t^2-\Delta$. Maxwell’s equations give, distributionally, $$\Box E=-\nabla\rho_f-\partial_tj_f,\qquad
 \Box B=\nabla\times j_f.$$ Thus the fields are $(-\nabla\Phi-\partial_t\mathcal A,\nabla\times\mathcal A)$ plus the stated homogeneous waves. Here it is important that the electric potential contribution has initial time derivative $-j_f(0)$.

We justify this assertion with only $C^1$ sources. In the variables $y=x-rn$, the potentials integrate a $C^1$ source against $r\,dr\,dn$. Their first derivatives are continuous, and locally uniformly in $x$, $$\partial_t\mathcal A(t,x)=t j_f(0,x)+O(t^2),\qquad
 \nabla\Phi(t,x),\ \nabla\mathcal A(t,x)=O(t^2).$$ These formulas give the required zero initial values and initial difference quotients of the potential fields. For completeness, uniqueness of the resulting continuous distributional wave remainder can be reduced to classical uniqueness: convolve spatially with a compact smooth mollifier. The wave equation then supplies a continuous second time derivative, including at zero; the initial difference quotients identify its zero initial velocity. Local wave energy gives zero, and removal of the mollifier gives the assertion. No second derivative of $f$ is used.

*Interior kernels.* To differentiate <a href="#ret:label-potentials" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ret:label-potentials">[ret:label-potentials]</a>, hold the initial label fixed. Implicit differentiation of <a href="#ret:time-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ret:time-equation">[ret:time-equation]</a> gives $$s_t=1/d,\qquad\nabla_xs=-n/d.$$ Write $\ell=(rd)^{-1}$. With $s$ held fixed, $$\nabla_x\ell=-\frac{n-u}{r^2d^2}.$$ With $x$ fixed, $$r_s=-n\cdot u,\qquad
 n_s=-\frac{u-n(n\cdot u)}r,\qquad
 \ell_s=\frac{q^{-2}-d}{r^2d^2}+\frac{n\cdot b}{rd^2}.$$ Therefore the interior electric kernel is $$-\nabla_x^{\rm total}\ell-\partial_t(u\ell)
 =\frac{n-u}{r^2d^2}+\frac{n-u}{d}\ell_s-\frac b{rd^2},$$ which simplifies to <a href="#ret:kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ret:kernel">[ret:kernel]</a>. In the magnetic curl the corresponding expression is its cross product with $n$: indeed $$\nabla_x^{\rm total}\times(u\ell)
 =\left(-\frac{n-u}{r^2d^2}-\frac n d \ell_s\right)\times u
                  -\frac{n\times b}{rd^2}
 =n\times\mathscr K.$$

*Boundary terms and the cone tip.* The label domain has indicator $\mathbf1_{\{t>|x-y_0|\}}$. Its time derivative is $\delta(t-|x-y_0|)$ and its spatial gradient is $-n_0\delta(t-|x-y_0|)$. The resulting electric factor is $(n_0-u_0)/(td_0)$ and the magnetic one is its cross product with $n_0$. Integration over the initial sphere gives <a href="#ret:initial-boundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ret:initial-boundary">[ret:initial-boundary]</a>. The initial velocities are bounded on $\operatorname{supp}f_0$, so $d_0$ has a fixed positive lower bound. Sphere area and compact initial velocity support consequently give $|E^{\rm bd}|+|B^{\rm bd}|\leq C_{f_0}t$, uniformly in $x$.

The differentiations at the cone tip can be justified by inserting a smooth cutoff that vanishes for $r<\gamma$ and equals one for $r>2\gamma$. On the supported labels of the fixed compact time interval, $d$ is bounded below and $u,b$ are bounded. Derivatives of this cutoff are at most $C/\gamma$. By <a href="#ret:phase-jacobian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ret:phase-jacobian">[ret:phase-jacobian]</a>, the omitted potential and cutoff errors are bounded by integrals of $Cf/r$ over $r<2\gamma$, multiplied by at most $C/\gamma$ for the latter. They are $O(\gamma^2)$ and $O(\gamma)$, respectively. The omitted kernel integrals are $O(\gamma)$ because their worst radial singularity after this change of variables is $r^{-2}$. These bounds are locally uniform for $t>0$. The already established initial traces handle $t=0$. Thus no term supported at the cone tip remains.

Finally, Kirchhoff’s formula for a homogeneous wave $W$ with data $(W_0,W_1)$ bounds it by $$\|W(t)\|_\infty
 \leq\|W_0\|_\infty+t\|\nabla W_0\|_\infty+t\|W_1\|_\infty.$$ The assumed bounded initial derivatives give <a href="#ret:data-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ret:data-bound">[ret:data-bound]</a>. The homogeneous waves here need not together be a vacuum Maxwell solution; the representation requires only their displayed wave data. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Receiver geometry and pointwise force bounds

<a id="ret:force-kernels"></a>
Let $(X(t),V_X(t))$ be a supported receiver characteristic and write $$\begin{gathered}
 a=u(V_X),\qquad q_X=q(V_X),\qquad K=V_X',\\
 D=1-n\cdot a,\qquad e=1-a\cdot u,\qquad Q=D\mathrm{Id}+n\otimes a.
 \end{gathered}$$ Since $h+a\times(n\times h)=Qh$, Proposition <a href="#ret:representation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ret:representation">6</a> and <a href="#ret:phase-jacobian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ret:phase-jacobian">[ret:phase-jacobian]</a> give <a id="ret:force"></a>$$\label{ret:force}
 K=K^{\rm data}+\frac1{4\pi}\int_{|X(t)-y|<t}\int_{\mathbb R^3}
                (T_{\rm ker}+S_{\rm ker})\,dv\,dy,$$ where $|K^{\rm data}|\leq C_{T_0,f_0,E_0,B_0}$ and $$\label{ret:force-kernels}
 T_{\rm ker}=\frac{fQ(n-u)}{r^2q^2d^2},\qquad
 S_{\rm ker}=\frac{fQ\bigl((n-u)(n\cdot b)-db\bigr)}{rd^2}.$$ The density and source quantities in these integrals are evaluated at $(t-r,y,v)$, not at the receiver.

Figure <a href="#fig:retarded-geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:retarded-geometry">2</a> shows the source and receiver geometry.

<figure id="fig:retarded-geometry">

<figcaption>Retarded geometry along a receiver characteristic. The source event $(s,Y(s))$ lies on the backward light cone of $(t,X(t))$, with $r = t − s = |X(t)−Y(s)| > 0$. The spatial unit vector $n$ points from $Y(s)$ to $X(t)$. Panel (b) shows the source velocity $u(V(s)) = Y′(s)$ and the receiver velocity $a(t) = X′(t)$ at these respective events. The curves and vector directions are schematic.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="stmt-ret-direct-bounds"></a>
<div id="ret:direct-bounds" class="lemma">

<a id="ret:transport-bounds"></a>
<a id="ret:acceleration-bounds"></a>
<a id="ret:source-bounds"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Pointwise force bounds). *Let $\theta,\phi>0$ satisfy $\theta^2\asymp d$ and $\phi^2\asymp D$, with fixed comparison constants. The transport kernel splits into two terms, according to the normal and tangential parts of $n-u$, bounded by $$\label{ret:transport-bounds}
 T_r=\frac{Cf}{r^2q^2\theta^2},\qquad
 T_\perp=\frac{Cf\phi}{r^2q^2\theta^3}.$$ With $G$ as in Lemma <a href="#ret:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ret:energy">4</a>, the source acceleration satisfies $$\label{ret:acceleration-bounds}
 |b|\leq\frac Cq(G+\theta^2|B|),\qquad
 |n\cdot b|\leq\frac{C\theta}q(G+\theta^2|B|),\qquad
 |q'|\leq C(G+\theta|B|).$$ Consequently $$\label{ret:source-bounds}
 |S_{\rm ker}|\leq S_g+S_b,\qquad
 S_g=\frac{Cf\phi}{rq\theta^2}G,\qquad
 S_b=\frac{Cf\phi}{rq}|B|.$$ If $V_X\ne0$ and $P_X$ is orthogonal projection onto $V_X^\perp$, then $|P_XS_{\rm ker}|\leq C\phi(S_g+S_b)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ret-direct-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-ret-direct-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* The exact identities $$2d=|n-u|^2+q^{-2},\qquad
 2D=|n-a|^2+q_X^{-2}$$ give $|n-u|\leq C\theta$ and $|n-a|\leq C\phi$. Also $Qn=n$, whereas for $h\perp n$, $$|Qh|\leq D|h|+|a\cdot h|\leq C\phi|h|.$$ The normal component of $n-u$ is $dn$ and its tangential component has norm at most $C\theta$. This proves <a href="#ret:transport-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ret:transport-bounds">[ret:transport-bounds]</a>.

Put $u=(1-d)n+u_\perp$ and $H=E+n\times B$. The force at the source is $$F_{\rm src}=E+u\times B=H+(-dn+u_\perp)\times B.$$ Its tangential and normal components satisfy $$|(F_{\rm src})_\perp|\leq C(G+\theta^2|B|),\qquad
 |n\cdot F_{\rm src}|\leq C(G+\theta|B|).$$ Indeed the tangential part of $u_\perp\times B$ involves only $n\cdot B$, while its normal part is bounded by $C\theta|B|$. For $L_u=\mathrm{Id}-u\otimes u$ we have $$|L_un|\leq C\theta,\qquad
 |n\cdot L_un|=2d-d^2\leq C\theta^2,
 \qquad |n\cdot L_uh|\leq C\theta|h|\quad(h\perp n).$$ Combining these component bounds with $b=q^{-1}L_uF_{\rm src}$ proves the first two inequalities in <a href="#ret:acceleration-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ret:acceleration-bounds">[ret:acceleration-bounds]</a>. The last follows from $q'=u\cdot E=u\cdot H-u\cdot(n\times B)$.

The numerator $N=(n-u)(n\cdot b)-db$ is tangent to $n$, since $n\cdot N=d(n\cdot b)-d(n\cdot b)=0$. This cone tangency is also used in (Pallard 2015, Lemma 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[Pallard2015Refined](#ref-Pallard2015Refined)]. The preceding estimates give $$|N|\leq\frac{C\theta^2}{q}(G+\theta^2|B|).$$ The tangential bound for $Q$ now proves <a href="#ret:source-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ret:source-bounds">[ret:source-bounds]</a>. Finally $P_Xa=0$, so $|P_Xn|=|P_X(n-a)|\leq C\phi$. For $h\perp n$, $$|P_XQh|\leq D|h|+|P_Xn|\,|a\cdot h|
               \leq C\phi^2|h|.$$ Applying this to $N$ gives the additional projected factor. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-ret-receiver-time"></a>
<div id="ret:receiver-time" class="lemma">

<a id="ret:receiver-jacobians"></a>
**Lemma 8** (Source time along a receiver). *Along a retarded source label satisfying $t=s+|X(t)-Y(s,z)|$ away from collisions, $$\label{ret:receiver-jacobians}
 \frac{dt}{ds}=\frac dD,\qquad
 ds\,f_0(z)\,dz=D\,dt\,f(t-r,y,v)\,dy\,dv.$$ For $f_0(z)dz$-almost every label there is no same-time collision $Y(t,z)=X(t)$ anywhere in the compact time interval. Such a label has one continuously differentiable retarded branch after its initial-cone entry, when that entry occurs, and $r$ is bounded below on each closed part of this branch in the compact interval.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ret-receiver-time"><summary>Proof of stmt-ret-receiver-time</summary>

*Proof.* Differentiate $t-s-|X(t)-Y(s,z)|=0$ to obtain $D\,dt=d\,ds$. Both factors are positive. Combining this with <a href="#ret:phase-jacobian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ret:phase-jacobian">[ret:phase-jacobian]</a> gives the measure identity in <a href="#ret:receiver-jacobians" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ret:receiver-jacobians">[ret:receiver-jacobians]</a>.

To exclude same-time collisions, let $R_v$ bound the supported momenta on $[0,t_*]$. Use a time grid of mesh at most $\zeta$ with $O(1+t_*/\zeta)$ points. If a collision occurs, at a nearest grid time the two positions are at distance at most $2\zeta$, since both spatial speeds are at most one. At each grid time the mass of labels satisfying this distance bound is at most $C\|f_0\|_\infty R_v^3\zeta^3$, by phase-volume preservation. The union has mass $O(\zeta^2)$ and tends to zero.

For the remaining labels, strict timelikeness gives uniqueness and continuous differentiability of the retarded root. The entry time is unique because $t-|X(t)-Y(0,z)|$ is strictly increasing. At entry $r=t>0$, since an entry at zero would be an excluded initial collision. On any closed part of the branch, continuity and the absence of collisions give a positive minimum of $r$. This assertion is labelwise; no uniform lower bound over all labels is used. ◻

</details>

<a id="occ:section"></a>
### Occupation of retarded bins

We estimate how much particle mass a receiver can meet in a prescribed momentum, angle, and distance range. Two changes of variables give crude bounds. The signed increment assumption then limits how long a source can remain close to the receiver. A time-averaged energy budget will make the result summable over the small angular sector.

The residence-time and relative-velocity viewpoint is inspired by Pfaffelmoser’s Vlasov–Poisson argument (Pfaffelmoser 1992) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[Pfaffelmoser1992](#ref-Pfaffelmoser1992)]. This is methodological inspiration, not a theorem input for the Vlasov–Maxwell system: the retarded geometry and source-acceleration terms require the estimates proved here.

Throughout this section assume <a href="#eq:bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bootstrap">[eq:bootstrap]</a> up to a time $\tau\leq t_*$. Fix a supported receiver $X$, a dyad $w$, and a closed interval $J\subset[0,\tau]$ of length at most $I$, where <a id="occ:interval"></a>$$\label{occ:interval}
 0<I\leq(w/P)^2.$$ We integrate over any measurable subset of $\{t\in J:w/8\leq q_X(t)\leq8w\}$. The receiver need not remain in this momentum range at other times of $J$. All constants below may depend on the fixed horizon, initial energy, $\|f_0\|_\infty$, and the fixed partition parameters, but not on $M,A,P$, $\tau,J$, or the measurable subset. In particular, they are uniform over supported receivers. We use the already established bounds $$0\leq f\leq\|f_0\|_\infty,
 \qquad \sup_{0\leq s\leq\tau}
  \int_{\mathbb R^3\times\mathbb R^3}q(v)f(s,y,v)\,dy\,dv\leq C.$$

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The momentum and angular ranges

Let $H_0=\max(1,T_0)$. At a source point on the receiver’s backward cone write $$r=t-s>0,\qquad y=X(t)-rn,\qquad n\in S^2,$$ and retain the notation $$u=v/q(v),\quad a=a(t),\quad
 d=1-n\cdot u,\quad D=1-n\cdot a,\quad e=1-a\cdot u.$$ Choose smooth nonnegative dyadic partitions for $r/H_0,\sqrt{d/2},\sqrt{D/2},\sqrt{e/2}$, with representatives $h,\theta,\phi,\sigma\in\{1,2^{-1},2^{-2},\ldots\}$. Each factor is supported where its argument lies between one half and twice its representative. The partitions sum to one, including near the upper endpoint $1$, have bounded overlap, and have uniformly bounded derivatives with respect to the logarithm of each variable. For example, normalized dilates of a fixed bump supported in $(1/2,2)$ and positive on $[3/4,3/2]$ give such partitions. Use the analogous partition of $q\geq1$ with representatives $p_0=1,2,4,\ldots$, but write $p=64p_0$. On its support, $$p/128\leq q\leq p/32,\qquad 64\leq p\leq P.$$ The last inequality follows from the assumed bound on all supported momenta. We use closed support ranges for upper estimates; this preserves bounded overlap.

<a id="occ:angle-identities"></a>
These are angular scales with the inverse-momentum deficits included, not literal geometric angles. The exact identities $$\label{occ:angle-identities}
 \begin{split}
 2d&=|n-u|^2+q^{-2},\\
 2D&=|n-a|^2+q_X^{-2},\\
 2e&=|a-u|^2+q^{-2}+q_X^{-2}
 \end{split}$$ imply, on a nonempty bin, <a id="occ:angle-ranges"></a>$$\label{occ:angle-ranges}
 \theta\geq1/p,\qquad \phi\geq c/w,\qquad
 \sigma\geq c/m,\qquad m=\min(p,w).$$ All angles have at most $C\log(2+P)$ nonempty dyadic ranges. For the relations between them, apply the triangle and reverse triangle inequalities to $$(u-n,q^{-1},0),\qquad(n-a,0,q_X^{-1})\in\mathbb R^5.$$ Their norms are $\sqrt{2d},\sqrt{2D}$, and the norm of their sum is $\sqrt{2e}$. Consequently $\sigma\leq C(\theta+\phi)$, and $\sigma$ is comparable to the larger of $\theta,\phi$ when those two angles are sufficiently separated.

Fix a sufficiently small numerical $\kappa>0$. The *small sector* consists of representatives $\theta<\kappa\phi$; here $\sigma\asymp\phi$, and we omit the $\sigma$ partition entirely. All small-sector estimates below remain valid on the enlarged range $\theta\leq16\kappa\phi$. The *remaining sector* has $\theta\geq\kappa\phi$ and retains the $\sigma$ partition. In particular it includes $\theta\gg\phi$. For the two sectors define <a id="occ:sector-parameters"></a>$$\label{occ:sector-parameters}
 \begin{array}{c|c|c|c}
  &\nu&k_1&\omega\\ \hline
 \text{small}&\theta&1&\phi\\
 \text{remaining}&\min(\theta,\sigma)&1+\phi/\sigma&\sigma .
 \end{array}$$ The parameter $\omega$ will be the velocity tolerance in the occupation argument.

<a id="occ:density"></a>
Let $N(t,s,n)$ be the integral of $f(s,X(t)-rn,v)$ over the momentum restrictions of one bin, set to zero when the remaining restrictions fail. Then $$\label{occ:density}
 N\leq C p^3\nu^2.$$ Indeed the momentum shell has volume at most $Cp^3$. If $\theta$ is small, <a href="#occ:angle-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:angle-identities">[occ:angle-identities]</a> restricts its velocity direction to a spherical cap of radius $C\theta$ about $n$. If $\sigma$ is small, both velocity magnitudes are close to one and their directions differ by at most $C\sigma$, giving a cap about the receiver direction. Use whichever cap is smaller. When the controlling parameter is bounded below, the shell bound alone proves the same estimate with a larger constant. The same argument shows that the allowed $n$ have spherical area at most $C\phi^2$.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Two changes of variables

<a id="occ:measure"></a>
Define the occupation measure of the bin by $$\label{occ:measure}
 \mathsf M=\int dt\,ds\,dn\ r^2 D N(t,s,n),$$ where $t$ ranges over the specified receiver times and $0\leq s<t$. The constants below absorb $H_0$ in the comparison $r\asymp h$. The factor $D$ makes this cone integral equal to particle mass integrated over source-time hits, as the following changes of variables show.

<a id="stmt-occ-crude-lemma"></a>
<div id="occ:crude-lemma" class="lemma">

<a id="occ:energy"></a>
<a id="occ:time"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Crude occupation bounds). *For every bin and every allowed set of receiver times, $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathsf M&\leq C\frac{\max(I,h)}p,
                       \label{occ:energy}\\
 \mathsf M&\leq C I\frac{\phi^2}{\theta^2}.
                       \label{occ:time}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-occ-crude-lemma"><summary>Proof of stmt-occ-crude-lemma</summary>

*Proof.* The characteristic change of variables below is used in Pallard’s work (Pallard 2005, Lemma 2.1) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[Pallard2005](#ref-Pallard2005)]; see also Luk–Strain (Luk and Strain 2014, Proof of Proposition 5.2) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[LukStrain2014Continuation](#ref-LukStrain2014Continuation)]. Fix $s$. The map $$(t,n)\longmapsto y=X(t)-(t-s)n,\qquad t>s,$$ is injective. Indeed, for fixed $y$, the function $t-s-|X(t)-y|$ is strictly increasing: wherever differentiable its derivative is at least $1-|a(t)|>0$. Its absolute Jacobian is $r^2D$. This follows by taking two tangent vectors to the sphere and the time derivative $a-n$, whose normal component is $-D$. Hence <a href="#occ:measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:measure">[occ:measure]</a> is the integral of $f(s,y,v)\,ds\,dy\,dv$ over the corresponding hits. The possible source times lie in an interval of length at most $C(I+h)$. On the momentum bin $q\geq cp$, so the particle energy bound proves <a href="#occ:energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:energy">[occ:energy]</a>.

<a id="occ:label-measure"></a>
Passing at each fixed $s$ to the initial phase labels $z$, whose measure is preserved by the characteristic flow, gives equivalently $$\label{occ:label-measure}
 \mathsf M=\int f_0(z)\,dz\int_{\text{hits of }z}ds.$$ By Lemma <a href="#ret:receiver-time" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ret:receiver-time">8</a>, labels having a same-time collision have zero $f_0(z)\,dz$ measure. Every remaining label has a single increasing $C^1$ retarded branch, and <a id="occ:retarded-derivative"></a>$$\label{occ:retarded-derivative}
 \frac{dt}{ds}=\frac dD,\qquad \frac{ds}{dt}=\frac Dd.$$ The branch begins when it enters the initial cone, if such an entry occurs. Since its hit receiver times form a subset of $J$, their total length is at most $I$. On the bin, $D/d\leq C\phi^2/\theta^2$. Thus the hit duration of each label is at most $CI\phi^2/\theta^2$. Integrating in <a href="#occ:label-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:label-measure">[occ:label-measure]</a> and using the conserved finite particle mass proves <a href="#occ:time" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:time">[occ:time]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
###### The three changes of variables.

The preceding formulas use different fixed variables. For later reference, their Jacobians are collected here:

<div class="center">

| Fixed     | Change of variables                       | Jacobian          |
|:----------|:------------------------------------------|:------------------|
| $(t,x)$   | Retarded $(y,v)$ to the initial label $z$ | $dz=d\,dy\,dv$    |
| $s$       | $(t,n)\mapsto X(t)-(t-s)n$                | $dy=r^2D\,dt\,dn$ |
| Label $z$ | $t=s+|X(t)-Y(s,z)|$                       | $dt/ds=d/D$       |

</div>

In particular the combined measure identity is $ds\,f_0(z)dz=D\,dt\,f\,dy\,dv$.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Stability on a short cell

<a id="occ:lambda"></a>
Fix large constants $C_0,C_1$, chosen below, and set $$\label{occ:lambda}
 \lambda=\left[
 C_1\frac Pm\left(\frac M\omega+
                         \sqrt{\frac{AL}{\omega}}\right)
                         \right]^{-2}.$$ Call a bin *actual* if $\omega>C_0/m$, and *deficit* otherwise. In an actual bin <a href="#occ:angle-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:angle-identities">[occ:angle-identities]</a> implies $|u(s)-a(t)|\geq c\omega$ at each hit: the two inverse-momentum terms are smaller than a fixed small multiple of $\omega^2$ once $C_0$ is large. In the small sector use additionally $\theta\leq16\kappa\phi$. In a deficit bin, <a id="occ:deficit-lambda"></a>$$\label{occ:deficit-lambda}
 \lambda\leq\frac{C_0^2}{C_1^2P^2}.$$

<a id="occ:stability"></a>
We record explicitly the stability supplied by the bootstrap. Given a fixed constant $C_*$ and a hit, all source times within $C_*\lambda$ of its source time, and all receiver times within $C_*\lambda$ of its receiver time, clipped to $[0,\tau]$, satisfy $$\label{occ:stability}
 |\Delta V|\leq\varepsilon_0 m\omega,
 \qquad |\Delta u|\leq\varepsilon_0\omega,
 \qquad q\asymp q_{\rm hit}.$$ Here $\varepsilon_0>0$ can be prescribed by choosing $C_1$ sufficiently large in terms of $C_*$ and the fixed comparisons, independently of $M,A$. To prove this, choose a dyad $w'$ nearest $q_{\rm hit}$ and stop at the first exit from $[q_{\rm hit}/2,2q_{\rm hit}]$. Before exit the bootstrap applies with $w'\asymp q_{\rm hit}\geq cm$. Its two terms on an interval of length at most $C_*\lambda$ are bounded by $$MP\sqrt{C_*\lambda}\leq
       \frac{\sqrt{C_*}}{C_1}m\omega,
 \qquad
 C A C_*\lambda\frac{P^2L}{m}
       \leq\frac{CC_*}{C_1^2}m\omega.$$ For sufficiently large $C_1$, this excludes the first exit, because $q$ is 1-Lipschitz and $\omega\leq1$. The same stopping argument runs backwards from the hit. The segment between the two momentum vectors has $q\geq cq_{\rm hit}$; integrating $\|D_vu\|\leq1/q$ on that segment then proves the velocity estimate. This proves <a href="#occ:stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:stability">[occ:stability]</a>. We now fix $C_*$ large enough to cover the cell and retarded-time separations used below, and then fix $C_1$ accordingly.

<a id="stmt-occ-cell-lemma"></a>
<div id="occ:cell-lemma" class="lemma">

<a id="occ:actual"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Occupation on stable cells). *In every actual bin, $$\label{occ:actual}
 \mathsf M\leq C\frac{hk_1}{p}
                       \max(1,I/\lambda,h/\lambda).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-occ-cell-lemma"><summary>Proof of stmt-occ-cell-lemma</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose first $h\leq\lambda$, and partition $[0,\tau]$ into intervals of length $\lambda$, with the last interval clipped if necessary. For upper bounds we may enlarge the receiver times to all eligible times in $J$ and use closed bin ranges. The labels with some hit in a closed cell form a measurable set: it is the projection of a compact subset of the product of the compact initial support, the cell, $J$, and $S^2$; the fixed bin has $r\geq ch>0$.

For any label with a hit $(s_0,t_0)$ in the cell, use that hit as a reference. At every same-time point $s$ of the cell, both $u(s,z)$ and $a(s)$ are within a small multiple of $\omega$ of their respective reference velocities: the time separations are at most $\lambda+C_{T_0}h\leq C_*\lambda$. The actual velocity difference therefore has projection at least $c\omega$ in one fixed direction throughout the cell. This direction may depend on the label and its reference hit. At any hit in the cell, $$Y(s,z)-X(s)=\int_s^t(a(t')-n)\,dt',
 \qquad |Y(s,z)-X(s)|\leq Ch(\phi+\omega),$$ using $|a(t)-n|\leq C\phi$ and receiver stability between $s$ and $t$. The projected same-time separation is strictly monotone with derivative of magnitude at least $c\omega$. Its values at hits lie in an interval of length $Ch(\phi+\omega)$, so their total $s$-duration is at most $Ch(1+\phi/\omega)\leq Chk_1$. This holds even if the hit set is disconnected.

All labels that hit the cell have $q\asymp p$ at any one common time in the cell, by stability. Conservation of phase volume and the energy bound give their total label mass at most $C/p$. The source-time range of all hits has length at most $C(I+h)$, so at most $C\max(1,I/\lambda,h/\lambda)$ cells can occur. Formula <a href="#occ:label-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:label-measure">[occ:label-measure]</a> proves <a href="#occ:actual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:actual">[occ:actual]</a>. If $h>\lambda$, instead use <a href="#occ:energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:energy">[occ:energy]</a>: its right-hand side is bounded by that of <a href="#occ:actual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:actual">[occ:actual]</a>, since $k_1\geq1$ and $h\max(I,h)/\lambda\geq\max(I,h)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### One energy budget for all distance and source-angle bins

Choose a fixed enlargement $$J^*=\{s\in[0,\tau]:\operatorname{dist}(s,J)\leq C_{T_0}I\},$$ with its constant large enough for every source cell considered below when $h\leq\lambda\leq I$. In particular $|J^*|\leq CI$. For fixed $s,y$, let $t_{\rm ret}(s,y)\in(s,\tau]$ be the unique solution of $t-s=|X(t)-y|$, if it exists; omit its tip $y=X(s)$. This definition deliberately imposes no restriction that $t_{\rm ret}$ belong to $J$ or to the eligible receiver subset.

<a id="occ:energy-budget"></a>
<a id="occ:energy-budget-sum"></a>
The first indicator below is used to average the mass of hit labels over a stable cell, using the velocity separation that persists there. The second records the receiver angle at a retarded hit and is used when the spatial change of variables estimates hits directly. For each $p,\phi$ define these indicators with fixed sufficiently enlarged comparison constants: $$\begin{split}
 H^{(1)}_{p\phi}(s,y,v)
  &=\mathbf1\{q(v)\asymp p, |u(v)-a(s)|\asymp\phi\},\\
 H^{(2)}_{p\phi}(s,y,v)
  &=\mathbf1\left\{q(v)\asymp p, t_{\rm ret}\text{ exists},
    \sqrt{\frac{1-n_{\rm ret}\cdot a(t_{\rm ret})}{2}}\asymp\phi
                    \right\},\\
 n_{\rm ret}&=\frac{X(t_{\rm ret})-y}{t_{\rm ret}-s}.
 \end{split}$$ The second indicator is zero when its retarded time does not exist. The strictly monotone implicit equation makes its non-tip solution continuous when the root lies in $(s,\tau)$ and continuously extendible to a root at $\tau$. The existence condition is a closed inequality at $\tau$, after excluding the tip; thus the indicators are measurable. Set $$\label{occ:energy-budget}
 \mathcal E_{p\phi}=\frac1I\int_{J^*}\int_{\mathbb R^3\times\mathbb R^3}
 qf\bigl(H^{(1)}_{p\phi}+H^{(2)}_{p\phi}\bigr)\,dy\,dv\,ds.$$ These numbers depend on the receiver, $J,I,p,\phi$, but not on $\theta,h$ or on the eligible measurable subset. Their comparison constants, and hence the bound $$\label{occ:energy-budget-sum}
 \sum_{p,\phi}\mathcal E_{p\phi}\leq C,$$ are independent of $M,A$. Indeed at each $(s,y,v)$ only a bounded number of $p$ and $\phi$ dyads can occur in either family. For the second family there is just one retarded time. Summing the indicators and using the energy bound and $|J^*|\leq CI$ proves <a href="#occ:energy-budget-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:energy-budget-sum">[occ:energy-budget-sum]</a>.

<a id="stmt-occ-refined-proposition"></a>
<div id="occ:refined-proposition" class="proposition">

<a id="occ:refined"></a>
<a id="occ:R-bound"></a>
**Proposition 11** (Baseline occupation with a joint energy budget). *Suppose $h\leq I$, excluding only deficit bins with $h\leq\lambda$. Then $$\label{occ:refined}
 \mathsf M\leq C\frac{hk_1}{p}
             \left(1+\frac I\lambda\mathcal E\right),
 \qquad
 \mathcal E=\begin{cases}
  \mathcal E_{p\phi},&\text{small sector},\\
  1,&\text{remaining sector}.
 \end{cases}$$ Consequently, with $R=1+\sqrt{I/\lambda}\sqrt{\mathcal E}$, $$\label{occ:R-bound}
 \mathsf M\leq C\frac{hk_1}{p}R^2$$ for all $h$, except deficit bins with $h\leq\min(I,\lambda)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-occ-refined-proposition"><summary>Proof of stmt-occ-refined-proposition</summary>

*Proof.* In the remaining sector, <a href="#occ:actual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:actual">[occ:actual]</a> proves the claim for actual bins; if $h>\lambda$, <a href="#occ:energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:energy">[occ:energy]</a> gives $\mathsf M\leq CI/p\leq Ch(I/\lambda)/p$. This also treats the allowed deficit bins.

Consider the small sector. When $h\leq\lambda\leq I$ and the bin is actual, denote the set of hit labels in a cell $C$ by $Z_C$. At every time $s\in C$ all these labels have both $q\asymp p$ and $|u-a(s)|\asymp\phi$, by the same stability argument as in Lemma <a href="#occ:cell-lemma" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="occ:cell-lemma">10</a>. For a full cell, averaging its mass over its whole length therefore gives $$\int_{Z_C}f_0(z)\,dz
 \leq\frac C{p\lambda}\int_C\int qf
                  H^{(1)}_{p\phi}\,dy\,dv\,ds.$$ All such cells lie in $J^*$ and their interiors are disjoint. The at most two cells clipped by the endpoints of $[0,\tau]$ have mass at most $C/p$ by the common-time argument, without averaging. Each label has hit duration at most $Ch$ in its cell. Summing <a href="#occ:label-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:label-measure">[occ:label-measure]</a> proves $\mathsf M\leq Chp^{-1}(1+(I/\lambda)\mathcal E_{p\phi})$. If $\lambda>I$, the source-time range has length at most $CI$ and meets only a bounded number of cells. Their crude mass and duration bounds already give $\mathsf M\leq Ch/p$; no averaging outside $J^*$ is used.

Finally suppose $\lambda<h\leq I$, whether actual or deficit. In the space map of Lemma <a href="#occ:crude-lemma" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="occ:crude-lemma">9</a>, each hit belongs to $H^{(2)}_{p\phi}$ and its source time belongs to $J^*$. Hence $$\mathsf M\leq\frac C p\int_{J^*}\int qf
                    H^{(2)}_{p\phi}\,dy\,dv\,ds
       \leq\frac{CI}p\mathcal E_{p\phi}.$$ Since $h/\lambda>1$, this is bounded by the right-hand side of <a href="#occ:refined" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:refined">[occ:refined]</a>. The estimate $1+(I/\lambda)\mathcal E\leq R^2$ proves <a href="#occ:R-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:R-bound">[occ:R-bound]</a> when $h\leq I$. For $h>I$, <a href="#occ:energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:energy">[occ:energy]</a> gives $\mathsf M\leq Ch/p$, and $k_1R^2\geq1$ completes the proof. The budget <a href="#occ:energy-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:energy-budget">[occ:energy-budget]</a> can be defined for every bin, regardless of whether $h\leq I$; its use in this last case requires only nonnegativity. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:direct"></a>
### Direct bounds for the retarded force

We first estimate the retarded force by absolute values. These estimates control every term except the narrow-angle part of $S_g$ at the scale needed for the signed bootstrap. They also give an absolute bound for the full force, with an additional factor $\sqrt w$.

<a id="dir:time-range"></a>
Retain the decomposition and occupation estimates of the preceding section. Let $J\subset[0,\tau]$ have length at most $I$, where $$\label{dir:time-range}
 0<I\le (w/P)^2,$$ and let $\mathcal T\subset J$ be any measurable set of times at which $w/8\le q_X\le8w$. Every integral below is restricted to $t\in\mathcal T$. Estimates for a nonnegative integrand remain valid if it is multiplied by any measurable weight between zero and one.

<a id="stmt-dir-direct"></a>
<div id="dir:direct" class="proposition">

<a id="dir:baseline"></a>
<a id="dir:absolute"></a>
**Proposition 12** (Integrated force bounds). *Assume the bootstrap <a href="#eq:bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bootstrap">[eq:bootstrap]</a>. The bounded data term, $T_r$, $T_\perp$, $S_b$, and the remaining-sector part of $S_g$ have combined integral at most $$\label{dir:baseline}
 C\bigl[P\sqrt I+(M+\sqrt A)I S(w)\bigr].$$ The integral of the full force satisfies $$\label{dir:absolute}
 \int_{\mathcal T}|K(t)|\,dt
 \le C\sqrt w\bigl[P\sqrt I+(M+\sqrt A)I S(w)\bigr].$$ The constant $C$ is independent of $P,w,I,M,A$.*

</div>

We prove the proposition in several steps. All dyadic sums below have fixed overlap constants. The normalization of the radius by $\max(1,T_0)$ is absorbed into $C$, so that $r\asymp h$.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Field budgets and a single bin

<a id="dir:G-budget"></a>
We require field budgets shared by all the momentum and source-scale bins. Write $\chi_\phi(t,s,n)$ for the indicator of a fixed enlarged support of the receiver-angle bin. The overlap of these supports is bounded independently of all parameters. Define $$\label{dir:G-budget}
 \mathcal G_\phi
 =\frac1I\int_{\mathcal T}dt\int_0^t ds\int_{\mathbb S^2}dn\,
       r^2\chi_\phi(t,s,n)G(s,X(t)-rn,n)^2.$$ The cone-energy estimate <a href="#eq:cone-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cone-energy">[eq:cone-energy]</a> gives $\sum_\phi\mathcal G_\phi\le C$. In particular this budget does not depend on $p,\theta,\sigma,h$.

<a id="dir:F-budget"></a>
<a id="dir:magnetic-budget"></a>
For the magnetic field, let $J^*$ be the common enlargement of $J$ from the preceding section, chosen to contain all source times when $h\le I$; thus $|J^*|\le CI$. At a point $(s,y)$ with a retarded receiver time $t_{\rm ret}(s,y)\in(s,\tau]$, put $$n_{\rm ret}(s,y)
 =\frac{X(t_{\rm ret}(s,y))-y}{t_{\rm ret}(s,y)-s}.$$ Set the indicator in the following definition to zero when the retarded time does not exist: $$\label{dir:F-budget}
 \mathcal F_\phi
 =\frac1I\int_{J^*}ds\int_{\mathbb R^3}dy\,|B(s,y)|^2
    \chi_\phi\bigl(t_{\rm ret}(s,y),s,n_{\rm ret}(s,y)\bigr).$$ The retarded time is unique and is a measurable function on its domain. Bounded overlap and conserved spatial energy <a href="#eq:energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:energy">[eq:energy]</a> imply $\sum_\phi\mathcal F_\phi\le C$. Again the budget is independent of $p,\theta,\sigma,h$. The Jacobian $r^2D$ of the map $(t,n)\mapsto X(t)-(t-s)n$, together with $D\asymp\phi^2$, gives $$\label{dir:magnetic-budget}
 \int_{\mathrm{bin}} r^2\phi^2|B|^2\,dt\,ds\,dn
 \le C(I+h)\mathcal F,
 \qquad
 \mathcal F=
 \begin{cases}
  \mathcal F_\phi,&h\le I,\\
  1,&h>I.
 \end{cases}$$ For $h>I$ the source times lie in an interval of length $O(I+h)$, so conserved spatial energy proves the second case directly. The budgets in <a href="#dir:G-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:G-budget">[dir:G-budget]</a>–<a href="#dir:F-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:F-budget">[dir:F-budget]</a> may always be enlarged by fixed factors when supports are enlarged.

<a id="dir:density-integrals"></a>
For a fixed bin, let $N=\int_{\mathrm{bin}}f\,dv$ and denote its hit measure by $\mathsf M$. By <a href="#occ:density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:density">[occ:density]</a>, the angular cap bound, and the definition of $\mathsf M$, $$\label{dir:density-integrals}
 \begin{split}
 N&\le Cp^3\nu^2,\qquad
 \int_{\mathrm{bin}}dt\,ds\,dn\le CIh\phi^2,\\
 \int N\,dt\,ds\,dn&\le \frac{C\mathsf M}{h^2\phi^2},\\
 \int N^2\,dt\,ds\,dn
 &\le C\min\left(Ih\phi^2p^6\nu^4,
              \frac{p^3\nu^2\mathsf M}{h^2\phi^2}\right).
 \end{split}$$ Here and below $N$ vanishes outside the indicated time and spatial restrictions. In the small sector $\nu=\theta$ and the $\sigma$ partition is omitted.

<a id="dir:near-far"></a>
The force majorants on this bin are, up to fixed constants, $$T_r=\frac{f}{r^2p^2\theta^2},\qquad
 T_\perp=\frac{f\phi}{r^2p^2\theta^3},\qquad
 S_g=\frac{f\phi G}{rp\theta^2},\qquad
 S_b=\frac{f\phi|B|}{rp}.$$ Using $dy=r^2ds\,dn$ yields the following bounds for their integrals. Both columns apply on every bin: the near bound uses the pointwise density estimate, while the far bound also uses the occupation measure. Their names indicate which estimate is useful at smaller or larger radii. $$\label{dir:near-far}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.5}
\begin{array}{c|c|c}
 &\text{near bound}&\text{far bound}\\ \hline
 T_r&Ihp(\nu/\theta)^2\phi^2&-\\
 T_\perp&Ihp\theta^{-3}\nu^2\phi^3
         &\mathsf M/(h^2\phi p^2\theta^3)\\
 S_g&I\sqrt h\,p^2(\nu/\theta)^2\phi^2\sqrt{\mathcal G_\phi}
     &\sqrt I\sqrt{p\mathsf M}\,\nu(\theta^2h)^{-1}
        \sqrt{\mathcal G_\phi}\\
 S_b&\sqrt{I(I+h)}\sqrt h\,p^2\nu^2\phi\sqrt{\mathcal F}
     &\sqrt{I+h}\sqrt{p\mathsf M}\,\nu(\phi h)^{-1}
        \sqrt{\mathcal F}.
\end{array}$$ The first two rows follow from <a href="#dir:density-integrals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:density-integrals">[dir:density-integrals]</a>. For $S_g$, apply Cauchy–Schwarz to $rGN$: the first squared factor is bounded by $I\mathcal G_\phi$ and the second by either bound for $\int N^2$ in <a href="#dir:density-integrals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:density-integrals">[dir:density-integrals]</a>. The prefactor is $\phi/(p\theta^2)$. For $S_b$, use $r\phi|B|$ as the first factor, apply <a href="#dir:magnetic-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:magnetic-budget">[dir:magnetic-budget]</a>, and use the prefactor $1/p$. This proves every entry of the table.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Summing the radius

<a id="dir:occupation"></a>
Recall the occupation factor $$R=1+\sqrt{I/\lambda}\sqrt{\mathcal E},
 \qquad
 \mathcal E=
 \begin{cases}
  1,&\theta\ge\kappa\phi,\\
  \mathcal E_{p\phi},&\theta<\kappa\phi,
 \end{cases}
 \qquad \sum_{p,\phi}\mathcal E_{p\phi}\le C.$$ It is independent of $h$, and also of $\theta$ in the small sector. The refined occupation estimate <a href="#occ:refined" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:refined">[occ:refined]</a> gives $$\label{dir:occupation}
 \mathsf M\le C h k_1R^2/p$$ when $h\le I$, except in deficit bins with $h\le\lambda$. For $h>I$ the same inequality follows from <a href="#occ:energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:energy">[occ:energy]</a>, since $R,k_1\ge1$.

<a id="dir:balance"></a>
<a id="dir:balanced"></a>
For positive $a,b,\alpha,\beta$, the elementary dyadic estimate $$\label{dir:balance}
 \sum_{h\in2^{\mathbb Z}}
       \min(ah^\alpha,bh^{-\beta})
 \le C_{\alpha,\beta}
       a^{\beta/(\alpha+\beta)}b^{\alpha/(\alpha+\beta)}$$ follows by splitting at $h=(b/a)^{1/(\alpha+\beta)}$ and summing geometric series on both sides. It remains an upper bound when the allowed radii form only a subset of the dyadic scales. Substituting <a href="#dir:occupation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:occupation">[dir:occupation]</a> into <a href="#dir:near-far" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:near-far">[dir:near-far]</a> and applying <a href="#dir:balance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:balance">[dir:balance]</a> gives $$\label{dir:balanced}
 \begin{aligned}
 T_\perp:&\quad
 C\sqrt I\,R p^{-1}\theta^{-3}\nu\phi\sqrt{k_1},\\
 S_g:&\quad
 C I^{3/4}R^{1/2}p\phi\nu^{3/2}\theta^{-2}
       k_1^{1/4}\sqrt{\mathcal G_\phi},\\
 S_b\ (h\le I):&\quad
 C I^{3/4}R^{1/2}p\nu^{3/2}k_1^{1/4}
       \sqrt{\mathcal F_\phi}.
 \end{aligned}$$ The first line balances $h$ with $h^{-1}$; the other two balance $h^{1/2}$ with $h^{-1/2}$. The exceptional deficit radii are excluded from these balances and are treated below.

<a id="dir:large-radius"></a>
For $S_b$ with $h>I$, use instead the time-change bound <a href="#occ:time" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:time">[occ:time]</a>, namely $\mathsf M\le CI\phi^2/\theta^2$. The corresponding minimum in <a href="#dir:near-far" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:near-far">[dir:near-far]</a> is $$C\sqrt I\min\left(hp^2\nu^2\phi,
                     h^{-1/2}\sqrt p\,\nu/\theta\right).$$ Its radius sum is at most $$\label{dir:large-radius}
 C\sqrt I\,p\nu^{4/3}\theta^{-2/3}\phi^{1/3}.$$ If one of $\theta,\phi$ is much larger than the other, $\sigma$ has bounded multiplicity or is absent, and the angular factor is at most $\theta^{2/3}\phi^{1/3}$. If $\theta\asymp\phi$, it is at most $C\theta(\sigma/\theta)^{4/3}$, with $\sigma\lesssim\theta$. The angular sums are geometric in both cases. Summing $p$ proves that all large-radius $S_b$ terms cost at most $CP\sqrt I$.

<a id="dir:deficit"></a>
In an exceptional deficit bin, $h\le\min(I,\lambda)$ and $\lambda\le CP^{-2}$ by <a href="#occ:lambda" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="occ:lambda">[occ:lambda]</a>. Summing the near bounds over these radii gives, per remaining bin, $$\label{dir:deficit}
 \begin{aligned}
 S_g+S_b&\le CI\frac{p^2}{Pm^2},\\
 T_\perp&\le
 \begin{cases}
  CIp/P^2,&\theta\ge\kappa\phi,\\
  CIp^2/P^2,&\theta<\kappa\phi.
 \end{cases}
 \end{aligned}$$ Indeed, in the remaining sector $\phi\lesssim\theta$ and $\nu\lesssim m^{-1}$, whereas in the small sector $\nu=\theta$, $\phi\lesssim m^{-1}$, and $\theta^{-1}\le p$. The individual field budgets are bounded by their total budgets. There are at most $CL^4$ remaining bins. Since $$\frac{p^2}{Pm^2}\le \frac1P+\frac{P}{w^2},\qquad
 \frac{p^2}{P^2}\le1,$$ the ratio of their total cost to $I S(w)$ is at most $$CL^3\left(\frac{w}{P^3}+\frac1{Pw}+\frac{w}{P^2}\right)
 \le \frac{CL^3}{P}.$$ Thus these terms are absorbed uniformly into $CI S(w)$.

<a id="dir:radial-transport"></a>
The radius sum for $T_r$ is also immediate from its near bound. When $\theta\asymp\phi$, summing $(\nu/\theta)^2$ over $\sigma$ is a geometric sum; otherwise $\sigma$ has bounded multiplicity or is omitted. Summing $h$, the $O(L)$ values of $\theta$, then $\phi^2$ and $p$, gives $$\label{dir:radial-transport}
 \int T_r\le CIPL\le CI S(w).$$

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Angular and momentum sums

<a id="dir:momentum-sums"></a>
The radius sums are complete, including the deficit exception and the magnetic terms with $h>I$. It remains to sum the angular and momentum factors in <a href="#dir:balanced" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:balanced">[dir:balanced]</a>. We keep the small-sector $S_g$ contribution separate because it yields the absolute-force bound with its additional factor $\sqrt w$. The following elementary momentum sums will be used repeatedly: $$\label{dir:momentum-sums}
 \begin{aligned}
 \sum_p p&\le CP,&
 \sum_p\frac p m&\le C(L+P/w),&
 \sum_p\frac{\sqrt p}{m}&\le C(1+\sqrt P/w),\\
 \sum_p p\sqrt{P/m}&\le CP\sqrt{P/w},&&
 \sum_p p^{3/2}\sqrt{P/m}&\le CP^2/\sqrt w.
 \end{aligned}$$ To verify them, split at $p=w$. For $p\le w$ one has $m=p$; for $p>w$ one has $m=w$. Each resulting positive-power sum is geometric, while a constant summand costs at most $CL$.

<a id="dir:R-expansion"></a>
Write $\omega=\sigma$ in the remaining sector and $\omega=\phi$ in the small sector. The definition of $\lambda$ gives the exact parameter dependence $$\label{dir:R-expansion}
 \begin{split}
 R-1
 &\le C\sqrt I\frac Pm
       \left(\frac M\omega+
                     \frac{\sqrt{AL}}{\sqrt\omega}\right)
       \sqrt{\mathcal E},\\
 R^{1/2}
 &\le C\left[1+I^{1/4}\sqrt{P/m}
       \left(\frac{\sqrt M}{\sqrt\omega}+
                     \frac{(AL)^{1/4}}{\omega^{1/4}}\right)
       \right].
 \end{split}$$ For the second line we used $\mathcal E\le C$; the first line retains the joint small-sector budget when it is needed.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
###### The small-sector transport term.

<a id="dir:small-budget"></a>
Here $\nu=\theta$ and $k_1\asymp1$. Summing the first line of <a href="#dir:balanced" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:balanced">[dir:balanced]</a> over $\theta\ge1/p$ gives $$\label{dir:small-budget}
 C\sqrt I\,R p\phi.$$ The term with $R$ replaced by one sums to $CP\sqrt I$. The $M$ term in <a href="#dir:R-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:R-expansion">[dir:R-expansion]</a> costs $$CIM P\sum_{p,\phi}\frac p m\sqrt{\mathcal E_{p\phi}}
 \le CIM P\bigl(L+\sqrt L\,P/w\bigr).$$ Indeed, Cauchy–Schwarz and the joint budget reduce the sum to the square root of $\sum_{p,\phi}(p/m)^2$, which is at most $C[L^2+L(P/w)^2]$ before taking the square root. For the $\sqrt{AL}$ term the weight has an additional factor $\sqrt\phi$; its squared sum is at most $C[L+(P/w)^2]$. That contribution is therefore at most $$CI\sqrt{AL}\,P(\sqrt L+P/w)
 =CI\sqrt A\,P(L+\sqrt L\,P/w).$$ Since $1\le w\le P$ and $L\ge1$, both parameter terms are bounded by $C(M+\sqrt A)I S(w)$.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
###### The remaining-sector transport term.

Split into $\theta>16\phi$, where $\sigma\asymp\theta$, and $\theta\asymp\phi$, where $\sigma\lesssim\theta$. Fixed comparison constants may depend on $\kappa$. The angular factors in the plain term of the first line of <a href="#dir:balanced" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:balanced">[dir:balanced]</a> are respectively $$p^{-1}\theta^{-2}\phi,
 \qquad p^{-1}\theta^{-1}(\sigma/\theta)^{1/2}.$$ Their sums are bounded uniformly for each $p$: sum $\phi\lesssim
\theta$ in the first expression, and $\sigma\lesssim\theta$ in the second, then use $\theta\ge1/p$. Thus the plain contribution is at most $CL\sqrt I\le CP\sqrt I$.

After extracting $CIM P(p/m)$, the $M$ terms have angular factors $$(p\theta)^{-2}\frac\phi\theta,
 \qquad (p\theta)^{-2}(\theta/\sigma)^{1/2}.$$ The second $\sigma$ sum is at most $C\sqrt{m\theta}\le
C\sqrt{p\theta}$ because $\sigma\gtrsim1/m$; hence both remaining angular sums are bounded. After extracting $CI\sqrt{AL}\,P\sqrt p/m$, the corresponding factors are $$(p\theta)^{-3/2}\frac\phi\theta,
 \qquad (p\theta)^{-3/2}.$$ The number of allowed $\sigma$ values in the second case is at most $C[1+\log_+(m\theta)]\le C\sqrt{p\theta}$ on a nonempty bin, so these angular sums are bounded as well. Here $\log_+s=\max(0,\log s)$. By <a href="#dir:momentum-sums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:momentum-sums">[dir:momentum-sums]</a>, the two parameter contributions are at most $$CI M P(L+P/w),\qquad
 CI\sqrt{AL}\,P(1+\sqrt P/w).$$ Both are bounded by $C(M+\sqrt A)I S(w)$.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
###### The remaining-sector good-field term.

<a id="dir:field-parameters"></a>
Apart from $CI^{3/4}pR^{1/2}\sqrt{\mathcal G_\phi}$, the angular factors for $S_g$ in the same two cases are $$\sqrt\theta\,\frac\phi\theta,
 \qquad \sqrt\theta(\sigma/\theta)^{5/4}.$$ At fixed $\phi$ their $\theta,\sigma$ sums are at most $C\sqrt\phi$. Consequently the plain term is at most $CI^{3/4}P\le CP\sqrt I$, by Cauchy–Schwarz with $\sum_\phi\mathcal G_\phi\le C$ and $\sum_\phi\phi\le C$. Dividing the displayed factors by $\sqrt\sigma$ leaves, up to constants, $$\phi/\theta,
 \qquad (\sigma/\theta)^{3/4},$$ whose sums at fixed $\phi$ are bounded. Division by $\sigma^{1/4}$ gives no larger bound, since $\sigma\le1$. There are at most $CL$ receiver-angle bins, so $\sum_\phi\sqrt{\mathcal G_\phi}\le C\sqrt L$. The parameter terms in <a href="#dir:R-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:R-expansion">[dir:R-expansion]</a> therefore cost at most $$\label{dir:field-parameters}
 CI\bigl(\sqrt M+(AL)^{1/4}\bigr)
       P\sqrt{P/w}\sqrt L
 \le C(M+\sqrt A)I S(w).$$ The last inequality follows from $w\le P$, $L\ge1$, and $M,A\ge1$.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
###### The magnetic remainder for $h\le I$.

For $S_b$ the base angular factors in the two remaining-sector subcases and in the small sector are respectively $$\theta^{3/2},\qquad
 \theta^{3/2}(\sigma/\theta)^{5/4},\qquad
 \theta^{3/2}.$$ Their sums at fixed $\phi$ are bounded. This remains true after division by $\sqrt\sigma$ in the remaining-sector subcases, giving $\theta$ and $\theta(\sigma/\theta)^{3/4}$, or by $\sqrt\phi$ in the small sector, where the $\theta$ sum is at most $C\phi$. The quarter-power divisions give no larger bound. Using $\sum_\phi\sqrt{\mathcal F_\phi}\le C\sqrt L$ thus gives the parameter estimate <a href="#dir:field-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:field-parameters">[dir:field-parameters]</a> again. The plain term is at most $CI^{3/4}P\sqrt L$. Uniformly in $I$, <a id="dir:plain-magnetic"></a>$$\label{dir:plain-magnetic}
 I^{3/4}\sqrt L\le \sqrt I+IL,$$ as follows from $x\le1+x^2$ with $x=I^{1/4}\sqrt L$. Since $PL\le S(w)$, this completes the bound for $S_b$.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
###### The small-sector good-field term.

This term is needed only for the absolute estimate <a href="#dir:absolute" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:absolute">[dir:absolute]</a>. Its base angular factor is $\phi/\sqrt\theta$, whose $\theta$ sum is at most $C\sqrt p\,\phi$. The plain contribution is therefore bounded by $CI^{3/4}P^{3/2}$. The time restriction <a href="#dir:time-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:time-range">[dir:time-range]</a> gives $$I^{3/4}P^{3/2}\le \sqrt w\,P\sqrt I.$$ For the two parameter terms the $\phi$ factors after this sum are $\phi^{1/2}$ and $\phi^{3/4}$. Their sums against $\sqrt{\mathcal G_\phi}$ are bounded by Cauchy–Schwarz and geometric summation. The last momentum sum in <a href="#dir:momentum-sums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:momentum-sums">[dir:momentum-sums]</a> gives a total of at most $$CI\bigl(\sqrt M+(AL)^{1/4}\bigr)P^2/\sqrt w
 \le C\sqrt w(M+\sqrt A)I S(w).$$ The exceptional bins were already included in <a href="#dir:deficit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:deficit">[dir:deficit]</a>.

Combining these bounds with <a href="#dir:large-radius" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:large-radius">[dir:large-radius]</a>, <a href="#dir:deficit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:deficit">[dir:deficit]</a>, and <a href="#dir:radial-transport" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:radial-transport">[dir:radial-transport]</a> proves <a href="#dir:baseline" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:baseline">[dir:baseline]</a>. The data force contributes at most $CI$, which is bounded by $CP\sqrt I$ since $I\le1$. Finally the force representation is bounded by these nonnegative majorants; adding the small-sector $S_g$ estimate and using $w\ge1$ proves <a href="#dir:absolute" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:absolute">[dir:absolute]</a>. This proves Proposition <a href="#dir:direct" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dir:direct">12</a>.

<a id="can:section"></a>
### Signed cancellation along the source trajectories

The direct force estimates lose an angular factor when the source velocity is much closer to the cone direction than the receiver velocity is. In this region we integrate along the source trajectory before taking absolute values. The transport part of the force must be included: it cancels a term produced by differentiating the cone geometry.

<a id="can:measure"></a>
<a id="can:geometry"></a>
Keep a supported receiver $X(t)$, with velocity $a=X'$, energy $q_X$ and momentum derivative $K=V_X'$. A source label has position $Y(s)$, velocity $u=Y'$ and energy $q$. Along its retarded branch, set $$t=s+r,\quad rn=X(t)-Y(s),\quad
 d=1-n\cdot u,\quad D=1-n\cdot a,\quad e=1-a\cdot u,
 \qquad |n|=1.$$ A prime denotes differentiation in $s$ along this branch, and $a_t$ denotes the receiver derivative in its own time. By Lemma <a href="#ret:receiver-time" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ret:receiver-time">8</a>, the retarded measure identities are $$\label{can:measure}
 \frac{dt}{ds}=\frac dD,
 \qquad ds\,f_0(z)\,dz=D\,dt\,f(s,y,v)\,dy\,dv.$$ In particular $d,D>0$. Differentiating the retarded relation gives $$\label{can:geometry}
 r'=\frac dD-1,\qquad
 rn'=N_0:=\frac dD(a-n)+n-u,\qquad n\cdot N_0=0.$$ Define the matrices and vectors <a id="can:definitions"></a>$$\label{can:definitions}
 Q=D\mathrm{Id}+n\otimes a,\qquad H=Q/D,\qquad
 g=\frac{u-n}{d},\qquad k_0=u-\frac eD n.$$ Thus $Hg=k_0/d$.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### The exact identity

After the change to $ds\,f_0(z)dz$, the two interior force terms in <a href="#ret:force-kernels" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ret:force-kernels">[ret:force-kernels]</a> together have kernel <a id="can:input-kernel"></a>$$\label{can:input-kernel}
 \mathcal K=-\frac Hr\,\partial_s^{(u)}g
             -\frac{Hg}{r^2q^2d}.$$ Here $\partial_s^{(u)}$ differentiates through $u$ alone. The second term is the transport kernel. As in the force representation, we suppress the common constant $1/(4\pi)$ throughout this section.

<a id="stmt-can-identity"></a>
<div id="can:identity" class="lemma">

<a id="can:label-identity"></a>
<a id="can:bulk"></a>
**Lemma 13** (Signed force identity). *At every non-tip point of a retarded branch, $$\label{can:label-identity}
 \mathcal K
 =-\left(\frac{k_0}{rd}\right)'
  +\frac{e}{r^2D^2}\left(\frac{n}{q_X^2D}-a\right)
  +\frac{n(a_t\cdot k_0)}{rD^2}.$$ Consequently the two nondifferentiated terms, in $dt\,dy\,dv$ measure, are $$\label{can:bulk}
 \mathcal R=\frac{fe}{r^2D}\left(\frac{n}{q_X^2D}-a\right),
 \qquad
 \mathcal A=\frac{fn(a_t\cdot k_0)}{rD}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-can-identity"><summary>Proof of stmt-can-identity</summary>

*Proof.* The product rule first rewrites the source-acceleration term as $$-\frac Hr\partial_s^{(u)}g
 =-(Hg/r)'+(H/r)'g+(H/r)\partial_s^{(n)}g.$$ For a variation $A$ of $a$, with $u,n$ fixed, $$\partial_a k_0[A]=\frac nD(A\cdot k_0).$$ Since $a'=a_t d/D$, its contribution is the last term of <a href="#can:label-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:label-identity">[can:label-identity]</a>. The terms from $r,n$, including the transport term, are $$\frac1r\left(\frac{k_0}{d}\right)'_n
       -\frac{k_0r'}{r^2d}-\frac{k_0}{r^2q^2d^2}.$$ Multiplying by $r^2d^2$ gives $$rd(k_0)'_n+k_0(N_0\cdot u-dr'-q^{-2}).$$ Directly from <a href="#can:geometry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:geometry">[can:geometry]</a> and $|u|^2=1-q^{-2}$, $$N_0\cdot u-dr'-q^{-2}=-\frac{ed}{D},
 \qquad
 rd(k_0)'_n=-\frac{ed}{D}
            \left(N_0+\frac{n(N_0\cdot a)}D\right).$$ Finally, $|a|^2=1-q_X^{-2}$ gives $$N_0+\frac{n(N_0\cdot a)}D+k_0
   =\frac dD\left(a-\frac{n}{q_X^2D}\right).$$ These identities prove the middle term in <a href="#can:label-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:label-identity">[can:label-identity]</a>, with its stated sign. Multiplication by the factor $fD$ in <a href="#can:measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:measure">[can:measure]</a> gives <a href="#can:bulk" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:bulk">[can:bulk]</a>. ◻

</details>

The two residual densities $\mathcal R$ and $\mathcal A$ contain neither the source acceleration $b$ nor inverse powers of the source deficit $d$. The geometric residual $\mathcal R$ will be estimated directly. The receiver term $\mathcal A$ contains $a_t$, so its coefficient must be small enough to use an absolute bound on the receiver force. The primitive $k_0/(rd)$ retains the source singularity: after integration by parts it creates boundary and cutoff-derivative terms. We now keep track of those terms.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Smooth cutoffs and the small angular sector

Use the dyadic representatives $p,\theta,\phi,h$ for, respectively, $$q,\qquad \sqrt{d/2},\qquad \sqrt{D/2},\qquad r/\max(1,T_0).$$ The fixed factor in the convention for $p$ is retained. Thus $q\asymp p$, $q_X\asymp w$, $d\asymp\theta^2$, $D\asymp\phi^2$ and $r\asymp h$, with constants depending on $T_0$ where necessary. The small-sector representatives satisfy $\theta<\kappa\phi$. All estimates below remain valid on the wider overlap range $\theta\leq16\kappa\phi$, after choosing the fixed $\kappa>0$ sufficiently small.

<a id="can:partition"></a>
Use the smooth partitions chosen in Section <a href="#occ:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="occ:section">4</a>, whose factors have uniformly bounded derivatives in the logarithm of each variable. For an index $j=(p,\theta,\phi,h)$, let $\beta_j$ be the product of its four factors. These products satisfy $$\label{can:partition}
 \beta_j\geq0,\qquad \sum_j\beta_j=1.$$ The sum is locally finite for $r>0$, with uniformly bounded overlap. Derivative bounds are taken on the closed support indicators; no estimate of a derivative by the bump itself is assumed.

<a id="stmt-can-cutoff-cancellation"></a>
<div id="can:cutoff-cancellation" class="lemma">

<a id="can:partition-cancel"></a>
**Lemma 14** (Derivatives of a selected sum). *Let $\mathcal S$ be any fixed set of representative indices, all in the small sector, and put $\chi=\sum_{j\in\mathcal S}\beta_j$. Selection may depend on the interval and fixed parameters, but not on the variables being differentiated. For differentiation through source variables $(q,u)$ alone, or through geometric variables $(r,n)$ alone, $$\label{can:partition-cancel}
 \partial\chi=-\sum_{j\notin\mathcal S}\partial\beta_j.$$ This derivative vanishes unless selected and unselected supports meet. On their intersection, representatives in each coordinate differ by at most a factor of four. In particular all relevant supports remain in the wider small-sector range above.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-can-cutoff-cancellation"><summary>Proof of stmt-can-cutoff-cancellation</summary>

*Proof.* Differentiate the locally finite identity <a href="#can:partition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:partition">[can:partition]</a> before restricting to a retarded trajectory. This proves <a href="#can:partition-cancel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:partition-cancel">[can:partition-cancel]</a> separately for each indicated partial derivative. A smooth nonnegative function and its first derivatives vanish at every point where the function vanishes. Thus if either family has no positive summand, the corresponding derivative sum is zero. Meeting supports constrain each representative to within a factor of two of the same variable, proving the factor-four comparison. The ratio $\theta/\phi$ therefore changes by at most a factor of sixteen. ◻

</details>

The cancellation applies to the cutoff derivative only after its common physical multiplier has been factored out. It does not compare derivatives of two different kernels. An additional $\sigma$-partition may subsequently be inserted into a nonnegative remaining-sector bound at the structural edge; it is not differentiated in <a href="#can:partition-cancel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:partition-cancel">[can:partition-cancel]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Bounds for each derivative

<a id="can:source-input"></a>
<a id="can:vector-bounds"></a>
<a id="can:bulk-bounds"></a>
The Lorentz acceleration satisfies $$a_t=(\mathrm{Id}-a\otimes a)K/q_X.$$ The source estimates needed here, with $G=|E+n\times B|+|n\cdot B|$ at the source point, are $$\label{can:source-input}
 |u'|\lesssim p^{-1}(G+\theta^2|B|),\quad
 |n\cdot u'|\lesssim p^{-1}\theta(G+\theta^2|B|),\quad
 |q'|\lesssim G+\theta|B|.$$ These are <a href="#ret:acceleration-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ret:acceleration-bounds">[ret:acceleration-bounds]</a> with $q\asymp p$. For nonzero $V_X$, let $P_X$ denote orthogonal projection perpendicular to $V_X$. In the small sector, $$\label{can:vector-bounds}
 |e-D|\lesssim\phi\theta,\quad
 |k_0|\lesssim\theta/\phi,\quad
 |P_Xk_0|\lesssim\theta,\quad
 |P_Xn|\lesssim\phi.$$ Indeed $e-D=a\cdot(n-u)$; splitting $a=n+(a-n)$ proves the first bound, and then $k_0=(u-n)+n(1-e/D)$ proves the next two. Also $P_Xa=0$. Since $q_X^{-2}\lesssim D$, $$\label{can:bulk-bounds}
 |\mathcal R|\lesssim f/r^2,\quad
 |P_X\mathcal R|\lesssim f\phi/r^2,\quad
 |\mathcal A|\lesssim\frac{f\theta|K|}{wr\phi^2},\quad
 |P_X\mathcal A|\lesssim\frac{f\theta|K|}{wr\phi}.$$ For the last two bounds, $|a_t|\lesssim|K|/w$ and $|n\cdot a_t|\lesssim\phi|K|/w$. Insert the decomposition of $k_0$ in <a href="#can:vector-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:vector-bounds">[can:vector-bounds]</a> to obtain $|a_t\cdot k_0|\lesssim\theta|K|/w$.

After integration by parts, a cutoff derivative in source time has common multiplier $fDk_0/(rd)$ in $dt\,dy\,dv$ measure. Its size is at most $Cf\phi/(r\theta)$, with an additional factor $\phi$ after projection. The separate logarithmic rates and resulting bounds are as follows.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
###### Source derivatives.

<a id="can:source-cutoff-bound"></a>
From <a href="#can:source-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:source-input">[can:source-input]</a>, $$\frac{|q'|}{q}+\frac{|d'_u|}{d}
 \lesssim\frac1p\left(\frac G\theta+\theta|B|\right).$$ Thus their error kernels are bounded by $$\label{can:source-cutoff-bound}
 C\left(\frac{f\phi}{rp\theta^2}G
            +\frac{f\phi}{rp}|B|\right).$$ They coincide with the direct source-force majorants. By Lemma <a href="#can:cutoff-cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:cutoff-cancellation">14</a> they may be assigned to overlapping unselected support indicators.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
###### Geometric derivatives.

<a id="can:spatial-cutoff-bound"></a>
Equation <a href="#can:geometry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:geometry">[can:geometry]</a> gives $|r'|\lesssim1$ and $|n'|\lesssim\theta/r$. Since $n'\perp n$, $$\frac{|d'_n|}{d}\lesssim r^{-1},\qquad
 \frac{|D'_n|}{D}\lesssim\frac{\theta}{r\phi}\lesssim r^{-1}.$$ Together with the logarithmic rate of $r$, these give $$\label{can:spatial-cutoff-bound}
 C\frac{f\phi}{r^2\theta}.$$ These errors can also be assigned to unselected neighbors by Lemma <a href="#can:cutoff-cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:cutoff-cancellation">14</a>.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
###### Receiver derivatives.

<a id="can:receiver-cutoff-bound"></a>
Only $D$ in the cutoff depends directly on $a$. Its source-time rate is $$\frac{|D'_a|}{D}
   =\frac{|n\cdot a_t|}{D}\frac dD
   \lesssim\frac{\theta^2|K|}{w\phi^3}.$$ Multiplication by $fDk_0/(rd)$ gives $$\label{can:receiver-cutoff-bound}
 C\frac{f\theta|K|}{wr\phi^2}.$$ This is estimated on selected supports directly. Every bound <a href="#can:source-cutoff-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:source-cutoff-bound">[can:source-cutoff-bound]</a>–<a href="#can:receiver-cutoff-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:receiver-cutoff-bound">[can:receiver-cutoff-bound]</a> gains a factor $\phi$ after projection by $P_X$.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
###### Time weights and a varying projection.

<a id="can:time-weight-bound"></a>
If $\psi(t)$ is a Lipschitz weight, its derivative contributes $$\label{can:time-weight-bound}
 \psi_t\chi\,\frac{fk_0}{r},
 \qquad\left|\frac{fk_0}{r}\right|\lesssim\frac{f\theta}{r\phi}.$$ For $|V_X|\asymp w$, put $\Pi=wP_X/|V_X|$. Its norm is bounded and $\|\Pi_t\|\lesssim |K|/w$, by differentiation of the unit direction $V_X/|V_X|$ and of $|V_X|^{-1}$. Consequently <a id="can:projector-derivative"></a>$$\label{can:projector-derivative}
 \left|\frac{f\Pi_tk_0}{r}\right|
       \lesssim\frac{f\theta|K|}{wr\phi}.$$ This has the size of the projected receiver term. The estimate uses $|k_0|\lesssim\theta/\phi$, so it retains the longitudinal part of $k_0$.

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### Integration by parts and the initial cone

Write $\mathcal K_t=fD\mathcal K$ for the interior force density in $dt\,dy\,dv$ measure; the subscript identifies the measure and does not denote a derivative.

<a id="stmt-can-weighted-formula"></a>
<div id="can:weighted-formula" class="proposition">

<a id="can:weighted-terms"></a>
<a id="can:initial-boundary-bound"></a>
**Proposition 15** (Weighted signed formula). *Let $J=[t_1,t_2]$ lie in a compact classical time interval. Let $0\leq\psi\leq1$ be Lipschitz on $J$, vanish at its endpoints, and be extended by zero outside $J$. Let $\chi$ be a selected sum as in Lemma <a href="#can:cutoff-cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:cutoff-cancellation">14</a>. One may integrate <a href="#can:label-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:label-identity">[can:label-identity]</a> labelwise against $\psi\chi$ and a bounded $C^1$ matrix $\Pi(t)$, including $\Pi=\mathrm{Id}$. More precisely, $$\begin{aligned}
\label{can:weighted-terms}
 \int_J\!\int\psi\chi\Pi\mathcal K_t\,dy\,dv\,dt
 ={}&\mathcal B_0+\int_J\!\int
 \biggl[\psi\chi\Pi(\mathcal R+\mathcal A)
       +\psi_t\chi\Pi\frac{fk_0}{r}\notag\\
 &\hspace{25mm}+\psi\chi\Pi_t\frac{fk_0}{r}
       +\psi\Pi\frac{fDk_0}{rd}\chi'_s\biggr]\,dy\,dv\,dt,
\end{aligned}$$ where the spatial integral has $r=|X(t)-y|<t$, all source quantities are evaluated at $s=t-r$, and $\mathcal B_0$ is the initial-cone boundary term. If $\|\Pi\|\lesssim1$, that boundary term is bounded in norm by $$\label{can:initial-boundary-bound}
 C\int_J|\psi(t_0)|\,dt_0.$$ The constant in <a href="#can:initial-boundary-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:initial-boundary-bound">[can:initial-boundary-bound]</a> depends only on the datum and fixed time horizon. For $\Pi=\mathrm{Id}$, and for $\Pi=wP_X/|V_X|$ when $|V_X|\asymp w$, the derivative kernels satisfy the bounds above, uniformly in the fixed selected index set. For a general $\Pi$, its derivative term retains the explicit bound $Cf\theta\|\Pi_t\|/(r\phi)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-can-weighted-formula"><summary>Proof of stmt-can-weighted-formula</summary>

*Proof.* The null set of labels with a same-time collision with the receiver can be discarded by Lemma <a href="#ret:receiver-time" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ret:receiver-time">8</a>. For any remaining label, continuity on the compact time interval gives $\min_t|Y(t)-X(t)|>0$. At a retarded point, $$|Y(t)-X(t)|\leq |Y(t)-Y(s)|+r\leq2r,$$ so $r$ is bounded below on that label’s branch. The branch is $C^1$ and increasing. Its entry at source time $s=0$, when present, has receiver time $t_0$ determined by $t_0=|X(t_0)-Y(0)|$. All other integration endpoints occur where $\psi=0$. Products with Lipschitz $\psi$ are absolutely continuous along the branch. Integrating the total derivative in <a href="#can:label-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:label-identity">[can:label-identity]</a> is therefore legitimate for each such label. The factors $\psi_s=\psi_t d/D$ and $\Pi_s=\Pi_t d/D$, followed by <a href="#can:measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:measure">[can:measure]</a>, give exactly <a href="#can:weighted-terms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:weighted-terms">[can:weighted-terms]</a>.

The sign at $s=0$ is positive. Its integrand relative to initial labels is $$f_0(z)\psi(t_0)\chi\Pi(t_0)\frac{k_0}{t_0d(0)}.$$ For fixed initial velocity, parameterize the initial position by $y_{\rm in}=X(t_0)-t_0n$. This map is one-to-one on the initial cone and has absolute Jacobian $t_0^2D$. The initial momentum support bounds $d(0)$ below, and $$Dk_0=Du-ne$$ is bounded in norm. Since $\chi\leq1$, boundedness and compact velocity support of $f_0$, and $t_0\leq T_0$, give <a href="#can:initial-boundary-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:initial-boundary-bound">[can:initial-boundary-bound]</a>. The same proof allows disjoint time weights to be summed, using their sum at the initial boundary.

Finally these operations also hold after integrating the labels and summing the dyadic products. On a fixed compact classical interval, $d,D$ have positive lower bounds on the relevant supported trajectories, and source and receiver accelerations are bounded. After conversion to $dt\,dy\,dv$, all differentiated kernels have an integrable majorant $C_{\rm comp} f(r^{-2}+r^{-1})$, using bounded partition overlap. Indeed $dy=r^2dr\,dn$ and the momentum support is bounded. The constant here may depend on compact classical bounds and on the fixed weight; it is used only to justify the identity, not in the uniform estimates. For a completely truncated argument, insert a radial cutoff vanishing for $r<\gamma$ and equal to one for $r>2\gamma$. Its new derivative term is bounded by $C_{\rm comp}f/(\gamma r)$ on $r\asymp\gamma$, whose integral is $O_{\rm comp}(\gamma)$. All remaining terms converge by domination. Local finiteness for $r>0$ and the same majorants justify the infinite sum of distance bins and its differentiated version. Thus no cone-tip boundary remains. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### Coefficients used in the later selections

<a id="can:receiver-coefficient"></a>
We record a coefficient bound used in both selections, so that their receiver-derivative costs can be read without repeating the geometry. At a fixed receiver time, the coefficient on $|K|$ in the receiver term of <a href="#can:bulk-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:bulk-bounds">[can:bulk-bounds]</a> or in <a href="#can:receiver-cutoff-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:receiver-cutoff-bound">[can:receiver-cutoff-bound]</a>, integrated over one small bin, obeys $$\label{can:receiver-coefficient}
 \frac Cw\min\left(h^2(p\theta)^3,
                       \frac1{p\theta h\phi^2}\right).$$ For the first bound use the density estimate $\int_{\rm bin}f\,dv\lesssim p^3\theta^2$, the $n$-cap area $O(\phi^2)$, and $dy=r^2ds\,dn$. For the second, divide the kernel $f\theta/(wr\phi^2)$ by the particle cone-flux density $qdf$ and use the cone budget. Projection adds $\phi$ to the first bound; the $\Pi_t$ term has the same projected size.

The direction estimate in Section <a href="#ang:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ang:section">7</a> will use the first, projected coefficient with its own selection of indices; that argument also accounts for the varying projector. The final signed-increment estimate in Section <a href="#sel:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sel:section">8</a> will use the two-sided minimum in <a href="#can:receiver-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:receiver-coefficient">[can:receiver-coefficient]</a>. Both applications keep their selected index sets fixed while differentiating the cutoffs.

<a id="ang:section"></a>
### Direction changes and improved occupation

We now improve the occupation estimate when both particles have high energy and their relative angle is intermediate.
