The additional information is a bound on the number of direction changes of a prescribed angular size. Its proof uses the signed bootstrap and the baseline estimates already established; it does not use the improved occupation estimate proved at the end of this section.

We retain the notation of the preceding sections. In particular, $L=\log(2+P)$, $S(w)=P^2L/w$, and $m=\min(p,w)$. All constants below may depend on the datum, the fixed time horizon, and the fixed decomposition parameters. A constant written $C(M,A)$ may also depend on the two bootstrap parameters. The lower threshold for $P$ may depend on $M,A$. Fix a bootstrap endpoint $\tau$: throughout this section the bootstrap is assumed only on $[0,\tau]$, and all time intervals are clipped to this range.

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### A bound on changes of direction

<a id="stmt-ang-direction"></a>
<div id="ang:direction" class="lemma">

<a id="ang:count"></a>
**Lemma 16** (Direction changes). *Assume the signed increment bootstrap <a href="#eq:bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bootstrap">[eq:bootstrap]</a> on $[0,\tau]$. Let $w$ be a dyad with $w\ge P^{3/5}$, and let $J\subset[0,\tau]$ be a closed interval of length at most $(w/P)^2$ on which a supported characteristic satisfies $w/8\le q_X\le8w$. Suppose $$w^{-3/5}\le\delta\le w^{-1/10}.$$ Starting at the left endpoint of $J$, stop whenever the direction $\xi=V_X/|V_X|$ first has chordal distance $\delta$ from its direction at the preceding stopping time. Include the last, possibly incomplete, interval. For sufficiently large $P$, the number of intervals is at most $$\label{ang:count}
 C(M,A)L^6/\delta.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ang-direction"><summary>Proof of stmt-ang-direction</summary>

<a id="ang:normalization"></a>
*Proof.* Put $I=(w/P)^2$. Throughout $J$, $|V_X|\asymp w$, so normalization of nonzero vectors gives $$\label{ang:normalization}
 |\xi(t)-\xi(s)|\le Cw^{-1}|V_X(t)-V_X(s)|.$$ Define $$\ell=\min\left\{\left(\frac{w\delta}{MP}\right)^2,
                         \frac{w\delta}{AS(w)}\right\}.$$ The bootstrap and <a href="#ang:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:normalization">[ang:normalization]</a> bound the direction change on an interval of length $c\ell$ by $C(\sqrt c+c)\delta$. Choosing $c>0$ sufficiently small proves that each complete first-exit interval has length at least $c\ell$. In particular their number, denoted by $N_c$, is finite.

<a id="ang:weighted-exit"></a>
On each complete interval choose an endpoint-zero Lipschitz function $0\le\psi\le1$ with monotone ramps of length $c'\ell$ and value one between the ramps. Choose $c'$ sufficiently small that the ramps are disjoint. On a ramp, integrate the removed weight against $\xi_t$ after subtracting the direction at its outer endpoint. The derivative of the removed weight has $L^1$ norm one, so the removed signed integral is bounded by $C(\sqrt{c'}+c')\delta$. Here the bootstrap bounds endpoint-relative increments; no bound on absolute direction variation is used. After reducing the fixed constant $c'$ once more, each complete interval satisfies $$\label{ang:weighted-exit}
 \left|\int\psi\,w\xi_t\,dt\right|\ge\frac12w\delta,
 \qquad \int|\psi_t|\,dt=2.$$

<a id="ang:selection"></a>
Let $P_X=\mathrm{Id}-\xi\otimes\xi$ and $\Pi=wP_X/|V_X|$, so $w\xi_t=\Pi K$. Apply Proposition <a href="#can:weighted-formula" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:weighted-formula">15</a> with this multiplier and with the fixed selection $$\label{ang:selection}
 \theta<\kappa\phi,\qquad p\theta\sqrt h\le1.$$ The same representative index set is used on every interval. The weights have disjoint interiors and sum to at most one. Thus, after taking norms, all bulk errors can be bounded over their union with the single upper length $I$. Only derivatives of the weights will produce a factor $N_c$.

First consider the receiver and time-weight derivatives. Their bounds explain the radial threshold in <a href="#ang:selection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:selection">[ang:selection]</a>. The signed primitive contains $k_0=u-ne/D$, with $$|k_0|\le C\theta/\phi,\qquad |P_Xk_0|\le C\theta.$$ The projected receiver-acceleration and receiver-cutoff kernels have size at most $$Cf\theta|K|/(wr\phi).$$ The moving projector has precisely the same size: writing $\rho=|V_X|$ gives $$\Pi_t=-\frac{w}{\rho^2}
 \left[(\xi\cdot K)P_X+(P_XK)\otimes\xi
                         +\xi\otimes(P_XK)\right],
 \qquad
 |\Pi_tk_0|\le\frac{C\theta|K|}{w\phi}.$$ The representative $w$ is fixed here; no dyad indicator is differentiated.

At fixed receiver time, integrating these kernels over a bin uses the radial factor $Ch^2$, the velocity density bound $Cp^3\theta^2$, and an angular cap of area $C\phi^2$. Their coefficient on $|K|$ is therefore <a id="ang:receiver-coefficient"></a>$$\label{ang:receiver-coefficient}
 \frac{Ch^2(p\theta)^3\phi}{w}
 \le \frac{C\sqrt h\,\phi}{w}
 \quad\hbox{on selected indices}.$$ The sums in $h,\phi$ are geometric. The remaining $p,\theta$ count is $O(L^2)$, so the total coefficient is $CL^2/w$. The baseline absolute-force bound on our interval is $$\int_J|K|\,dt
 \le C\sqrt w\{P\sqrt I+(M+\sqrt A)IS(w)\}
 \le C(M,A)w^{3/2}L.$$ Thus all receiver and projector derivative terms contribute at most $C(M,A)w^{1/2}L^3$. They do not contribute a factor $N_c$.

For a weight derivative, the kernel is $f\Pi k_0/r$, and its fixed-time coefficient is instead <a id="ang:weight-coefficient"></a>$$\label{ang:weight-coefficient}
 Ch^2(p\theta)^3\phi^2\le C\sqrt h\,\phi^2.$$ Its sum is $CL^2$. Equation <a href="#ang:weighted-exit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:weighted-exit">[ang:weighted-exit]</a> consequently gives a total weight-derivative cost $CL^2N_c$. Initial-cone boundary terms are bounded by $C\int\sum\psi\,dt\le CI$ and create no further cell count.

<a id="ang:baseline-R"></a>
<a id="ang:R-bound"></a>
We now estimate the remaining terms. On the complementary small-sector bins, $p\theta\sqrt h>1$ will make the projected good-field far bound summable. Outside the small-scale deficit exception, the baseline occupation estimates give $$\label{ang:baseline-R}
 \mathsf M\le ChR^2/p,
 \qquad R=1+\sqrt{I/\lambda}.$$ For $h>I$ this follows instead from the elementary energy bound $\mathsf M\le Ch/p$. We use the unrefined $R$ in <a href="#ang:baseline-R" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:baseline-R">[ang:baseline-R]</a>; in particular no enhanced occupation estimate enters this proof. The definition of $\lambda$ in the small sector yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \sqrt I\,Rp\phi
 &\le \frac{wp\phi}{P}
   +C\frac{w^2p}{Pm}\left(M+\sqrt{AL\phi}\right)
 \le C(M,A)w\sqrt L,                                      \label{ang:R-bound}
\end{aligned}$$ where $wp\le mP$ was used in the last inequality.

The baseline direct force bound contributes at most $C(M,A)wL$. For the small-sector good-field term, perpendicular projection supplies an additional factor $\phi$. Its far bound outside <a href="#ang:selection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:selection">[ang:selection]</a> is therefore $$C\sqrt I\,R\frac{\phi}{\theta\sqrt h}
 \le \frac{C(M,A)w\sqrt L}{p\theta\sqrt h}.$$ When the second condition in <a href="#ang:selection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:selection">[ang:selection]</a> fails, the denominator is greater than one and $h>P^{-2}$. Hence only $O(L)$ radial dyads occur. There are also $O(L)$ momentum dyads and $O(L)$ dyads for each angle. Summing gives at most $C(M,A)wL^5$.

The small deficit exception is entirely within <a href="#ang:selection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:selection">[ang:selection]</a> after increasing the fixed constant $C_1$ in the definition of $\lambda$. Indeed, when $h\le\lambda$ and $\phi\le C_0/m$, $$p\theta\sqrt h\le p\theta\sqrt\lambda
 \le C\kappa(p/P)m\phi^2/C_1
 \le C\kappa C_0^2/(C_1m)\le1.$$ Source cutoff derivatives are assigned to overlapping unselected bins by Lemma <a href="#can:cutoff-cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:cutoff-cancellation">14</a>. At the structural edge $\theta\asymp\kappa\phi$, use the direct bounds for the remaining sector. Fixed-factor neighboring indices change only constants in these estimates.

By <a href="#can:spatial-cutoff-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:spatial-cutoff-bound">[can:spatial-cutoff-bound]</a>, the projected spatial cutoff kernel is bounded by $Cf\phi^2/(r^2\theta)$. Its baseline near/far balance, followed by the geometric sum in $h$, is $$C\sqrt I\,Rp\phi^2$$ per remaining indices. By <a href="#ang:R-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:R-bound">[ang:R-bound]</a>, the sum over $p,\theta,
\phi$ is at most $C(M,A)wL^4$. The projected central residual is $Cf\phi/r^2$, which is smaller in this sector. For the exceptional deficit scales, use their near bounds directly. Summing $h\le\lambda\le C/P^2$ and then $\theta\le C\phi$ gives $$CI P^{-2}p^3\phi^5\le CI p^3/(P^2m^5).$$ These terms fit the same bulk bound after summing the remaining dyads. This separate near estimate avoids using <a href="#ang:baseline-R" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:baseline-R">[ang:baseline-R]</a> on its exceptional set.

Summing <a href="#ang:weighted-exit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:weighted-exit">[ang:weighted-exit]</a> now yields the conservative bound $$\tfrac12N_cw\delta
 \le C(M,A)wL^6+CL^2N_c.$$ Since $w\delta\ge w^{2/5}\ge P^{6/25}$, the last term is absorbed for sufficiently large $P$. Division by $w\delta$ and addition of the last incomplete interval prove <a href="#ang:count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:count">[ang:count]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
#### Occupation at intermediate angles

The baseline cell argument keeps each velocity within a small multiple of $\phi$ of its value at a reference hit, so its cell length $\lambda$ shrinks with that tolerance. We now use cells whose length is chosen to keep only the energies comparable to $p$ and $w$. Within each such cell, Lemma <a href="#ang:direction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ang:direction">16</a> partitions the source and receiver motions into intervals of nearly fixed direction. Their common refinement permits the same monotone-crossing argument as before; the number of direction intervals controls the total hit duration.

<a id="stmt-ang-improved-hit"></a>
<div id="ang:improved-hit" class="lemma">

<a id="ang:window"></a>
<a id="ang:improved-occupation"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Direction-based occupation at intermediate angles). *Assume the signed increment bootstrap on $[0,\tau]$. Let a small-sector bin satisfy $$\label{ang:window}
 p,w\ge P^{7/10},\qquad
 j^{-49/100}\le\phi\le j^{-4/25}\quad(j=p,w).$$ Let the eligible receiver times lie in a closed interval $J\subset[0,\tau]$ of length at most $I\le(w/P)^2$, with receiver energy in $[w/8,8w]$ at those times. Set $I_m^0=(m/P)^2$ and $\beta=29/25$. Then, for $P\ge P_*(M,A)$, the hit measure satisfies $$\label{ang:improved-occupation}
 \mathsf M\le C\frac hp\phi^{-\beta}
      \max\left(1,\frac I{I_m^0},\frac h{I_m^0}\right).$$ The constant $C$ is independent of $M,A$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ang-improved-hit"><summary>Proof of stmt-ang-improved-hit</summary>

<a id="ang:margins"></a>
*Proof.* The window <a href="#ang:window" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:window">[ang:window]</a> implies $$\label{ang:margins}
 m\phi\ge m^{51/100},\qquad
 m^{-2}/\phi\le m^{-151/100},\qquad
 \phi\le P^{-14/125}.$$ Thus the bins are actual for large $P$, and speed-magnitude deficits are small compared to $\phi$.

Choose a sufficiently large fixed constant $C_2$ and put $$Q_0=C_2(M^2+AL),\qquad \lambda_0=I_m^0/Q_0.$$ For $h>\lambda_0$, the elementary energy estimate already suffices. Indeed, for sufficiently large $P$, <a href="#ang:margins" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:margins">[ang:margins]</a> implies $\phi^{-\beta}\ge Q_0$, and hence $h\phi^{-\beta}\ge I_m^0$. Consequently $$h\phi^{-\beta}\max(1,I/I_m^0,h/I_m^0)\ge\max(I,h),$$ which proves <a href="#ang:improved-occupation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:improved-occupation">[ang:improved-occupation]</a> in this case.

Suppose now that $h\le\lambda_0$. For upper bounds we may enlarge to all eligible receiver times in $J$ and use closed bin support ranges. Partition source time into intervals of length $\lambda_0$, clipped to $[0,\tau]$. All hit source times lie in an interval of length at most $C(I+h)$, so at most $C(1+(I+h)/\lambda_0)$ original intervals can contribute. Fix one such interval $F$. Extend it to the right by $C_T h$, where $C_T$ bounds $r/h$, and clip this extension to $[0,\tau]$.

If $F$ has a hit, the receiver energy is comparable to $w$ throughout the extended interval. For every label hitting $F$, its source energy is comparable to $p$ throughout $F$. To see this, apply the bootstrap up to the first exit from a fixed enlarged range around a reference hit energy. On a time interval of length $C_T\lambda_0$, the two bootstrap terms are at most $Cm/\sqrt{C_2}$ and $Cm/C_2$. Increasing $C_2$ excludes the first exit. The argument works in both time directions and is unaffected by clipping.

For each use of Lemma <a href="#ang:direction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ang:direction">16</a>, choose a dyad $j'$ nearest the relevant reference-hit energy. It is comparable to $w$ for the receiver and to $p$ for a source. The entire interval stays within $[j'/8,8j']$. Increasing $C_2$ also ensures that its length is at most $(j'/P)^2$. For large $P$, $j'\ge P^{3/5}$. Choose a small fixed $c_2>0$ and use $\delta=c_2\phi$. Uniformly for $j'\asymp j$, $$\frac{\delta}{j'^{-3/5}}\ge c j^{11/100}\longrightarrow\infty,
 \qquad
 \frac{\delta}{j'^{-1/10}}\le Cj^{-3/50}\longrightarrow0.$$ Therefore all the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#ang:direction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ang:direction">16</a> hold.

<a id="ang:label-duration"></a>
Choose one common receiver direction partition on the extended interval. Its number of cells is at most $C(M,A)L^6/\phi$. For each hit label, independently choose its source direction partition on $F$, with the same bound on the number of cells. We shall prove that the hit duration for each such label is at most $$\label{ang:label-duration}
 C(M,A)hL^6/\phi.$$

Call a hit bad if a boundary $b$ of the receiver direction partition lies in $[s,t]$, and call all other hits good. Since $0<t-s\le C_T h$, the source time of a bad hit belongs to $[b-C_T h,b]$. The total duration of bad hits is thus bounded by $C(M,A)hL^6/\phi$. This bound uses only the time difference $t-s$; it is independent of the retarded derivative $dt/ds$.

<a id="ang:small-separation"></a>
At a remaining hit, $s,t$ lie in one receiver direction cell. Throughout $[s,t]$ its direction varies by at most $2c_2\phi$, and its speed differs from one by $O(w^{-2})$. The bin condition $|a(t)-n|\le C\phi$ and <a href="#ang:margins" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:margins">[ang:margins]</a> therefore give $$\label{ang:small-separation}
 |Y(s)-X(s)|
 =\left|\int_s^t(a(\zeta)-n)\,d\zeta\right|
 \le Ch\phi.$$

On $F$, refine the source and receiver direction partitions together. The number of resulting intervals is bounded by the sum of the two numbers of cells plus one. In one such interval containing a good hit, choose that hit as reference. Actual small-angle geometry gives $|u(s)-a(t)|\ge c\phi$. Replacing $a(t)$ by $a(s)$ changes this vector by at most $Cc_2\phi+Cm^{-2}$. At any other time in the combined interval, the change of relative velocity has the same bound, since each direction varies by at most $2c_2\phi$ and both speed deficits are $O(m^{-2})$. Choose $c_2$ small and then $P$ large. There is a fixed unit vector $e_F$ for this label and combined interval such that $$e_F\cdot(u(s)-a(s))\ge c\phi$$ throughout it. Thus $e_F\cdot(Y-X)$ is strictly increasing with derivative at least $c\phi$. At good hits it lies in an interval of length at most $Ch\phi$, by <a href="#ang:small-separation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:small-separation">[ang:small-separation]</a>. Their total duration in this combined interval is consequently at most $Ch$. This remains true when the good-hit set has several components. Summing the combined intervals and adding the bad-hit bound proves <a href="#ang:label-duration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:label-duration">[ang:label-duration]</a>.

The set of labels hitting $F$ has mass at most $C/p$: at any one time of $F$, all of these labels have energy at least $cp$, by the preceding stability argument, and total particle energy is bounded. The closed support convention makes the set of hit labels a compact projection before the null collision labels are discarded, as in the baseline occupation argument. The hit set is measurable, and the preceding reasoning bounds each of its source-time sections. The label-dependent reference hits and direction partitions serve only to prove this numerical bound; no measurability of those choices is required.

<a id="ang:before-absorption"></a>
Multiplying the mass bound by <a href="#ang:label-duration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:label-duration">[ang:label-duration]</a> and then counting the original intervals yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathsf M
 &\le C(M,A)\frac hp\phi^{-1}L^6
                   \left(1+\frac{I+h}{\lambda_0}\right)\notag\\
 &\le C(M,A)\frac hp\phi^{-1}L^7
                   \max\left(1,\frac I{I_m^0},\frac h{I_m^0}\right).
                                                        \label{ang:before-absorption}
\end{aligned}$$ Clipped intervals and final direction remainders satisfy all the same estimates. Finally, since $\beta-1=4/25$, <a href="#ang:margins" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:margins">[ang:margins]</a> gives $$\phi^{-(\beta-1)}\ge P^{56/3125}.$$ This positive power absorbs the factor $C(M,A)L^7$ for $P\ge P_*(M,A)$. Equation <a href="#ang:before-absorption" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:before-absorption">[ang:before-absorption]</a> becomes <a href="#ang:improved-occupation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ang:improved-occupation">[ang:improved-occupation]</a>, with a constant independent of the bootstrap parameters. ◻

</details>

The gain in Lemma <a href="#ang:improved-hit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ang:improved-hit">17</a> comes from controlling how often relative motion can change its direction. Within each combined direction interval, a hit is confined to a short monotone crossing. Only after this estimate has been proved will it be used in the final selection of bins for the signed momentum bound.

<a id="sel:section"></a>
### Selecting the signed part of the force

<a id="sel:short"></a>
<a id="sel:baseline"></a>
<a id="sel:absolute"></a>
Fix a supported receiver and an interval $J=[t_1,t_2]$ of length $I>0$ on which $w/8\le q_X\le8w$. Throughout this section we assume <a href="#eq:bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bootstrap">[eq:bootstrap]</a> and $$\label{sel:short}
 I\le (w/P)^2.$$ Write $L=\log(2+P)$ and $S(w)=P^2L/w$. The estimates proved above give $$\label{sel:baseline}
 \mathcal B(I,w)=P\sqrt I+(M+\sqrt A)I S(w)$$ as a bound, up to a fixed factor, for the direct transport and magnetic-remainder terms and the remaining-sector good-field term. They also give $$\label{sel:absolute}
 \int_J|K(t)|\,dt\le C\sqrt w\,\mathcal B(I,w).$$ The extra factor $\sqrt w$ in this absolute estimate will be canceled by the small coefficient of the receiver terms after signed integration by parts.

Only the narrow-angle contribution controlled by $S_g$ remains outside the baseline bound. We estimate bins directly when a near or far bound for $S_g$ is already below a summable threshold. On the other bins we apply the signed identity to the original force kernels, including the transport term. The main task is to show that these selected bins have a sufficiently small coefficient on the receiver acceleration.

We use the small-sector representatives $p,\theta,\phi,h$, with $\theta<\kappa\phi$, $\theta\ge1/p$, $p\le P$, and $\phi,h\le1$. All constants below may depend on the fixed decomposition and on <a id="sel:epsilon"></a>$$\label{sel:epsilon}
 \epsilon=10^{-5},\qquad \beta=1.16,$$ but are independent of $M,A,P,I,w$. A sufficiently large lower threshold for $P$ may depend on $M,A$.

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### The selection and its summable weight

<a id="sel:lambda"></a>
<a id="sel:pure"></a>
Put $m=\min(p,w)$ and recall the small-sector stability length $$\label{sel:lambda}
 \lambda^{-1/2}
 =C_1\frac Pm\left(\frac M\phi+\sqrt{\frac{AL}{\phi}}\right).$$ Put $I_m^0=(m/P)^2$. The three occupation bounds available for selection come from energy, stable source-time cells, and the direction estimate, respectively. Define $\mathsf M_*$ to be the minimum of the applicable entries in the following table. The displayed entries contain no implicit constants. $$\label{sel:pure}
\begin{array}{c|l}
 \text{entry}&\text{condition for inclusion}\\ \hline
 \max(I,h)/p&\text{always}\\[2pt]
 (h/p)\max(1,I/\lambda,h/\lambda)&\phi>C_0/m\\[2pt]
 (h/p)\phi^{-\beta}\max(1,I/I_m^0,h/I_m^0)
 &p,w\ge P^{.7},\quad
   j^{-.49}\le\phi\le j^{-.16}\ (j=p,w),
\end{array}$$ The occupation estimates give $\mathsf M\le C\mathsf M_*$ in every small bin. The distinction between this inequality and the exact entries of <a href="#sel:pure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:pure">[sel:pure]</a> is useful: the selection below is an exact inequality between representative quantities.

We assign a summable weight to each bin: <a id="sel:weight"></a>$$\label{sel:weight}
 W=(P\theta)^{-\epsilon}\phi^\epsilon h^\epsilon.$$ The same weight has two roles. The good-field contribution of an unselected bin will be at most $CP\sqrt I\,W$ by direct estimation. For a selected bin, we will prove that the coefficient of $|K|$ in the receiver terms is at most $Cw^{-1/2}W$. Summing the latter coefficients will cancel the factor $\sqrt w$ in <a href="#sel:absolute" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:absolute">[sel:absolute]</a>. The remaining task will then be to control the central residual and the cutoff and time-weight derivatives in the signed formula.

A small bin is *selected* if <a id="sel:selection"></a>$$\label{sel:selection}
 \min\left\{I\sqrt h\,p^2\phi^2,
       \frac{\sqrt I\sqrt{p\mathsf M_*}}{\theta h}\right\}
       >P\sqrt I\,W.$$ This is a fixed set of indices during $J$. In particular, taking a partial derivative of a cutoff does not differentiate the selection.

<a id="stmt-sel-weight-sum"></a>
<div id="sel:weight-sum" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18**. *The sum of $W$ over all small-sector indices is bounded by a constant depending only on $\epsilon$ and the fixed decomposition. The unselected small-sector good-field terms have total integral at most $CP\sqrt I$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sel-weight-sum"><summary>Proof of stmt-sel-weight-sum</summary>

*Proof.* The $\theta$ sum satisfies $$\sum_{\theta\ge1/p}(P\theta)^{-\epsilon}
 \le C_\epsilon(p/P)^\epsilon.$$ The remaining sums in $p\le P$, $\phi\le1$, and $h\le1$ are geometric. The small sector has no additional $\sigma$ partition. For an unselected bin, the near and far good-field estimates, together with $\mathsf M\le C\mathsf M_*$, bound its integral by $CP\sqrt I\,W$. Summing proves the second assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-41"></a>
#### The receiver coefficient on selected bins

<a id="sel:U"></a>
We first verify the coefficient bound promised by the selection. The source-time identity produces both the receiver-acceleration term $\mathcal A$ and a derivative of the receiver-angle cutoff. By <a href="#can:bulk-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:bulk-bounds">[can:bulk-bounds]</a> and <a href="#can:receiver-cutoff-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:receiver-cutoff-bound">[can:receiver-cutoff-bound]</a>, both have majorant $Cf\theta|K|/(wr\phi^2)$. At a fixed receiver time, the density bound $\int_{\rm bin}f\,dv\le Cp^3\theta^2$ and the angular cap give a coefficient at most $Cw^{-1}h^2(p\theta)^3$ on $|K|$. Alternatively, the particle cone-flux bound gives $C/(wp\theta h\phi^2)$. Thus the coefficient, as recorded in <a href="#can:receiver-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:receiver-coefficient">[can:receiver-coefficient]</a>, is at most a fixed multiple of $$\label{sel:U}
 U=w^{-1}\min\left\{h^2(p\theta)^3,
                         (p\theta h\phi^2)^{-1}\right\}.$$

<a id="stmt-sel-exponents"></a>
<div id="sel:exponents" class="lemma">

<a id="sel:safe"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Receiver coefficient on selected bins). *For fixed $M,A$, there is a threshold $P_*(M,A)$ such that every selected index with $P\ge P_*(M,A)$ satisfies the exact representative inequality $$\label{sel:safe}
 U\le w^{-1/2}W.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sel-exponents"><summary>Proof of stmt-sel-exponents</summary>

*Proof.* We argue by contradiction: a selected bin violating <a href="#sel:safe" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:safe">[sel:safe]</a> will first be forced into the actual regime, then into the improved-occupation window, where its exponent inequalities are incompatible.

<a id="sel:near-exponent"></a>
<a id="sel:far-energy"></a>
<a id="sel:delta-small"></a>
**Step 1: an unsafe selected bin has actual velocity separation.** Write the representatives as exact powers of $P$: <a id="sel:powers"></a>$$\label{sel:powers}
 \begin{gathered}
 p=P^{1-\alpha},\qquad w=P^z,\qquad c=1-z,\\
 p\theta=P^b,\qquad \phi=P^{-y},\qquad h=P^{-H},\qquad
 \Delta=\epsilon(\alpha+b+y+H).
 \end{gathered}$$ All terminating decimals below denote exact rational numbers. Here $\alpha,z,c,b,y,H$ are nonnegative and $z+c=1$. The weight is $W=P^{-\Delta}$. Near selection and <a href="#sel:short" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:short">[sel:short]</a> give $$\label{sel:near-exponent}
 2\alpha+H/2+2y<z+\Delta.$$ Far selection with the first pure bound in <a href="#sel:pure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:pure">[sel:pure]</a> gives $$\label{sel:far-energy}
 \begin{cases}
 b<H/2-\alpha+\Delta,&H\le2c,\\
 b<H-c-\alpha+\Delta,&H>2c.
 \end{cases}$$ Indeed, when $H\le2c$ one has $h\ge I$, whereas otherwise $\max(I,h)\le P^{-2c}$. In either case $b\le H-\alpha+\Delta$. Putting $d_*=\alpha+b+y+H$, we obtain $$d_*\le2H+y+\Delta<4z+5\Delta.$$ It follows that $$\label{sel:delta-small}
 z>0,\qquad
 \Delta<\frac4{99995}z<.001z.$$ Only the pure energy bound has been used so far.

<a id="sel:unsafe"></a>
<a id="sel:H-lower"></a>
<a id="sel:actual-margin"></a>
Suppose <a href="#sel:safe" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:safe">[sel:safe]</a> fails. Both terms of the minimum in <a href="#sel:U" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:U">[sel:U]</a> must exceed $w^{-1/2}W$, so $$\label{sel:unsafe}
 b>\frac{2H}3+\frac z6-\frac\Delta3,
 \qquad b<H+2y-\frac z2+\Delta.$$ If $H\le2c$, the first inequalities of <a href="#sel:far-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:far-energy">[sel:far-energy]</a> and <a href="#sel:unsafe" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:unsafe">[sel:unsafe]</a> force $H+z+6\alpha<8\Delta<.008z$, which is impossible. Hence $H>2c$, and the other far bound gives $$\label{sel:H-lower}
 H>\frac z2+3(\alpha+c)-4\Delta
 >.496z+3(\alpha+c).$$ Combining this with <a href="#sel:near-exponent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:near-exponent">[sel:near-exponent]</a>, $$\label{sel:actual-margin}
 3.5\alpha+1.5c+2y<.753z,\qquad
 z>\frac{500}{751}>.66,\qquad
 y<.377z-1.75\alpha-.75c.$$ In particular, $$z-y>.623z+1.75\alpha+.75c\ge.623,$$ and $$1-\alpha-y>.623z+.75\alpha+1.75c\ge.623.$$ Thus $m\phi>P^{.3}$, and the bin is actual for sufficiently large $P$. This conclusion precedes any absorption of constants into powers of $P$.

<a id="sel:far-actual"></a>
<a id="sel:high-momenta"></a>
**Step 2: stable-cell occupation forces that bin into the improved window.** Put $\rho=\max(\alpha,c)$, so $P/m=P^\rho$. Equation <a href="#sel:lambda" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:lambda">[sel:lambda]</a> and $\phi\le1$ give $$\lambda^{-1/2}
 \le C_1(M+\sqrt{AL})P^{\rho+y}
 \le P^{\rho+y+.001z}$$ once $P\ge P_*(M,A)$. The last threshold is uniform over the indices because $z>.66$. Since $I,h\le P^{-2c}$ and $\rho\ge c$, the actual pure bound gives $$\sqrt{p\mathsf M_*}
 \le P^{-H/2+\rho-c+y+.001z}.$$ Far selection therefore implies $$\label{sel:far-actual}
 b<H/2-\min(\alpha,c)+y+.002z.$$ Comparing <a href="#sel:far-actual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:far-actual">[sel:far-actual]</a> with the first inequality in <a href="#sel:unsafe" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:unsafe">[sel:unsafe]</a>, then using <a href="#sel:near-exponent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:near-exponent">[sel:near-exponent]</a>, yields <a id="sel:middle"></a>$$\label{sel:middle}
 2y>H/3+.328z+2\min(\alpha,c),\qquad
 H<.8076z<.81z.$$ Together with <a href="#sel:H-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:H-lower">[sel:H-lower]</a>, this gives $$\label{sel:high-momenta}
 \alpha+c<.105z,\qquad z>\frac{200}{221}>.90,
 \qquad1-\alpha>.895.$$ The two inequalities in <a href="#sel:unsafe" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:unsafe">[sel:unsafe]</a> also imply $$y>\frac z3-\frac H6-\frac{2\Delta}3>.19z.$$

We now check both ends of the improved-occupation window for both momenta. The last inequality gives $y>.171>.16\max(z,1-\alpha)$. For the opposite end put $Y=.377z-1.75\alpha-.75c$; by <a href="#sel:actual-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:actual-margin">[sel:actual-margin]</a>, $y<Y$, and $$\begin{aligned}
 .49z-Y&=.113z+1.75\alpha+.75c>0,\\
 .49(1-\alpha)-Y&=.113z+1.26\alpha+1.24c>0.
\end{aligned}$$ Therefore $$.16\max(z,1-\alpha)<y<.49\min(z,1-\alpha).$$ Together with <a href="#sel:high-momenta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:high-momenta">[sel:high-momenta]</a>, these are exactly the conditions for including the improved entry of <a href="#sel:pure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:pure">[sel:pure]</a>.

**Step 3: improved occupation contradicts an unsafe coefficient.** Since $I_m^0=P^{-2\rho}$, that entry gives $$\sqrt{p\mathsf M_*}
 \le P^{-H/2+\rho-c+\beta y/2}.$$ Far selection and <a href="#sel:unsafe" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:unsafe">[sel:unsafe]</a> consequently give $$b<H/2-\min(\alpha,c)+\beta y/2+\Delta,
 \qquad
 \beta y>H/3+.33z+2\min(\alpha,c).$$ On the other hand, multiplying <a href="#sel:near-exponent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:near-exponent">[sel:near-exponent]</a> by $\beta/2=.58$ and using <a href="#sel:delta-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:delta-small">[sel:delta-small]</a> yields $$\beta y<.58z-.29H+.58\Delta<.582z-.29H.$$ Thus $H<(378/935)z<.41z$, contradicting <a href="#sel:H-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:H-lower">[sel:H-lower]</a>. This proves <a href="#sel:safe" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:safe">[sel:safe]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
#### The spatial transition between selected and unselected bins

The selected receiver terms now have the coefficient needed to use <a href="#sel:absolute" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:absolute">[sel:absolute]</a>. We next estimate the source, spatial, and time-weight derivatives, together with the central residual $\mathcal R$, in the signed formula. The spatial transition requires particular care: its angular sum must retain the joint energy budget without introducing another logarithmic factor.

<a id="sel:spatial-kernel"></a>
Equation <a href="#can:spatial-cutoff-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:spatial-cutoff-bound">[can:spatial-cutoff-bound]</a> gives the spatial-cutoff majorant $$\label{sel:spatial-kernel}
 C\frac{f}{r^2}\frac\phi\theta.$$ By Lemma <a href="#can:cutoff-cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:cutoff-cancellation">14</a>, the sum of the partial source or spatial derivatives of selected cutoff products is the negative of the corresponding sum over unselected products. It is supported where selected and unselected products overlap. Indeed, the full smooth partition sums to one, and a nonnegative smooth cutoff has zero derivative where it vanishes. Thus we estimate these errors on overlapping unselected supports. Neighboring representatives have ratios bounded by four.

<a id="sel:spatial-near-far"></a>
For fixed $p,\phi$, let <a id="sel:R"></a>$$\label{sel:R}
 R=1+\sqrt{I/\lambda}\sqrt{\mathcal E_{p\phi}},
 \qquad \sum_{p,\phi}\mathcal E_{p\phi}\le C.$$ Outside the deficit exception $\phi\le C_0/m$ and $h\le\min(I,\lambda)$, the near and far bounds for <a href="#sel:spatial-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:spatial-kernel">[sel:spatial-kernel]</a> are $$\label{sel:spatial-near-far}
 C\min\left\{Ihp^3\theta\phi^3,
                 \frac{R^2}{ph\theta\phi}\right\}.$$ For $h>I$ the pure energy bound $\mathsf M\le Ch/p$ gives the second bound because $R\ge1$. Hence $R$ is independent of both $\theta$ and $h$ in every use of <a href="#sel:spatial-near-far" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:spatial-near-far">[sel:spatial-near-far]</a>.

<a id="stmt-sel-spatial-sum"></a>
<div id="sel:spatial-sum" class="lemma">

**Lemma 20**. *The total spatial-cutoff error is at most $C\mathcal B(I,w)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sel-spatial-sum"><summary>Proof of stmt-sel-spatial-sum</summary>

*Proof.* First consider an unselected structural neighbor, with $\theta\ge\kappa\phi$, overlapping a selected support. Its ratio $\theta/\phi$ lies within fixed factors of $\kappa$, so for each $\phi$ there are only a bounded number of such $\theta$. Balancing <a href="#sel:spatial-near-far" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:spatial-near-far">[sel:spatial-near-far]</a> in $h$ gives $C\sqrt I\,Rp\phi$, which fits the shared-budget calculation following <a href="#dir:small-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="dir:small-budget">[dir:small-budget]</a>: the sum of $C\sqrt I\,Rp\phi$ over $p,\phi$ is at most $C\mathcal B(I,w)$.

<a id="sel:h0"></a>
For another overlapping unselected bin, fix $p,\phi,\theta$ and define $h_0>0$ by $$\label{sel:h0}
 I\sqrt{h_0}\,p^2\phi^2
 =P\sqrt I(P\theta)^{-\epsilon}\phi^\epsilon h_0^\epsilon.$$ The ratio of the near quantity to its threshold is $(h/h_0)^{1/2-\epsilon}$. Overlap with a selected neighbor therefore implies $h\ge c h_0$, for a fixed $c>0$. If the unselected bin fails the near inequality, it also has $h\le h_0$. Only a bounded number of radial dyads occur in this case.

<a id="sel:radial-crossing"></a>
Put <a id="sel:n-f"></a>$$\label{sel:n-f}
 n_0=Ih_0p^3\theta\phi^3,
 \qquad f_*=\frac{R^2}{ph_0\theta\phi},
 \qquad D_0=(n_0f_*)^{1/2}=\sqrt I\,Rp\phi.$$ The near-failing transition bins cost at most $C\min(n_0,f_*)$. If instead the bin fails the far inequality, then $$\mathsf M\le C\mathsf M_*
 \le CP^2W^2\theta^2h^2/p.$$ Substitution into the far spatial bound gives, by <a href="#sel:h0" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:h0">[sel:h0]</a>, the additional estimate $$\frac{CP^2W^2\theta}{p\phi}
 =Cn_0(h/h_0)^{2\epsilon}.$$ Together with <a href="#sel:spatial-near-far" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:spatial-near-far">[sel:spatial-near-far]</a>, this bounds the sum over $h\ge ch_0$ by $$\label{sel:radial-crossing}
 C\begin{cases}
 f_*,&f_*\le n_0,\\
 n_0^{1/(1+2\epsilon)}f_*^{2\epsilon/(1+2\epsilon)},&f_*>n_0.
 \end{cases}$$ To see this, split the dyadic sum of $\min(n_0t^{2\epsilon},f_*/t)$ at $t=(f_*/n_0)^{1/(1+2\epsilon)}$, where $t=h/h_0$. Both resulting sums are geometric. The same bound includes the near-failing transition bins. No restriction on the size of $h_0$ is needed; enlarging the actual radial range to $t\ge c$ only increases this upper bound.

Equation <a href="#sel:h0" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:h0">[sel:h0]</a> gives $$h_0\propto\theta^{-\epsilon/(1/2-\epsilon)},
 \qquad n_0\propto\theta^\gamma,
 \qquad \gamma=\frac{1-4\epsilon}{1-2\epsilon}>0.$$ Since $D_0$ is independent of $\theta$, writing $x=n_0/D_0$ turns <a href="#sel:radial-crossing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:radial-crossing">[sel:radial-crossing]</a> into $$CD_0\begin{cases}
 x^{(1-2\epsilon)/(1+2\epsilon)},&x\le1,\\
 x^{-1},&x\ge1.
 \end{cases}$$ Both $\theta$ tails are geometric. Their sum is at most $CD_0$, with no additional logarithm. Summing $C\sqrt I\,Rp\phi$ over $p,\phi$ as above gives $C\mathcal B(I,w)$.

Finally, in the omitted deficit exception one has $\lambda\le CP^{-2}$ and $\phi\le C/m$. The near spatial bound, summed over $h$ and $\theta$, is at most $CIp^3\phi^4/P^2$. The remaining momentum weights are bounded by $1/(P^2p)$ when $p\le w$ and by $P/w^4$ when $p>w$. These fit $CI S(w)$, including the fixed logarithmic counts from the decomposition. This treats the exception without using the unavailable far occupation bound there. ◻

</details>

Source-cutoff derivatives have the direct good-field and magnetic-remainder majorants in <a href="#can:source-cutoff-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:source-cutoff-bound">[can:source-cutoff-bound]</a>. On overlapping unselected small bins, Lemma <a href="#sel:weight-sum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sel:weight-sum">18</a> applies to the good-field part; the magnetic-remainder part and structural neighbors are bounded by <a href="#sel:baseline" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:baseline">[sel:baseline]</a>. The central term in <a href="#can:bulk-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:bulk-bounds">[can:bulk-bounds]</a> has majorant $Cf/r^2$. Its near/far balance is $C\sqrt I\,Rp\theta$, whose $\theta$ sum is $C\sqrt I\,Rp\phi$. Its deficit exception is bounded by the same near estimate used at the end of Lemma <a href="#sel:spatial-sum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sel:spatial-sum">20</a>.

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
#### Time weights

Choose $0<\eta<1/2$ and an endpoint-zero Lipschitz function $\psi$ on $J$, equal to one away from monotone ramps of length $\eta I$, with $0\le\psi\le1$ and $|\psi_t|\le C/(\eta I)$. Equation <a href="#can:time-weight-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:time-weight-bound">[can:time-weight-bound]</a> gives the time-weight majorant $C_\eta f\theta/(Ir\phi)$.

<a id="stmt-sel-time-weight"></a>
<div id="sel:time-weight" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21**. *The total time-weight derivative is at most $C_\eta\mathcal B(I,w)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sel-time-weight"><summary>Proof of stmt-sel-time-weight</summary>

*Proof.* If $h\le I$, the ratio of this majorant to $f/r^2$ is bounded by $C_\eta h\theta/(I\phi)\le C_\eta$. The preceding $f/r^2$ estimate therefore applies.

<a id="sel:long-weight"></a>
If $h>I$, the near integral, divided by $P\sqrt I$, is at most $$\begin{aligned}
\label{sel:long-weight}
 \frac{C_\eta h^2p^3\theta^3\phi}{P\sqrt I}
 &\le C_\eta h(p/P)^5\phi^3(P\theta\sqrt h)^3W^{-1}\notag\\
 &\le C_\eta h(p/P)^5\phi^3W^{-4}.
\end{aligned}$$ The first inequality is the near-selection inequality. For the second, use far selection and the pure bound $\mathsf M_*\le h/p$ to obtain $P\theta\sqrt h<W^{-1}$. Equivalently, $$(P\theta)^{1-\epsilon}
 <\phi^{-\epsilon}h^{-1/2-\epsilon}.$$ Summing the increasing factor $(P\theta)^{4\epsilon}$ in the last line of <a href="#sel:long-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:long-weight">[sel:long-weight]</a> leaves $$C_\eta(p/P)^5
 h^{1-6\epsilon/(1-\epsilon)}
 \phi^{3-4\epsilon/(1-\epsilon)}.$$ Both exponents are positive. The sums in $h,\phi,p$ are geometric, so these terms cost at most $C_\eta P\sqrt I$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-44"></a>
#### The weighted signed estimate

<a id="stmt-sel-bulk-impulse"></a>
<div id="sel:bulk-impulse" class="proposition">

<a id="sel:bulk"></a>
**Proposition 22**. *For the weight $\psi$ above and $P\ge P_*(M,A)$, $$\label{sel:bulk}
 \left|\int_J\psi(t)K(t)\,dt\right|
 \le C_\eta\big[P\sqrt I+(M+\sqrt A)I S(w)\big].$$ The constant $C_\eta$ is independent of $M,A$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sel-bulk-impulse"><summary>Proof of stmt-sel-bulk-impulse</summary>

*Proof.* Let $\chi=\sum_{j\in\mathcal S}\beta_j$ be the sum of the selected cutoffs, with the index set $\mathcal S$ fixed throughout $J$. Split the interior force density as $$T_{\rm ker}+S_{\rm ker}
 =\chi(T_{\rm ker}+S_{\rm ker})
      +(1-\chi)(T_{\rm ker}+S_{\rm ker}).$$ Although the selection criterion uses the good-field majorant $S_g$, the selected term contains the transport kernel as required by Lemma <a href="#can:identity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:identity">13</a>. Apply Proposition <a href="#can:weighted-formula" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="can:weighted-formula">15</a> to that term with $\Pi=\mathrm{Id}$, and use the direct bounds on its complement. Lemmas <a href="#sel:weight-sum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sel:weight-sum">18</a>, <a href="#sel:spatial-sum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sel:spatial-sum">20</a>, and <a href="#sel:time-weight" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sel:time-weight">21</a> bound the unselected good-field terms and all spatial and time-weight derivatives. The source derivatives, the central signed term, the unselected transport and magnetic-remainder terms, and structural neighbors were bounded above by $C_\eta\mathcal B(I,w)$. By <a href="#can:initial-boundary-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="can:initial-boundary-bound">[can:initial-boundary-bound]</a>, the initial-cone boundary has a bounded-data cost $CI$, which fits $CP\sqrt I$ by <a href="#sel:short" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:short">[sel:short]</a>; the endpoint weights remove the other time boundaries.

For the receiver terms, Lemmas <a href="#sel:weight-sum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sel:weight-sum">18</a> and <a href="#sel:exponents" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sel:exponents">19</a> give, at every receiver time, $$\sum_{\mathrm{selected}}CU\le Cw^{-1/2}.$$ Multiplying this coefficient by <a href="#sel:absolute" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:absolute">[sel:absolute]</a> bounds their total integral by $C\mathcal B(I,w)$. All sums used fixed geometric exponents; their constants are independent of $M,A$. The only enlargement of the large-$P$ threshold occurred in Lemma <a href="#sel:exponents" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sel:exponents">19</a>. This proves <a href="#sel:bulk" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:bulk">[sel:bulk]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:closure"></a>
### Closing the signed-increment bootstrap

We now prove Proposition <a href="#prop:increment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:increment">2</a>. The preceding estimates were conditional on the signed bound <a href="#eq:bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bootstrap">[eq:bootstrap]</a>. Their constants permit a strict improvement, uniformly over the chosen compact solution interval. A continuity argument then removes that assumption.

Fix an interval $[t_1,t_2]$ of length $I$ on which $w/8\le q_X\le8w$. Write the full bootstrap bound as $$\mathcal B_w(I)=MP\sqrt I+A I S(w).$$ If $\mathcal B_w(I)\ge32w$, then $$|V_X(t_2)-V_X(t_1)|\le q_X(t_2)+q_X(t_1)
 \le16w\le\tfrac12\mathcal B_w(I).$$ Otherwise, once $M\ge32$, we have $I\le(w/P)^2$, as required in the estimates. The case $I=0$ is immediate.

<a id="eq:closure-improvement"></a>
Let $0<\eta<1/2$ and choose a Lipschitz function $\psi$ on $[t_1,t_2]$ that vanishes at the endpoints, equals one off the two edge intervals of length $\eta I$, and has linear monotone ramps on those intervals. The weighted impulse estimate <a href="#sel:bulk" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:bulk">[sel:bulk]</a> gives <a id="eq:closure-bulk"></a>$$\label{eq:closure-bulk}
 \left|\int_{t_1}^{t_2}\psi(t)K(t)\,dt\right|
 \le C_\eta\bigl[P\sqrt I+(M+\sqrt A)I S(w)\bigr].$$ We restore each edge using the signed bootstrap, without estimating the absolute force there. On the left edge put $\chi=1-\psi$ and $F(t)=V_X(t)-V_X(t_1)$. Since $F(t_1)=0$ and $\chi$ vanishes at the other edge endpoint, $$\int\chi(t)K(t)\,dt=-\int\chi'(t)F(t)\,dt.$$ The monotonicity of the ramp gives $\int|\chi'|=1$; every such $F(t)$ has norm at most $\mathcal B_w(\eta I)$ by <a href="#eq:bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bootstrap">[eq:bootstrap]</a>. The right edge has the same bound, anchoring at $t_2$. Hence $$\label{eq:closure-improvement}
 |V_X(t_2)-V_X(t_1)|
 \le (C_\eta+2M\sqrt\eta)P\sqrt I
  +\bigl[C_\eta(M+\sqrt A)+2A\eta\bigr]I S(w).$$

<a id="eq:strict-improvement"></a>
Choose $\eta$ so that $2\sqrt\eta\le1/8$ and $2\eta\le1/8$. Fix $M\ge\max(32,8C_\eta)$ and then $$A\ge\max(1,16C_\eta M,256C_\eta^2).$$ The right side of <a href="#eq:closure-improvement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:closure-improvement">[eq:closure-improvement]</a> is at most $\mathcal B_w(I)/4$, and in particular at most $\mathcal B_w(I)/2$. Finally choose $P_0$ large enough for all preceding estimates with these fixed $M,A$. These choices depend only on the datum and $T_0$, and not on $t_*$. Thus every bootstrap interval satisfies the uniform improvement $$\label{eq:strict-improvement}
 |V_X(t_2)-V_X(t_1)|\le\tfrac12\mathcal B_w(t_2-t_1).$$

For completeness, fix $P\ge P_0$ satisfying <a href="#eq:P-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:P-range">[eq:P-range]</a> on $[0,t_*]$. Let $\mathcal T$ be the set of $\tau\in[0,t_*]$ for which <a href="#eq:bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bootstrap">[eq:bootstrap]</a> holds for all eligible receivers, scales and intervals contained in $[0,\tau]$. It contains zero and is an initial interval. It is closed: truncate any nontrivial eligible interval at an increasing sequence of endpoints from below and use continuity of $V_X$. The range condition is preserved by truncation.

It is also relatively open to the right. On this fixed compact solution interval there is a finite bound $C_*$ for $|K|$ on all supported characteristics. Consequently some $\ell_0>0$ ensures <a href="#eq:bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bootstrap">[eq:bootstrap]</a> for every interval of length at most $\ell_0$, since $C_*I\le MP\sqrt I$ there. If $\tau\in\mathcal T$, a longer eligible interval ending in $[\tau,\tau+\varepsilon]$ has a prefix ending at $\tau$. The strict estimate <a href="#eq:strict-improvement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:strict-improvement">[eq:strict-improvement]</a> on that prefix is at most half the full interval’s bound. The suffix has impulse at most $C_*\varepsilon$, which is smaller than the remaining margin when $\varepsilon$ is sufficiently small, uniformly because the full bound is at least $MP\sqrt{\ell_0}$. An interval starting after $\tau$ is short if $\varepsilon<\ell_0$. This proves relative openness. Hence $\mathcal T=[0,t_*]$ and Proposition <a href="#prop:increment" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:increment">2</a> follows.

The compact-time force bound $C_*$ was used only to propagate the bootstrap for a fixed $P$. It does not enter $M,A$ or $P_0$. This distinction preserves the uniformity needed in the momentum-doubling argument of Section <a href="#sec:setup" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:setup">2</a>.

<a id="sec:continuation-proof"></a>
### Local theory and continuation for finite-energy fields

We prove Proposition <a href="#prop:continuation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:continuation">3</a>. The distinction between bounded smooth derivatives and square-integrable derivatives matters here: the initial fields in the theorem need not belong to $H^5$. We first give the local theory for Sobolev fields, then replace the fields outside a sufficiently large ball while preserving the divergence constraints. For any prescribed finite horizon, a vacuum field transfers solutions between the original and modified data on every interval of existence within that horizon.

Throughout this section, $F=(E,B)$, $u(v)=v/(1+|v|^2)^{1/2}$, and $$\rho_f=\int_{\mathbb R^3}f\,dv,
 \qquad j_f=\int_{\mathbb R^3}u(v)f\,dv.$$ The data are $0\le f_0\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^6)$ and $E_0,B_0\in C_b^\infty(\mathbb R^3)\cap L^2(\mathbb R^3)$, with every spatial derivative bounded and with $\nabla\cdot E_0=\rho_{f_0}$, $\nabla\cdot B_0=0$. Choose $R_0,R_v$ so that $$\operatorname{supp}f_0\subset
 \{(x,v):|x|\le R_0,\ |v|\le R_v\}.$$ As in Proposition <a href="#prop:continuation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:continuation">3</a>, a classical solution is a $C^1$ pointwise solution whose fields are continuous into $L^2_x$ and whose density has compact phase-space support on every compact time interval.

<a id="pm-h-47"></a>
#### Characteristics and the Maxwell evolution

<a id="cont:characteristics"></a>
<a id="cont:spatial-support"></a>
For any $C^1$ fields on a closed finite time interval, the characteristic system is $$\label{cont:characteristics}
 \dot X=u(V),\qquad
 \dot V=E(t,X)+u(V)\times B(t,X).$$ It has a unique solution through every phase point throughout that interval. Indeed, $|\dot X|\le1$ confines $X$ to a compact spatial ball; the fields are bounded on the resulting compact spacetime cylinder, so $|\dot V|$ is bounded there. The usual local ODE solution therefore cannot escape in finite time. Transport along these characteristics gives $$\label{cont:spatial-support}
 f(t,X(t),V(t))=f_0(X(0),V(0)),\qquad
 \operatorname{supp}_x f(t)\subset\{|x|\le R_0+t\}.$$ On every closed finite slab the same argument gives a common momentum bound, provided the $C^1$ solution exists on that closed slab. It does not give a bound up to an excluded endpoint of existence. The phase-space vector field in <a href="#cont:characteristics" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cont:characteristics">[cont:characteristics]</a> has zero divergence: $u=\nabla_v(1+|v|^2)^{1/2}$ and hence $\nabla_v\cdot(u\times B)=0$. Consequently positivity and the $L^\infty$ norm of $f$ are preserved. Integration of the transport equation gives $\partial_t\rho_f+\nabla\cdot j_f=0$, which, together with Maxwell’s equations, propagates both divergence constraints.

<a id="cont:maxwell-duhamel"></a>
We shall use the homogeneous *first-order Maxwell evolution* on $F=(E,B)$. If $C_\xi w=\xi\times w$, its Fourier generator is $$A(\xi)=\begin{pmatrix}0&iC_\xi\\-iC_\xi&0\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad A(\xi)^*=-A(\xi).$$ Thus $U(t)$, defined by the multiplier $\exp(tA(\xi))$, is a strongly continuous unitary group on $L^2$ and on every $H^k$. No divergence constraint is needed for this assertion. The formula $$\label{cont:maxwell-duhamel}
 F(t)=U(t)F(0)+\int_0^t U(t-s)(-j_f(s),0)\,ds$$ therefore yields $$\|F(t)\|_{H^k}\le\|F(0)\|_{H^k}
       +\int_0^t\|j_f(s)\|_{H^k}\,ds.$$

We also need finite propagation without any global integrability assumption. For a $C^1$ Maxwell field with current $j$, set $e=(|E|^2+|B|^2)/2$. Direct differentiation gives <a id="cont:local-energy"></a>$$\label{cont:local-energy}
 \partial_t e+\nabla\cdot(E\times B)=-E\cdot j,
 \qquad |E\times B|\le e.$$ Integrating over the shrinking balls $B(x,r+t-s)$, $0\le s\le t$, shows that a field with zero initial data and zero current in this backward cone vanishes on the final ball $B(x,r)$: the boundary term is $-\int_{\partial B}(e+(E\times B)\cdot\nu)\le0$. This local argument uses neither global $L^2$ bounds nor divergence constraints.

<a id="stmt-cont-exterior"></a>
<div id="cont:exterior" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23** (Exterior comparison). *Suppose that a $C^1$ solution exists on $[0,S]$, that $f_0$ is compactly supported as above, and that $F(0)\in H^k(\mathbb R^3)$ for every integer $k$. Then the fields belong to $C([0,S];L^2)$ and have bounded first spatial derivatives on the entire slab. More precisely, $$F(t,x)=U(t)F(0)(x)\qquad\text{if }|x|>R_0+t.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cont-exterior"><summary>Proof of stmt-cont-exterior</summary>

*Proof.* The characteristic argument gives compact phase-space support on the whole slab, so $j_f$ is $C^1$ and vanishes outside $|x|\le R_0+t$. The homogeneous field $F^{\rm h}(t)=U(t)F(0)$ is smooth and has bounded spatial derivatives on $[0,S]\times\mathbb R^3$, by its uniform Sobolev bounds. The difference $W=F-F^{\rm h}$ has zero initial data and current $j_f$. If $|x|>R_0+t$, choose $r>0$ smaller than $|x|-R_0-t$. Every point $(s,y)$ of the backward cone from $B(x,r)$ satisfies $|y|>R_0+s$, so its current is zero. The local energy argument gives $W(t)=0$ on $B(x,r)$. Thus $W$ is supported in the fixed ball $|x|\le R_0+S$ on this slab. Its $C^1$ regularity on that compact cylinder gives $W\in C([0,S];L^2)$ and uniform bounds on its first derivatives. Adding $F^{\rm h}$ proves the claims. Notice that $F^{\rm h}$ can have nonzero electric divergence; only the first-order Maxwell equations, not a component free-wave equation, were used for this comparison. ◻

</details>

<a id="cont:local"></a>
#### Classical uniqueness and local Sobolev solutions

<a id="cont:uniqueness-energy"></a>
Uniqueness holds in the full classical class stated above. To see this, let $(f_i,F_i)$, $i=1,2$, have the same data, and work on an arbitrary compact common time interval. Choose $R$ containing both momentum supports, and put $g=f_1-f_2$, $\delta F=F_1-F_2$. With the transport operator of the first solution, $$(\partial_t+u\cdot\nabla_x+(E_1+u\times B_1)\cdot\nabla_v)g
 =-(\delta E+u\times\delta B)\cdot\nabla_v f_2.$$ The phase-space divergence is zero, $g$ is compactly supported, and $$\|j_{f_1}-j_{f_2}\|_{L^2_x}\le C_R\|g\|_{L^2_{x,v}}.$$ Since $\nabla_v f_2$ is bounded on this slab, integration of the transport and Maxwell energies gives, with a slab-dependent finite constant $C$, $$\label{cont:uniqueness-energy}
 \|g(t)\|_2^2+\|\delta F(t)\|_2^2
 \le C\int_0^t\bigl(\|g(s)\|_2^2+\|\delta F(s)\|_2^2\bigr)\,ds.$$ For completeness, the Maxwell integration is justified with cutoffs $\chi(x/N)$, where $\chi=1$ on the unit ball and $|\nabla\chi(x/N)|\le C/N$. Its boundary error is at most $C N^{-1}\int_0^S\|\delta F(s)\|_2^2\,ds$, which tends to zero because $\delta F\in C_tL^2_x$. No square-integrable field derivatives are required. Gronwall’s inequality in <a href="#cont:uniqueness-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cont:uniqueness-energy">[cont:uniqueness-energy]</a> proves uniqueness.

<a id="cont:tame"></a>
We next record the estimate needed both to construct smooth solutions and to preserve their regularity. For an integer $k\ge6$, write $$Y_k=\|f\|_{H^k(\mathbb R^6)}+\|F\|_{H^k(\mathbb R^3)}.$$ For a smooth solution with momentum support in $|v|\le R$, $$\label{cont:tame}
 Y_k(t)\le Y_k(0)+C_{k,R}\int_0^t
 \bigl(1+\|F(s)\|_{W^{1,\infty}_x}
          +\|\nabla_{x,v}f(s)\|_\infty\bigr)Y_k(s)\,ds.$$ The $H^k$ norm of $F$ is the Hilbert norm of the six-component vector $(E,B)$; this is the norm preserved by $U(t)$. The coefficient in <a href="#cont:tame" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cont:tame">[cont:tame]</a> involves no derivatives above first order. For smooth solutions in these Sobolev spaces, bounds on the momentum support and this coefficient give a bound on $Y_k$ by Gronwall’s inequality. The local construction below will use the $Y_6$ bound to obtain a common restart time.

<a id="cont:integer-interpolation"></a>
To retain this dependence in the commutators, we use the integer Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality on the whole space and include its proof; see also (Nirenberg 1959, Lecture II) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[Nirenberg1959](#ref-Nirenberg1959)] for its classical context. For $d\ge1$, an integer $s\ge1$, a scalar or finite-dimensional vector-valued $g\in H^s(\mathbb R^d)\cap L^\infty$, and an integer $0<j<s$, $$\label{cont:integer-interpolation}
 \|D^j g\|_{L^{2s/j}}
 \le C_{d,s}\|g\|_\infty^{1-j/s}
                   \|D^s g\|_2^{j/s}.$$ Here $D^j g$ denotes the array of all ordered $j$th derivatives, with its Euclidean pointwise norm; this convention is equivalent to the usual multiindex convention up to constants depending on $d,s$. For $g\in C_c^\infty$ put $p_j=2s/j$ and $A_j=\|D^jg\|_{p_j}$, with $A_0=\|g\|_\infty$. Integrating one derivative by parts in $\int|D^jg|^{p_j}$ gives $$A_j^{p_j}
 \le C_{d,s}\int |D^{j-1}g|\,|D^{j+1}g|\,
                         |D^jg|^{p_j-2}
 \le C_{d,s} A_{j-1}A_{j+1} A_j^{p_j-2}.$$ The last step is Hölder’s inequality, since $1/p_{j-1}+1/p_{j+1}+(p_j-2)/p_j=1$, with $p_0=\infty$. Thus $A_j^2\le C_{d,s}A_{j-1}A_{j+1}$. For a nonzero compactly supported $g$ all $A_j$ are positive. Choose $C\ge1$ valid for these inequalities and define $$b_j=\log A_j-(1-j/s)\log A_0-(j/s)\log A_s.$$ Then $b_0=b_s=0$ and $2b_j-b_{j-1}-b_{j+1}\le\log C$. The discrete maximum principle, applied after subtracting $\tfrac12(\log C)j(s-j)$, gives $b_j\le\tfrac12(\log C)j(s-j)$, which proves <a href="#cont:integer-interpolation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cont:integer-interpolation">[cont:integer-interpolation]</a>; the zero function is immediate. To pass to $H^s\cap L^\infty$, first multiply by smooth compactly supported cutoffs bounded by one and then mollify with nonnegative unit-mass kernels. A diagonal sequence converges in $H^s$, has $L^\infty$ norm at most $\|g\|_\infty$, and has the relevant derivatives converging almost everywhere along a subsequence. Fatou’s lemma proves <a href="#cont:integer-interpolation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cont:integer-interpolation">[cont:integer-interpolation]</a> in the stated class.

<a id="cont:tame-product"></a>
We give the commutator argument explicitly. If $s\ge1$ is an integer, $|\alpha|+|\beta|=s$, and the functions are in $H^s\cap L^\infty$, then the Gagliardo–Nirenberg interpolation inequality and Hölder’s inequality give $$\label{cont:tame-product}
 \|D^\alpha g\,D^\beta h\|_2
 \le C_s\bigl(\|g\|_\infty\|h\|_{H^s}
                  +\|h\|_\infty\|g\|_{H^s}\bigr).$$ Indeed, put $j=|\alpha|$. For $0<j<s$ apply <a href="#cont:integer-interpolation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cont:integer-interpolation">[cont:integer-interpolation]</a> to $g$ and the analogous bound with derivative order $s-j$ to $h$, then use weighted arithmetic–geometric mean. The cases $j=0,s$ are direct. The same estimate for lower positive total derivative orders follows by using that order in place of $s$; total order zero is immediate.

Differentiate the Vlasov equation in $(x,v)$ to order at most $k$ and pair with the corresponding derivative of $f$ in $L^2$. The leading transport term vanishes by phase-space divergence. Derivatives falling on $u$ are bounded and cost only $\|f\|_{H^k}$. For the force terms, choose a smooth momentum cutoff $\zeta_R$ equal to one on a neighborhood of $|v|\le R$, and set $a_R=\zeta_R(v)(E+u\times B)$. It is used only in the commutators; the leading transport operator is unchanged. We have $$\|a_R\|_{H^k(\mathbb R^6)}\le C_{k,R}\|F\|_{H^k(\mathbb R^3)},\qquad
 \|\nabla a_R\|_\infty\le C_R\|F\|_{W^{1,\infty}_x}.$$ Each commutator product is a derivative of $\nabla a_R$ times a derivative of $\nabla f$, with total derivative order at most $k-1$. Equation <a href="#cont:tame-product" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cont:tame-product">[cont:tame-product]</a> therefore bounds their sum by $$C_{k,R}\bigl(\|F\|_{W^{1,\infty}_x}\|f\|_{H^k}
             +\|F\|_{H^k}\|\nabla f\|_\infty\bigr).$$ Finally, $\|j_f\|_{H^k_x}\le C_R\|f\|_{H^k_{x,v}}$ by Cauchy–Schwarz in $v$, and <a href="#cont:maxwell-duhamel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cont:maxwell-duhamel">[cont:maxwell-duhamel]</a> completes <a href="#cont:tame" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cont:tame">[cont:tame]</a>. The estimates for unsquared norms follow from the squared energy estimates by regularizing a vanishing norm and taking the regularization parameter to zero.

Here are sufficient details of the local construction to make the restart time in this argument explicit. Suppose first that $F_0$ belongs to every $H^k$. Starting with the time-independent data, solve successively the linear transport equation for $f^{n+1}$ with force $F^n$, and the linear Maxwell equations for $F^{n+1}$ with current $j_{f^n}$; every iterate has the same initial data. Characteristics and <a href="#cont:maxwell-duhamel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cont:maxwell-duhamel">[cont:maxwell-duhamel]</a> produce smooth iterates. Because $H^6(\mathbb R^6)$ controls first derivatives in $L^\infty$, the preceding estimates give, while all momentum supports lie in a fixed ball, $$Y_6^{n+1}(t)\le Y_6(0)+C_R\int_0^t
       \bigl((1+Y_6^n)Y_6^{n+1}+Y_6^n\bigr)\,ds.$$ Choose $M>2(Y_6(0)+1)$, and then choose $\tau>0$ small in terms of $M$ and $R_v$. Induction gives $Y_6^n\le M$ on $[0,\tau]$; shrinking $\tau$ further makes the momentum increase at most one, since $|\dot V|\le |E^n|+|B^n|\le C M$. This closes the support condition with $R=R_v+1$.

The $L^2$ difference estimate for consecutive iterates has the form $$D_{n+1}(t)\le C_{R,M}\int_0^t D_n(s)\,ds,
 \qquad
 D_n=\|f^n-f^{n-1}\|_2+\|F^n-F^{n-1}\|_2.$$ The term involving $f^n$ uses its uniform first-derivative bound, exactly as in <a href="#cont:uniqueness-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cont:uniqueness-energy">[cont:uniqueness-energy]</a>; the Maxwell estimate is linear. Taking $C_{R,M}\tau<1/2$ makes the iterates Cauchy in $C([0,\tau];L^2)$. For every higher $k$, the uniform $C^1$ bounds already obtained give $$Y_k^{n+1}(t)\le Y_k(0)+C_{k,R,M}\int_0^t
                    (Y_k^{n+1}(s)+Y_k^n(s))\,ds.$$ The exponential $Y_k(0)e^{2C_{k,R,M}t}$ is a common upper bound, by induction and the scalar comparison inequality. Interpolating the $L^2$ convergence with the uniform $H^{k+1}$ bounds gives convergence in $C([0,\tau];H^k)$ for every $k$. The limit solves the equations; it preserves positivity, compact phase-space support, and the constraints. Sobolev embedding and the equations give smoothness in both space and time. Crucially, the common lifespan $\tau$ depends only on $Y_6(0)$ and the initial momentum radius, not on higher norms.

<a id="pm-h-49"></a>
#### Changing the fields outside the particle region

We first construct the vacuum evolution needed for data whose bounded derivatives need not lie in $L^2$.

<a id="stmt-cont-vacuum"></a>
<div id="cont:vacuum" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24** (Smooth finite-energy vacuum fields). *Let $D_E,D_B\in C_b^\infty(\mathbb R^3)\cap L^2(\mathbb R^3)$ be divergence-free. There is a global smooth vacuum Maxwell solution on $[0,\infty)$ with these data, continuous into $L^2$, whose spatial derivatives of every order are bounded on each finite slab. If the data vanish on $B(0,L)$, the solution vanishes wherever $|x|+t<L$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cont-vacuum"><summary>Proof of stmt-cont-vacuum</summary>

*Proof.* For $M_tg(x)=(4\pi)^{-1}\int_{S^2}g(x+t\omega)\,d\omega$, define $$\begin{aligned}
 E^{\rm v}(t)&=\partial_t(tM_tD_E)+tM_t(\nabla\times D_B),\\
 B^{\rm v}(t)&=\partial_t(tM_tD_B)-tM_t(\nabla\times D_E).
\end{aligned}$$ Kirchhoff’s formula gives smooth free-wave solutions with all the asserted finite-slab derivative bounds. Their Maxwell residuals $E^{\rm v}_t-\nabla\times B^{\rm v}$ and $B^{\rm v}_t+\nabla\times E^{\rm v}$ solve the wave equation with zero initial values. Their initial time derivatives are respectively $\nabla(\nabla\cdot D_E)$ and $\nabla(\nabla\cdot D_B)$, hence zero. Local wave uniqueness makes both residuals vanish. The divergences vanish as well. The spherical-mean formula proves the stated finite propagation.

To justify strong $L^2$ continuity without assuming square-integrable derivatives, convolve the data with a smooth compactly supported approximate identity $\eta_\epsilon$. The resulting divergence-free data belong to every $H^k$, converge in $L^2$, and converge uniformly with every fixed spatial derivative: the next bounded derivative makes each derivative uniformly continuous. Their Kirchhoff solutions converge with all fixed derivatives on finite slabs to the displayed solution. The same solutions are $U(t)(\eta_\epsilon*D_E,
\eta_\epsilon*D_B)$, whose $L^2$ convergence is uniform in $t$ by unitarity. The two limits agree locally, so the displayed solution is $U(t)(D_E,D_B)$. It is therefore continuous into $L^2$ and preserves its $L^2$ norm. ◻

</details>

Fix a finite horizon $H>0$. Set $\rho_0=\rho_{f_0}$ and take its Coulomb field <a id="cont:coulomb"></a>$$\label{cont:coulomb}
 E_{\rm C}=-\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\rho_0,
 \qquad \nabla\cdot E_{\rm C}=\rho_0.$$ This field belongs to every $H^s$, $s\ge0$. Indeed, its Fourier transform is $-i\xi|\xi|^{-2}\widehat\rho_0(\xi)$. Near zero its size is $O(|\xi|^{-1})$, whose square is integrable in three dimensions, and at infinity $\widehat\rho_0$ decays faster than any power. Sobolev embedding gives bounded smooth derivatives. This argument allows arbitrary total charge.

<a id="cont:modified-data"></a>
<a id="cont:vacuum-vanishing"></a>
For a smooth divergence-free vector field $G$ on $\mathbb R^3$, define <a id="cont:potential"></a>$$\label{cont:potential}
 \mathcal A_G(x)=\left(\int_0^1 tG(tx)\,dt\right)\times x.$$ Then $\nabla\times\mathcal A_G=G$. To verify this, put $V(x)=\int_0^1tG(tx)\,dt$. We have $\nabla\cdot V=0$ and $$\nabla\times(V\times x)=2V+(x\cdot\nabla)V
 =\int_0^1\frac{d}{dt}\bigl(t^2G(tx)\bigr)\,dt=G(x).$$ Choose $\chi\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^3)$ equal to one on $B(0,L)$, where $L=R_0+2H+1$, and set $$\label{cont:modified-data}
 \begin{split}
 \overline E_0&=E_{\rm C}+
     \nabla\times\bigl(\chi\mathcal A_{E_0-E_{\rm C}}\bigr),\\
 \overline B_0&=\nabla\times\bigl(\chi\mathcal A_{B_0}\bigr).
 \end{split}$$ These fields belong to every $H^k$, satisfy the original divergence constraints, and agree with $E_0,B_0$ on $B(0,L)$. Their difference from the original fields is divergence-free, smooth with bounded derivatives, and in $L^2$. Let $D(t)$ be the vacuum solution of Lemma <a href="#cont:vacuum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cont:vacuum">24</a> with initial data $D(0)=(E_0-\overline E_0,B_0-\overline B_0)$. Then $$\label{cont:vacuum-vanishing}
 D(t,x)=0\quad\text{if}\quad
 0\le t\le H,\quad |x|\le R_0+t,$$ because $|x|+t\le R_0+2H<L$. Consequently <a id="cont:field-equivalence"></a>$$\label{cont:field-equivalence}
 (f,F)\longleftrightarrow(f,F-D)$$ is an equivalence, up to time $H$, between classical solutions with the original and modified initial fields. Maxwell’s equations and both constraints are preserved by subtracting a divergence-free vacuum solution. The transport equation is unchanged on the particle region by <a href="#cont:vacuum-vanishing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cont:vacuum-vanishing">[cont:vacuum-vanishing]</a>; outside that region $f$ and its momentum derivatives vanish. The equivalence preserves $C_tL^2$ and compact phase-space support.

The Sobolev construction above, applied to <a href="#cont:modified-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cont:modified-data">[cont:modified-data]</a> with, for example, $H=1$, and followed by addition of $D$, now proves local classical existence for the full initial field class. The uniqueness already proved permits these local solutions to be joined into a unique maximal classical solution.

<a id="cont:persistence"></a>
#### Smoothness through a classical interval

Suppose first that the initial fields belong to every $H^k$ and that a classical solution exists on $[0,S]$. Lemma <a href="#cont:exterior" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cont:exterior">23</a> gives a uniform global $W^{1,\infty}_x$ bound for its fields on this slab. Compact phase-space support and $C^1$ regularity give uniform bounds for the momentum radius and $\nabla_{x,v}f$. Wherever the solution is smooth, <a href="#cont:tame" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cont:tame">[cont:tame]</a> therefore bounds every $Y_k$, in particular $Y_6$, by a constant depending only on this slab and the initial norm.

To continue smoothness, suppose a first loss could occur before or at $S$. Choose times increasing to that first time from below. The $Y_6$ bounds and momentum bounds just obtained give the same positive local lifespan for the Sobolev construction at all these times. Restarting sufficiently close to the alleged loss extends a smooth solution beyond it. Classical uniqueness identifies this solution with the given one on their common interval, a contradiction. This argument requires no prior existence of an $H^6$ limit at the endpoint. The higher estimates on the same lifespan give persistence of every Sobolev order, and the equations give all time derivatives.

For the original field class, fix any compact classical interval, choose a larger finite horizon in <a href="#cont:modified-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cont:modified-data">[cont:modified-data]</a>, and subtract the associated vacuum field. The modified solution has all-Sobolev initial fields, so the preceding argument applies. Adding back the smooth vacuum proves smoothness on the chosen slab for the original solution.

The solutions just constructed satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#ret:energy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ret:energy">4</a>, so their total energy is conserved. Here $C_tL^2$ was established independently; no square-integrable field derivatives are needed to apply that energy identity.

<a id="cont:continuation"></a>
#### The bounded-momentum criterion

<a id="cont:normalization"></a>
The continuation input is the Glassey–Strauss criterion in (Luk and Strain 2014, Theorem 1.1 and Footnote 1, arXiv version) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[LukStrain2014Continuation](#ref-LukStrain2014Continuation)]. In its normalization, nonnegative compactly supported $H^5$ particle data and $H^5$ field data satisfying the constraints have the following property: a unique classical solution on $[0,T)$, $T<\infty$, extends uniquely as a $C^1$ solution past $T$ if there is a bounded continuous function $\kappa:[0,T)\to(0,\infty)$ such that $f(t,x,v)=0$ whenever $|v|\ge\kappa(t)$. The $H^5$ formulation in that statement does not require a separate momentum bound for approximation iterates. Its density and current are $4\pi\int f_{\rm LS}\,dv$ and $4\pi\int u f_{\rm LS}\,dv$, so it applies to the present normalization with $$\label{cont:normalization}
 f_{\rm LS}=f/(4\pi),\qquad E_{\rm LS}=E,
 \qquad B_{\rm LS}=B.$$

Now let a classical solution for the original data exist on $[0,T)$, where $T<\infty$, and suppose its momentum support is uniformly bounded there by $P_*<\infty$. Perform <a href="#cont:modified-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cont:modified-data">[cont:modified-data]</a> with $H=T+1$. The modified solution $(f,F-D)$ has all-Sobolev initial fields; its density is smooth and compactly supported initially, and the constraints and uniqueness have already been checked. After <a href="#cont:normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cont:normalization">[cont:normalization]</a>, all hypotheses of the cited theorem hold with the constant continuous choice $\kappa=P_*+1$. It gives a $C^1$ extension of the modified solution to $[0,T+\epsilon)$; shrink $\epsilon$ so that $\epsilon<1$.

It remains to check that this extension is in the classical class used here. On any closed subinterval of $[0,T+\epsilon)$, its characteristics give compact phase-space support, as in <a href="#cont:spatial-support" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cont:spatial-support">[cont:spatial-support]</a>. Lemma <a href="#cont:exterior" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cont:exterior">23</a> supplies $C_tL^2$ for its fields, including continuity through $T$. Thus it is a classical solution in the stated class. Adding back $D$, which vanishes throughout its particle region up to $T+\epsilon<H$, gives a classical extension for the original data. Section <a href="#cont:persistence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cont:persistence">10.4</a> then proves its smoothness on every compact slab; that conclusion was not imported from the cited theorem.

Finally, if $f_0=0$, the constraints make both initial fields divergence-free. Lemma <a href="#cont:vacuum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cont:vacuum">24</a> directly gives the global solution with $f=0$, and classical uniqueness applies to it. This proves the local existence, uniqueness, persistence, and bounded-momentum continuation assertions of Proposition <a href="#prop:continuation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:continuation">3</a>.
